- **Fix:** the game arguments are set before `nninitStartup` now, as on hardware. The region's size no longer comes from them: the host gives the region what the alias region has room for, in any Unreal Engine 4 / 5 main.
- **Engine-wide:** the rewriter finds the reservation without a build ID (`dm/tools/ue_region.py`, called by `patch_nso.py`), each link unique or nothing is gated: the option's name `SwitchVirtualAddressSpaceSize` in ro, `nninitStartup` (a `b` to the engine's function; in UE 4.18 a `bl` to it), and in that function the one `bl` to the `nn::os::AllocateAddressRegion` PLT stub whose straight-line code sets x0 to the region's base variable and stores the size in the word after it. x1 at the call must be that size (the stored register, or both set from one constant), and written after the store only with that same constant while the stored register still holds it; the rewriter then makes that instruction `mov x1, xS`. Observation gate `OBS_UE_REGION` goes on that store, with the register in the gate number's upper bits. The host (`hle_svc.c`) sets that register and x1 to StandardAllocator's room ([StandardAllocators over 12 to 62 GB of address space](#standardallocators-over-12-to-62-gb-of-address-space)): the alias region less the regions made so far and 256 MB for later ones, in 32 MB steps. That is 2.81 GB in the 3.75 GB block and 4.06 GB in Engage's 5 GB block. With a Switch-sized `DM_ALIAS_SIZE` the request fits and nothing changes. A chain that breaks after the option's name is a `[RED]` line in the rewrite, and the engine asks for its whole default.
  - **Forms found:**
    - a `csel` of the default and the option's size rounded up to 4 KB, then `str x1` (UE 4.27 and 5);
    - the default stored only while the variable is 0, then rounded into x1 and stored again (OCTOPATH TRAVELER II);
    - `str x8` of the default, with x1 set from the same constant (Pikmin 4, UE 4.26);
    - the same with the constant set after the store (TRIANGLE STRATEGY, UE 4.27). Until 2026-11-02 this form was a `[RED]` line in the rewrite: the provisional 64 GB alias region hid it, and a 3.75 GB block aborted in `nn::diag` just after the NVN device started (`main+0xd0f130`), with no region in the log;
    - `str x1` of the 16 GB default (DRAGON QUEST XI S, UE 4.18).
  - **Checked (2026-10-31):** on the 123 `main` files on the game drives, it found the reservation of each of the nine Unreal builds with the option and nothing in the others. OCTOPATH TRAVELER and Yoshi's Crafted World (UE 4.18, no virtual address memory) have the same code, which their `nninitStartup` never reaches. Checked again on 2026-11-02 for the form after the store, on the 131 `main` files on the drive: only TRIANGLE STRATEGY's result changed.
  - **Before:** until 2026-10-31, seven profiles set `-SwitchVirtualAddressSpaceSize=` in `game_args` (2048 MB in the 3.75 GB block, 4064 MB in the 5 GB one), and Pikmin 4's `patches.py` cut its default to 4064 MB.
- **Found on:**
  - [Octopath Traveler 0](../../dm/games/01005270232F2000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 5): `main+0x12e24`. With the host allocator ([Unreal's FMallocBinned3 replaced by the host allocator](#unreals-fmallocbinned3-replaced-by-the-host-allocator)) the region holds only what the engine maps itself; with SDK 19's node area cut the guest block is 3.75 GB, and the region 2.81 GB (2 GB until 2026-10-31).
  - [DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined](../../dm/games/0100A9D01C446000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.27, SDK 19.3.1): the same 62 GB region from `nninitStartup` (`main+0xe8a2c0`). With a 2 GB region it was mapped up to +0.75 GB through the opening and the new-game menus.
  - [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 5.6.1, SDK 20.5.17): `nninitStartup` (`main+0x13370`) reserves the same 62 GB region (`main+0x137d0`); the region gets 4.06 GB in Engage's 5 GB block, as DRAGON QUEST XI S's ([Unreal 5.6's page allocator never gives address space back](#unreal-56s-page-allocator-never-gives-address-space-back)).
  - [Pikmin 4](../../dm/games/0100B7C00933A000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.26.2, 1.1.1, SDK 10.4.8): the region option is the trap. This `nninitStartup` reserves the region only while its size variable is 0 (`ldr x8,[x22,#0x130]; cbnz x8` past the default 62 GB and its `AllocateAddressRegion`, `main+0x353f564`): the variable is also its "reserved" flag, so with the argument the base stayed 0 and the first commit went into Unreal's fatal error. The game's own code, not the host's: UE 4.27 and 5 reserve in both cases (the `csel` form). The default path takes the gate (`str x8` at `main+0x353f57c`). With Engage's 5 GB block and a 4064 MB region, from the boot into the Olimar prologue: up to +1.5 GB of the alias region with 1.0 GB mapped, an Unreal heap of 1 GB.
  - [DRAGON QUEST XI S](../../dm/games/01006C300E9F0000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.18, SDK 7.3.2) reads the same option and the others from `argv[1]` on (`main+0x177b5a4`; `main+0x177c440` parses each one: `atoi`, MB up to 4095, bytes above). Its region is 16 GB by default, reserved at `main+0x177bbf4` in the function `nninitStartup` calls first. A value of 4224 was taken as bytes: a 4 KB region, and the first 2 MB request crashed in `NumberLineAllocator::Compaction` at boot. With the node area cut and 2 MB alignment for large regions on that SDK build (`sdk_patches.py`), the region starts 64 MB into Engage's 4.375 GB alias region. On the Mac, with 4064 MB, the mapped total stays at 1.5 GB from the title to the first field (Cobblestone Tor), used up to +1.75 GB of the alias region; the process holds about 2.1 GB, 1.1 GB of it Metal allocations.
  - [SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance](../../dm/games/010069C01AB82000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.27): `main+0x1b89038`, the `csel` form.
  - [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.27, SDK 12.3.2): `main+0xbec3b4`.

- **Note:** without virtual address memory there is no engine region: [OCTOPATH TRAVELER](../../dm/games/010057D006492000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.18, SDK 3.5.1, no system resource in its NPDM) has `nninitStartup` set a heap of pool − used (3.08 GB) and the engine runs on it, so it takes the 4 GB heap layout. The provisional profile used to give every Unreal game the 64 GB alias layout, which left this one no heap region (`SetHeapSize OOM: req 0xc5400000 > cap 0` and a `Break` in `nninitStartup`); `profile_guess.py` now gives it only with virtual address memory.

### Unreal's FMallocBinned3 replaced by the host allocator

- **Symptom:** a null read in the out-of-memory report at `main+0x1005824` when the engine region is smaller than the allocator's pools (Octopath Traveler 0).
- **Cause:** Unreal's allocator, an FMallocBinned3, reserves 37 GB of the engine region (74 pools of 512 MB, pool index = offset >> 29). The pools are uneven (at character creation the largest holds 68 MB, the 74 together 732 MB; measured with `DM_ALIAS_WINDOWS=512`), so no smaller window fits a phone's ~5 GB.
- **Fix:** the host puts its own allocator in the object instead (`hle_uemalloc.c`: the FMalloc vtable on a host malloc zone, the trivial slots kept). With it the region holds only what the engine maps itself (709 MB at Octopath Traveler 0's character creation).
- **Engine-wide:** the rewriter finds the allocator in any Unreal Engine 4 / 5 main without a build ID (`dm/tools/ue_malloc.py`, called by `patch_nso.py`), each link unique or nothing is cut: the UTF-16 name `Binned3`, its getter (`adrp x0` + `add x0`, `ret`), the one data word holding the getter once the module's relative relocations are applied (OCTOPATH TRAVELER II's and Pikmin 4's vtables are relocated, the others' are in place), the vtable it is a slot of (back to a zero offset to top and either no RTTI or the typeinfo for `FMallocBinned3`, which the host's vtable copies with it, so Unreal code reading the allocator's type still finds it; ported from NXNative af9b70ea8: Captain Tsubasa 2's vtable carries that typeinfo, so the chain stopped there, and its own allocator crashed reserving its pools in the smaller alias region), the one function other than the vtable's two destructors that stores the vtable (the constructor) and its calls, each a `bl` (a `b`, a tail call, stops the chain: a nop there would fall through). It cuts each call and puts observation gate `OBS_UE_MALLOC` on it, where x0 is the object (made by `GCreateMalloc`, or a bss object in `nninitStartup`); the fixups table names the vtable and the getter's slot (`UE_MALLOC_VT`, `UE_MALLOC_SLOT`), which names the slot layout: 19 for UE 4.26 / 4.27, 22 for UE 5, 26 for UE 5.6. A chain that breaks after the name is a `[RED]` line in the rewrite and Unreal's own allocator runs. On the 68 `main` builds on the game drive (2026-10-30) it found exactly the call, vtable and layout of the five that had a cut of their own, and nothing in the other 63, Batman: Arkham Knight's included; on SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance's main, extracted from the archive drive, its registered ones too. Those cuts were removed, with FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE's Asian release's, which had the global release's offsets. DRAGON BALL Sparking! ZERO (UE 5.1, slot 19) calls its constructor from two places, one lazy construction inlined twice (`nninitStartup` at `main+0x1e4db30` and a getter at `main+0x1e4e048`: if the global at `main+0x92ec6a0` is null, malloc 0x1c38 bytes, construct, store). The rule asked for exactly one call until 2026-11-07, so it printed `[RED] … 2 calls and 0 jumps, not one call` and Unreal's own allocator ran, reserving 37 GB of the engine region; now both calls are cut and gated ([its note](../../dm/games/010035F022078000/README.md)). Until then each build had a cut and a gate in its profile's `patches.py` and a row of fixed addresses in `hle_uemalloc.c`.
- **Found on:**
  - [Octopath Traveler 0](../../dm/games/01005270232F2000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 5): the allocator is constructed in place in the bss.
  - [DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined](../../dm/games/0100A9D01C446000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.27, SDK 19.3.1): an FMallocBinned3 whose 37 GB of pools are cut out of the 62 GB region (the mapped high-water mark reached +38 GB of a 64 GB alias region at boot), so a capped region would starve it. Here the allocator is not in the bss: `GCreateMalloc` (`main+0x32758b0`) mallocs 0x1c38 bytes on the first allocation, during the static constructors, and constructs it there. Found from the commit calls (probe `nn::os::AllocateMemoryPages` past its `adrp`, `sdk+0x140f84`: every backtrace runs through the allocator's methods around `main+0x3270000`), the relative relocations that point at them (the vtable `main+0x5e4c0e8`; this build has no RTTI) and the one store of that vtable outside the destructors. No code outside the allocator reads its pool base or calls its methods directly. UE 4.27's FMalloc has one FExec slot, not four, so the slot numbers differ from UE5's (Malloc 3 … GetAllocationSize 9; `Free` is `main+0x10`, right after the 16-byte module header).
  - [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 5.6.1, SDK 20.5.17): `nninitStartup` constructs its FMallocBinned3 in place in the bss, as Octopath Traveler 0's (`main+0x77c6210`; the call at `main+0x137f0` is cut, and its constructor would reserve 38 GB). UE 5.5 and later add `MallocZeroed` / `TryMallocZeroed` after `Free`, two TLS cache calls (`MarkTLSCachesAsUsed` / `Unused`) and two cached-memory queries, so the FMalloc vtable has 32 slots (layout `UE5_6` in `hle_uemalloc.c`: Malloc 6 … Free 10, QuantizeSize 13, GetAllocationSize 14). The base vtable shows it: its three pure slots are 0, and its slots 11 and 12 are one function that calls slot 6, then `memset`.
  - [Pikmin 4](../../dm/games/0100B7C00933A000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.26.2, 1.1.1, SDK 10.4.8): the allocator is made as in 4.27, with the same 21-slot FMalloc vtable (`GCreateMalloc` `main+0x353f770` mallocs 0x1c38 bytes and calls the constructor `main+0x35ba610`, the one store of the vtable `main+0x8da4758` outside its two destructors; found from the FMallocBinned3 error strings through the relocated slots). The engine-wide rule's first relocated vtable with OCTOPATH TRAVELER II's.
  - [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 4.27, 1.1.1, SDK 12.3.2): as DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined's (`GCreateMalloc` `main+0xbecbf0`, the constructor `main+0xc4d778`, the vtable `main+0x64710b0`), with 20 slots: no `OnMallocInitialized`, so the 21st word the host copies is the next vtable's offset to top, which nothing calls.
  - [SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance](../../dm/games/010069C01AB82000/README.md) (1.0.3, SDK 15.3.2): the same UE4 layout (`GCreateMalloc` calls the constructor `main+0x1578bc0` at `main+0x1f2f63c`; the vtable `main+0x5a7f0a8`), checked on its main from the archive drive.
  - [Batman: Arkham Knight](../../dm/games/0100ACD0163D0000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 3 with a binned allocator of this kind, 1.0.5, SDK 14.3.0): the static constructors reserve 1 GB for its tables and 37 GB for its pools (`main+0x192eee8`, `main+0x192fe50`; the region check stops the provisional build there). The object is in the bss (`main+0x51818c0`) and its constructor is inlined into the guarded function behind `GMalloc` (`main+0x192eb30`), so the cut is a branch from the guard's acquisition to its release, not a nop; `hle_uemalloc.c` checks for that word. It has no `Binned3` name for the engine-wide rule, so its cut stays its build's (`patches.py`) with a row of fixed addresses. Its FMalloc interface is UE3's: 25 slots with 32-bit sizes and no `TryMalloc` / `TryRealloc` (layout `UE3`: QuantizeSize 3, Malloc 4, Realloc 5, Free 6, GetAllocationSize 20, which stores a 32-bit size), found by the vtable offsets of `GMalloc`'s 36,800 call sites (`+0x20` / `+0x28` / `+0x30` for 8,090 / 8,423 / 20,308 of them). The host heap held 1.75 GB 45 s into the boot.
- **Note:** Unreal Engine 4.18 (DRAGON QUEST XI S) needs no allocator cut: the SDK's `NumberLineAllocator` hands its region out in 64 KB units (`main+0x177c4d0`), and the engine maps only what it uses.

### Wwise loses the span header in a virtual block from Unreal's heap

- **Symptom:** Wwise crashes in `AK::MemoryMgr::Init` while reallocating or freeing a block, after Unreal switches to the host heap. Whether a boot crashes depends on where the heap puts the block, and the fault address can be a null read or garbage.
- **Cause:** Wwise requests page-aligned virtual memory through Unreal and locates a span header by clearing the low 16 bits of an interior pointer. A host allocation aligned to only 4 or 16 KiB can put that address before the block. Unreal's virtual allocator supplies 64 KiB alignment for these spans. On an M6 Mac with 16 GB, 10 of 16 boots of Princess Peach: Showtime! crashed this way, and none of 15 with the fix.
- **Fix:** the host preserves the 64 KiB boundary for allocations and reallocations of at least 64 KiB, and honors larger requested alignments. A reallocation still calls the zone's `realloc` first and copies only a block that comes back off the boundary. Copying every block of that size instead held 1,053 MB of footprint where 289 MB was enough for one array grown to 256 MB by 1.375 times, took 5 times as long, and gave nothing back on a shrink (a synthetic run on a Mac). Aligning the allocations themselves cost little: the footprint of 68 KiB blocks went from 1.182 to 1.185 times the requested size, and at frame 1,500 of a boot with no shader libraries DRAGON QUEST I & II held about 2% more resident memory over four runs each (2,192 to 2,237 MB, overlapping ranges).
- **Found on:** [Princess Peach: Showtime!](../../dm/games/01007A3009184000/README.md#found-on-this-game) 1.0.0 ([#996](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/996)), whose Wwise integration requests 4 KiB alignment.

### Freed blocks of Unreal's heap keep their contents for a while

- **Symptom:** in SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance every landed hit, and the player's escape, left the battle waiting forever: the attack and its damage number played, then idle animations and audio ran on and no input was taken ([#1034](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1034), [#2345](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2345)). Guard, Pass, buffs, healing and blocked attacks went on. No thread was blocked.
- **Cause:** a read after free in the game that FMallocBinned3 hides. The battle's task factory (`BattleActionTaskFactory`) keeps waiting tasks in a `TMap<int32, FBattleTaskArray>` keyed by the group of tasks they wait for. Its native `AddPoolTaskListInner` (`main+0xe704f8`) finds the group's value, appends the task to it in place and then calls `TMap::Add(Key, *Found)` with that reference into the map's own elements. When the Add needs a new slot, the elements are reallocated, the old block is freed, and the value is then copied from the freed block. Binned3 leaves a freed small block as it was until it is handed out again, so on the Switch the copy is right; the host's malloc zone zeroes a freed block at once, so the group's list became empty and the waiting task (the attack's battle message) was never started. It happens on the first add that needs the map's fifth slot, which a landed hit reaches and a guard does not. Traced on the Mac with breakpoints on the factory's native helpers: the add for the message task left its group empty, and the map held the freed temporary slot.
- **Fix:** `hle_uemalloc.c` holds the last 256 freed blocks of up to 32 KiB (at most 8 MiB, in practice a few KiB) before giving them to the zone, and moves a small block on reallocation itself so the old block is released the same way; a block that still holds the new size stays where it is. Larger blocks are freed at once, as Binned3's OS-backed ones are. `t_ue_heap_late.c` repeats the game's pattern.
- **Found on:** [SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance](../../dm/games/010069C01AB82000/README.md#found-on-this-game) 1.0.3 on the Mac: with the fix the first battle runs to its result screen (attack, the enemy's turn, Zio, a second attack). The community mod that sets the game's own `BattleDesignDebugIsUseOldTaskSystem` to true avoided the stall by using the older task processor, which never reads the map's values this way; with the fix it is not needed.

### Thread stacks from the host's Unreal heap

- **Symptom:** an abort right at `nnMain`, under `nn::os::CreateThread` → `ThreadManagerImplByHorizon::MapAliasStack` → `StackGuardManager::MapAtRandomAddress`; with the log line, `[RED] MapMemory refused (0xd401): <stack region> <- <address outside the guest block>`.
- **Cause:** Unreal allocates the stacks of the threads it creates with FMemory (OCTOPATH TRAVELER II's game thread: `nnMain` mallocs it with 4 KB alignment at `main+0x544`; its FRunnableThreads take theirs from inside larger allocations), and `CreateThread` moves such a stack into the stack region with `svcMapMemory`. With [the host allocator](#unreals-fmallocbinned3-replaced-by-the-host-allocator) that memory is a host malloc zone outside the guest block, and `MapMemory` takes only a backed source: the heap, mapped alias pages or a module's writable data (`map_source_backed`, `hle_svc.c`).
- **Fix:** with the host allocator installed, a source outside the guest block that the host has mapped readable and writable is taken too (`dm_ue_heap_owns`, `hle_uemalloc.c`; `vm_region_64`, since malloc has no cheap lookup from an interior pointer). A refused map now logs its arguments.
- **Found on:** [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md), after the stricter source check of `MapMemory`; [Pikmin 4](../../dm/games/0100B7C00933A000/README.md) boots with the same host.

### Unreal 5.6's page allocator never gives address space back

- **Symptom:** `nn::diag` abort from `main+0x11bc`, no message, in the first battle, with about 1 GB mapped (FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE with DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined's 2 GB region).
- **Cause:** the engine hands its region out with a page allocator of its own (`main+0x13ef0`), a bump pointer plus free lists per size class, so the address space it has used only grows, and past the region's end it calls `OnOutOfMemory` (`main+0x13c74`).
- **Fix:** the profile gives the region 4064 MB in Engage's 5 GB block, as DRAGON QUEST XI S's (profile key `game_args`). This SDK build aligns regions of 2 MB and more to 2 MB only (`VammManager::AllocateAddressRegion`, `sdk+0x145e00`), so it needs no alignment cut.
- **Found on:** [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md) (Unreal Engine 5.6.1, SDK 20.5.17). Probed where each new block comes back (`DM_PROBE=main:13fcc`: x0 the block, x21 its size, x8 the region's end): a 1 GB block at boot from a static constructor, +1.5 GB 15 s in, +2.0 GB in the battle, level at +2.03 GB over seven minutes.

### Ring heaps cut from a pool at 64 KB alignment

- **Symptom:** write to `0x268` right after the title screen (`main+0x5d6708`, a constructor whose `this` is `0x200`).
- **Cause:** `operator new` (`main+0x5a55d4`) takes the thread's ring heap, a bump allocator whose cursor starts at its base. One ring heap kept base 0, as in 13 Sentinels: the engine cuts ring heaps out of a memory pool at 64 KB GPU alignment, and the host's pool storage from malloc is only 4 KB aligned. The first allocation returned 0, which `operator new`'s null check skipped, and the next returned `0x200`.
- **Fix:** the profile key `alloc_big_align` aligns allocations of 64 KB or more to 64 KB ([dm/games/README.md](../../dm/games/README.md)); the game then plays from the title screen into its opening story scene.
- **Found on:** [Unicorn Overlord](../../dm/games/010069401ADB8000/README.md) (SDK 16.2.4, Vanillaware's engine as in [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md); one boot).

## Mapping SVCs

### `svcQueryMemory` must report each module's real layout

- **Symptom:** C++ exception aborts in `nn::ro::detail::GetExceptionInfo`.
- **Cause:** the SDK's unwinder finds a module from `svcQueryMemory`: it walks back over Code+RX regions to the module start, forward over Code then CodeData, and reads `MOD0` for `.eh_frame_hdr`.
- **Fix:** the host reports each loaded module's real layout and reports other host memory as free (`hle_svc.c`).
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Engage](../../dm/games/0100A6301214E000/README.md) (its note lists it among the host changes first needed there). IL2CPP throws a C++ exception for every managed exception, so Unity games need this; Mario ([Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md)) never threw.

### `MapPhysicalMemory` in 16 KB host pages

- **Symptom:** `MapPhysicalMemory` faults on part of a page.
- **Fix:** round to 16 KB host pages.
- **Found on:** the source names no game.

### Maps past the application pool fail as on Horizon

- **Symptom:** the alias region's mapped total runs past the application pool in the first minute.
- **Cause:** `nninitStartup` allocates and frees everything allocatable (pool − used, 3.15 GB, mapped at the top of the region) and the SDK keeps the freed pages mapped; later allocations map new pages at the bottom until a map fails, and only then does it unmap the cached block and retry. The host never refused a map, so the block stayed mapped beside the 0.4 GB the game used.
- **Fix:** profile key `mem_map_limit` (`hle_svc.c`): a map past the pool fails with ResultLimitReached, and the used memory `GetInfo` reports counts the mapped pages, as on Horizon (without that, the startup probe itself, sized as total − used, was refused).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md) (2.0.2, SDK 19.3.11; one boot). Measured: one refused 4 MB map, the 3.15 GB unmap, the same map again.

### Unmapped guest memory stays resident

- **Symptom:** after a large `UnmapPhysicalMemory` or heap shrink, RAM does not come back. `phys_footprint` may still fall, so it hides this: the `guest` class in `DM_MEMSTAT` shows the real figure.
- **Cause:** on Darwin `mprotect(PROT_NONE)` only removes the page table entries. The pages stay resident and dirty, out of `phys_footprint`, and `madvise(MADV_FREE)` on pages that are not writable does nothing, though it returns 0. Measured on 1 GiB on the Mac: 1,024 MiB still resident and dirty, footprint 1.2 MiB.
- **Fix:** `guest_release` (`hle_svc.c`) puts a fresh inaccessible mapping over the range (`MAP_FIXED`), which empties it at once and gives zero pages. `UnmapPhysicalMemory` then clears the range's exposed bits, so mapping it again needs no `memset`. A memory pool's no-copy buffer over the range keeps the old pages for the GPU until the buffer goes (measured: the GPU still reads the old data, the CPU sees zeros). A guest that unmaps and remaps under a pool it never finalized would see the two diverge, which Horizon does not allow, and neither does the host now: [Device-shared memory is not unmapped](#device-shared-memory-is-not-unmapped).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Scarlet](../../dm/games/0100A3D008C5C000/README.md), one A/B on its 180 s route on the Mac: peak footprint 4,540 → 3,775 MiB, guest charged 1,809 → 1,372 MiB, 30 fps held. 437 MiB of the footprint drop is guest memory; the other 328 MiB is not explained. [Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md) on its 95 s route: footprint flat (4,935 → 4,969 MiB), guest charged 1,887 → 1,685 MiB, with the baseline's pages partly compressed, so that drop is less certain. Open: the iPhone and Z-A, whose shader libraries were stale, were not measured.

### Device-shared memory is not unmapped

- **Symptom:** the GPU hangs or stalls with no failing draw: compute shaders that read counts from a memory pool loop forever (on the Mac a silent stall right after the title screen; on an iPhone "Caused GPU Hang Error"), and text or other data in that pool comes out scrambled.
- **Cause:** the game gives back pages of a live memory pool with `svcUnmapPhysicalMemory` and maps them again. On Horizon that call fails: NVN gives a pool's storage to the GPU through nvmap, whose device mapping (`LockForMapDeviceAddressSpace` → `KMemoryBlock::ShareToDevice`) sets `KMemoryAttribute_DeviceShared` on the pages, and `KPageTableBase::UnmapPhysicalMemory` refuses any page with an attribute set (ResultInvalidCurrentMemory, 0xD401), as does a `SetHeapSize` that shrinks (both read in Atmosphère's `kern_k_page_table_base.cpp`). The host accepted it: `guest_release` put fresh pages under the addresses while the pool's zero-copy `MTLBuffer` kept the old ones, so the CPU wrote one copy and the GPU read the other.
- **Fix:** `hle_nvn.c` registers each pool's storage as device-shared at `nvnMemoryPoolInitialize` and removes it at `nvnMemoryPoolFinalize` (`dm_device_share`, counted, so two pools over the same storage keep it until both are gone). `hle_svc.c` refuses `UnmapPhysicalMemory` and a shrinking `SetHeapSize` that reach any of it with 0xD401 and changes nothing, as Horizon does, logging the first few (`[mem] UnmapPhysicalMemory … refused: it holds device-shared memory`). Only NVN pools are registered; Vulkan and OpenGL memory are not.
- **Found on:** Monster Jam Steel Titans 2 (`010051B0131F0000`, 1.0.2), in [PR #1616](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1616): the 2 MB pool holding Unreal's forward light data ring is unmapped and mapped again at frame 6, and its light-grid compute shaders read stale light counts (`0xff000000`); about ten other pools of that game go through the same. The PR followed the pages in the graphics layer instead. Open: this fix was not run on that game (no profile or game files here), so how the game takes the refusal is unknown. If it aborts, it never unmaps a live pool on hardware, and the host's list of live pools is wrong further up.
- **Check:** `t_svc_memory` `device_shared_refused`: an unmap over any page of a registered range fails and leaves the pages, the mapped total and the bytes as they were; ranges beside or touching it still map and unmap; a range registered twice stays shared until both registrations go; a heap shrink over it fails, one that leaves it below the new end works.

### `GetInfo(UserExceptionContextAddress)` returns a real buffer

- **Symptom:** a write through a null pointer at +0x1c0 during startup.
- **Fix:** `GetInfo(UserExceptionContextAddress)` must return a real buffer.
- **Found on:** the source names no game.

## Running out of memory

### A null-pool buffer from a game heap that ran out

- **Symptom:** a null-pool buffer even with virtual pools working ([Virtual memory pools mapped onto one physical pool](nvn-objects.md#virtual-memory-pools-mapped-onto-one-physical-pool)).
- **Cause:** the game's own heap ran out.
- **Tracing it with probes** ([05](../agents/05-debugging.md#probes-without-a-debugger-dm_probe)): the gsys GPU allocator at `main+0x4290c0` and the heap's `tryAlloc` (vtable slot +0x30) show which heap, its size and every allocation into it; the `!xN` heap walk shows the free and used blocks at the moment of failure.
- **Note:** read the probe log carefully: `tryAlloc` probes show *requests*, not successes, so a running total that already exceeds the heap means an earlier request failed.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md), [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (the two entries below).

### Model heaps sized by the host's command record sizes

- **Symptom:** a model load exhausts its resource heap: a null `operator new` at the world load, or `BufferInitialize: pool has no storage` followed by a null-pool crash. A display-list heap that runs out shows as `AddCommandMemory` with a null pool outside a recording, then host-lent segments growing until `FATAL NVN command memory` ([Host-lent command memory segments](nvn-objects.md#host-lent-command-memory-segments)).
- **Cause:** games size model allocations by measuring scratch command recordings; oversized host records can exceed the budgets in their resource-size tables. In Breath of the Wild, each model's heap is its table entry + 0x1008, the file goes in 4 KB aligned, and the setup (`main+0x155a2ac`) takes the rest as a frame heap (vtable `main+0x1d4f950`, head `+0xe0`, tail `+0xe8`). Its use is the same in every run; only the file's alignment pad varies (0 to 0xff8), and the entries leave room for any pad, about 0xd00 to spare on most models. Three models were a 4 KB page short, so the loads with a large pad failed. The game sizes the model's prerecorded commands by recording each mesh's draw and each shape's per-pass state into a scratch command buffer and summing `GetCommandMemoryUsed` (`main+0xf9aeb4`, `main+0xf9bb54`), and our records were larger than the driver's (draws carried the indirect and multi-draw fields, every recording began with a BEGIN record). Super Mario Odyssey keeps its display lists in a 62 MB GPU bump heap named "DisplayList" (`main+0x5e8280`: used at `+0x68`, capacity at `+0x6c`, a null block when full), allocated while a kingdom loads; with 40-byte state binds (39% of the display-list bytes) it filled at the load and every later list got a null block.
- **Fix:** plain draws leave unused indirect fields out and recordings start with their first command. Uniform and single vertex-buffer bindings also use compact 24-byte records instead of 32, preserving full GPU addresses and retaining full-width fallback records for ranges above `UINT32_MAX`; multi-vertex ranges retain their array representation. State binds carry only the state object's bytes (16 bytes for color, channel mask and polygon state, 24 for blend, multisample and depth-stencil, instead of 40), and a single texture bind drops its pool bases when both are 0 (24 bytes instead of 32, the handle still 16 bytes in). Replay expands the compact forms into the same canonical binding state. The native inline uniform command takes 20 bytes and the native vertex-buffer command 24, so these small differences can accumulate into an extra 4 KB GPU-pool chunk. Report the bytes actually recorded; do not underreport usage or add a game-specific heap allowance. A recording's end, the links between its pieces and its calls take no command memory, as in NVN: they are entries in control memory ([A recording's end and links are in control memory](nvn-objects.md#a-recordings-end-and-links-are-in-control-memory)), which saves the 8 bytes of END on each scratch recording. On top of that, what games record per mesh and per pass takes compact forms that only command memory holds: `nvn_obj.h` `OPC_*`, with small arguments in the header's upper 24 bits. The queue's flatten expands each into the canonical records (`hle_nvn.c` `rec_expand`), so replay, capture and the replay walkers see no new op. They were sized against the driver's own commands, measured by running the NVN driver linked into a game's `sdk` in an emulator (`dm/tools/nvn_native_sizes.py`). The results, in command bytes, were identical on NVN1 (Fire Emblem: Three Houses, Cuphead, Minecraft Dungeons II) and NVN2 (Ocarina of Time, Donkey Kong Bananza) except where noted:

  | Call | Driver | Host before | Host now |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | DrawElements / with base vertex | 24 / 48-72 | 40 | 24 |
  | DrawArrays / instanced | 16 / 24 | 32 | 16 / 24 |
  | DrawElementsIndirect | 28-36 | 64 | 24 |
  | BindUniformBuffer / two | 20 / 24-40 | 24 / 48 | 16 / 24 |
  | BindTextures (two) / BindImage | 28 / 20 | 48 / 40 | 16 |
  | SetViewports (one) / SetStencilRef | 36 / 8 | 400 / 32 | 24 / 8 |
  | BindVertexAttribState (four) / stream (two) | 24 (NVN1: 68 / 72) | 32 | 24 |
  | color / polygon / depth-stencil state | 12 / 20-24 / 8 | 16 / 16 / 24 | 8 / 8 / 16 |
  | a mesh: vertex buffer, uniform buffer, texture, draw | 132 | 112 | 88 |

  Five calls stay above the driver's, at the floor of an 8-byte header and 8-byte records: the depth-stencil state (16, driver 8), BindTexture (24, driver 16), which keeps its layout because Luigi's Mansion 3 patches its handle 16 bytes in, BindStorageBuffer and SetBlendColor (24, driver 20), and SetScissor (16, driver 12). An empty recording takes no command memory, as on the driver. The driver also uses 56 bytes of control memory for a recording of a few commands and 24 for an empty one; the host uses 8. `t_nvn` records 51 calls both ways and checks that each compact record expands to exactly the canonical records. Donkey Kong Bananza's recorded command bytes per frame fell by a third on the same route (127 → 88 KB at frame 900, 334 → 223 KB at frame 3,000; Mac, no shader libraries for this chain). See [What `GetCommandMemoryUsed` counts](nvn-objects.md#what-getcommandmemoryused-counts).
- **Check:** for Breath of the Wild's whole scene, probe where the setup heap is cut (`main+0x155a520` with `!x21`, the resource heap) and the end of the setup (`main+0x155a5f4` with `!x2`, the frame heap), with `DM_PROBE_HEAP=28,38,40,e0,f8 DM_PROBE_HEAPFRAME=1d4f950 DM_PROBE_HEAPWORDS=1` ([05](../agents/05-debugging.md#probes-without-a-debugger-dm_probe)). A model fits any placement when entry + 0x1008 covers the resource heap's header and object (0x310), the file, the frame heap's block header and its peak use, plus 0xff8 of pad.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md), [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md#memory) (buffer bindings; the game note records the failed allocation, measured sizes and validation), [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md#services) (state and single texture binds; the note has the heap's measurements); reported for the Nintendo Switch 2 Edition of Super Mario Bros. Wonder and for Drag x Drive, the end of each recording in command memory ([PR #1609](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1609), not run here).

### Unity's GPU pool block allocation spins at size 0

- **Symptom:** the frames stop at the first loading screen, a graphics worker at 100% CPU (17 s in, `UnityGfxDeviceWorker` running, the main thread waiting on a condition variable, no red line).
- **Cause:** the heap check's 1 GB pool (the hardware pool had used the alias region up to +4.25 GB) left the game's malloc too little: Unity's GPU memory pool block allocation (`main+0x96f430`) calls `aligned_alloc` through `main+0x866810`, halves the request after each failure, and once the request rounds to 0 it calls on for ever with a size of 0.
- **Fix:** a 2 GB pool: the game then maps up to 2.26 GB of the alias region through its menus and first battle.
- **Found on:** [Advance Wars 1+2: Re-Boot Camp](../../dm/games/0100300012F2A000/README.md) (Unity 2020.2.1f3, SDK 10.4.1), after the node-area and alignment cuts.
- **Check:** `sample <pid>` on the Mac host shows such a spin (the guest frames are `???` in the host's image list; subtract the module base from the log's `base` lines); it worked here where lldb does not.

### Host calls on small guest stacks

- **Symptom:** SIGBUS inside a host hook (`snprintf` under `mount_ex` / `h_fs_MountSave`), then the process hangs (Super Mario 3D World + Bowser's Fury); SIGBUS inside Metal (`AGCDeserializedReply`, an AGX compiler block) at an address in the guest stack region (Breath of the Wild).
- **Cause:** host hooks run on the calling guest thread's stack. Super Mario 3D World's save thread has an 8 KB stack (CreateThread stack top `0x…9fe0`, fault `0x21a0` below it), and the file system hooks size their path buffers by PATH_MAX: one file open takes 6–7 KB (`-fstack-usage`: `OpenFile` 1.3 KB → `host_path_ex` 1.9 KB → `path_component` 3.2 KB); on hardware the same call is one IPC. In Breath of the Wild a host NVN call did Metal work on a game thread with a small stack: `nvnQueueSubmitCommands` replays the frame, and ending a render pass made the driver compile its fast-clear blit program on the calling thread.
- **Fix:** every hook in `hle_fs.c` now registers through `HLE_FORCE_BIG` (`hle.h`): a compile-time stub loads the implementation into x16 and jumps to `dm_bigstack_enter` (`hle_bigstack.c`), which moves the call to a per-thread 512 KB host stack (with a guard page) when sp lies in the guest address block, passing x0–x8 and d0–d7 through. Only for functions with register arguments only (no variadic or stack arguments). iOS forbids generating code at run time, so the stubs are static. NVN functions are not registered through `HLE_FORCE`, so they got their own big-stack entry: every implemented NVN function except the `nvnCommandBuffer*` recording calls goes through `nvn_big_entry` (`nvn_entry.S`), which finds the implementation by slot and hands it to `dm_bigstack_enter` ([NVN entry points on the big host stack](nvn-objects.md#nvn-entry-points-on-the-big-host-stack)).
- **Found on:** [Super Mario 3D World + Bowser's Fury](../../dm/games/010028600EBDA000/README.md) (file system hooks); [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (NVN entry points).


### Log lines on small guest stacks

- **Symptom:** SIGBUS in `__sfvwrite` / `__vfprintf` under `fprintf`, from a hook that only logs (`focus_sync` under `h_hid_ShowCtrl`, the controller applet), at an address a few bytes below sp and just under a page of the guest thread's stack; then the process hangs in the crash handler.
- **Cause:** formatting one log line takes about 2.7 KB of stack (`fprintf` to the unbuffered `stderr`, measured on macOS 27.2), and a hook runs on the stack of the game thread that calls it ([Host calls on small guest stacks](#host-calls-on-small-guest-stacks)). Any hook that logs was exposed on a thread with less left; the library applet stand-ins log twice before they answer (`DM_APPLET`'s `oe library applet` line, then `focus_sync`'s focus state), and Drag x Drive opens the controller applet from such a thread.
- **Fix:** `LOG` is one call, `dm_log` (`dm.h`), and the host's definition (`hle_bigstack.c`) formats the line through `dm_bigstack_enter`: on a guest stack the line is written on the thread's host stack, the variable arguments read from where the caller left them; anywhere else it is written directly. So a hook needs `HLE_FORCE_BIG` only for its own stack use, not for logging. Programs that do not link `hle_bigstack.c` (unit tests) get `dm.h`'s weak definition, which writes on the caller's stack. The controller applet's two hooks (`ShowControllerSupport`, `hle_hid.c`) also register through `HLE_FORCE_BIG`: with too few players the front end's prompt runs on the game's thread. The graphics layer's `MLOG` (`nvn_mtl.m`) is the same call: `nvnCommandBuffer*` recording runs on the game thread's stack. Writes that stay direct run on host stacks: SVCs (the `OutputDebugString` line) on the thread's gate stack (`entry.S`), the crash handler and its thread dumps on its alternate stack, probes, haptics and save sync on host threads or behind `HLE_FORCE_BIG`. Open: `dm_backtrace` writes directly and `nn::diag`'s abort hooks call it on the game's stack, so an abort on a thread with little stack left can fault before its backtrace.
- **Check:** `t_bigstack` logs from a hook with 1 KB of guest stack left (SIGBUS with the line written on the guest stack).
- **Found on:** Drag x Drive (`040041601D83A000`, v196608; PR #1943, its profile is a pull request of its own): once in a session, on the screen after a time trial. Not rerun there with this fix.

### Deferred-release drain without an autorelease pool

- **Symptom:** the iPhone app is killed for memory; resident memory grows steadily (about 0.2 MB a frame) while the residency set and the texture counts stay flat.
- **Cause:** the deferred-release drain ran on the guest submit thread with no autorelease pool, so autoreleased Metal objects were never freed (the Metroid Dread leak).
- **Fix:** `defer_drain` has its own `@autoreleasepool`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md): GitHub issue #9 (iPhone 16 Pro Max, handheld), killed in the first shrine. An 8 GB iPhone gives this app about 3.4 GB (resident plus `headroom to the system memory limit` stayed at 3,375 MB); resident memory grew from 2.6 GB at the shrine load until the kill. On the Mac a host without that pool grew 1 MB a frame in the shrine (GPU 3.0 → 4.4 GB in 2,400 frames); with it the shrine stays at 2.7–2.9 GB resident and 1.5–1.7 GB GPU, idle or walking (vmmap at 2.8 GB: about 1.6 GB guest memory, 0.75 GB private textures, 0.17 GB IOAccelerator).
- **Note:** found on the way: dead texture serials and the fixed-size pool tracking table ([Dead texture serials kept in a ghost table](nvn-objects.md#dead-texture-serials-kept-in-a-ghost-table), [Memory-pool tracking table grows in blocks](nvn-objects.md#memory-pool-tracking-table-grows-in-blocks)).

### Retired pool buffers pile up while a game submits nothing

- **Symptom:** out of focus, with the picture still and the sound on, GPU allocated climbs by hundreds of MB a second while the residency set stays flat, and swap follows it.
- **Cause:** a finalized pool's zero-copy buffer waited for the next submission's fence, and only a submission (at its start, or in its feedback handler) drained the deferred-release queue. A game may stop submitting while its other threads go on: Ocarina of Time out of focus waits in `oe::PopNotificationMessage` (main+0x3bc0354) while its streaming threads remake a run of about 10 MB of a virtual pool 70 times a second (`vrun_set`). The queue took 770 MB a second, and GPU allocated went from 11 to 20 GB in 12 s, with 6.7 GB of swap in 20 s, until the run was killed. A user's session that stayed frozen after a window switch fits this, but that link is inferred: that host was not run again.
- **Fix:** `mtl_pool_del` (`nvn_mtl.m`) bumps the pool generation first, then makes the buffer wait only for the last committed fence when no submission is open (`pool_gone_release`; `g_sub_open`, with a full fence on each side, so a submission that opens meanwhile either is seen or sees the bump and cannot look the buffer up). The open flag holds the open submission's own fence and is read before `g_fence`: a commit clears it only after its `++g_fence`, so a 0 read means the `g_fence` read after it counts that submission. As one expression, `g_fence + (open ? 1 : 0)`, C left the order of the two reads open, and a commit landing between them could leave the buffer at the fence before its own, freed while the submission ran; a 0/1 flag read first left it one fence late, which a game that stops submitting never reaches. `mtl_pool_replace` retires its pools through the same function. An entry queued with its fence already passed is drained at once off the submit thread (`defer_kick`). A drain's removals from the residency set free nothing until the set's next commit, so a feedback handler or a closing submission that finds none open commits them the same way (`defer_idle_commit`). Those drains run on a global utility-QoS queue, which nothing can wait for, and the commit that ends one frees what it retires after the drain that queued it has returned: each, retries included, is in one dispatch group (`defer_kick_group`), which a test that counts what was freed waits for (`t_depth_lazy`'s `settle`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pool_retire`: a buffer retired between submissions goes with none after it, one retired with a submission open stays until that submission completes, one retired with a submission in flight waits for that fence, and one retired while a commit lands between the two reads (a test hook, `g_pool_gone_between`) waits for exactly that commit's fence. In a game, `DM_OE_SCRIPT="focus=2@60,focus=1@90"`: GPU allocated stays flat between the two.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), measured and diagnosed by Pedro Nascimento in [PR #1392](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1392). The unordered reads were found by reading the code in [PR #2328](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2328), not in a game.

### The pool walk's statistics made chunks of a large pool

- **Symptom:** in an area load GPU allocated jumps by 6-10 GB between two stats windows (Ocarina of Time: 3.3 GB at frame 900, 15.8 GB at frame 1200 on a 16 GB M5, issue #2068; 3.5 to 13.7 GB inside the load on an M5 Pro) while the residency set grows by about 4 GB, and stays there. The memory census's "not counted" line is 8 GB, and the pool buffers made sum to twice the pools' size.
- **Cause:** the pool walk's log (`pool walk: ... in wired pools`, the first pool-table build of a scene and every 500th after) and the raw-memory log's pool age asked `pool_find(addr, 1)` where each texture's storage lies. For a large pool (`BIG_MIN` and up, in 16 MB strides) `pool_find` makes the stride's chunk, a zero-copy buffer of the asked length plus a stride, and a length of 1 then meant 64 MB past the address (`BIG_UNKNOWN`, since gone: [Unknown-length lookups made a chunk at every stride](#unknown-length-lookups-made-a-chunk-at-every-stride)): 80 MB or more, not in the residency set, overlapping its neighbours' chunks, none used by the GPU. A stride that had a shorter chunk got a longer one the same way, and the GPU's next lookup there put that one into the residency set beside the old. Ocarina's 2,432 MB pool has 153 strides; the walk touched 77 of them: 6.7 GB that `currentAllocatedSize` counts in full (it counts a no-copy buffer at its whole length, in a residency set or not). The pages are shared, so resident memory did not grow by that much (10.9 GB against 14.1 GB allocated), but the 16 GB Macs that run this game reach 17-18 GB allocated.
- **Fix:** `pool_peek` (`nvn_mtl.m`) answers the same question without making or lengthening a chunk: the stride's entry if it has one, else -1; it shares `pool_find`'s search of the other entries (`pool_held`). Both statistics use it.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pool_chunks`: asking where an address is makes no chunk, and finds the chunk of a stride that has one. In a game, the memory census line ("GPU allocation census") at the same point before and after: Metal's total and the pool buffers made.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), diagnosed from the allocation census of Pedro Nascimento's M5 Pro runs and the numbers in issue #2068.

### Unknown-length lookups made a chunk at every stride

- **Symptom:** command buffers fail with `Insufficient Memory (00000008:kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorOutOfMemory)` once a game has played a while, though the game's own memory is far from the Mac's. The residency census (`DM_CTL` `restop`, or the one printed at the first out-of-memory error) is mostly shared buffers of 80 to 96 MB each, several times the size of the one large pool they lie in.
- **Cause:** a lookup that does not know how far it reads asks for length 1: an indirect indexed draw's index buffer (`draw_`, the index count is in GPU memory) and a storage buffer whose address the game keeps in its own uniforms or pushes (`NVN_RB_SBUFU`, `NVN_RB_SBUFQ`). In a [large pool](nvn-objects.md#large-pools-wrapped-in-chunks) `big_lookup` gave it a chunk reaching 64 MB past the address, made or lengthened at its 16 MB stride. Each stride such lookups started in got its own chunk of 80 to 96 MB; they overlap one another, a lengthened chunk keeps its shorter one alive for what was encoded with it, and Metal counts each no-copy buffer at its whole length. The guess was also no bound: the GPU may read further.
- **Fix:** a lookup of unknown length takes one buffer of the whole pool (`big_full`, `nvn_mtl.m`), as raw global memory's pool table already did, and every later lookup of that pool shares it. The per-thread lookup cache answers one only with a buffer that reaches its pool's end (`pc_holds`, `REACH_SHORT`). Chunks made before stay, resident, for what was encoded with them. Lookups of known length keep their chunks until a pool needs the whole buffer. The host logs `memory pool …: one buffer of the whole pool (a lookup of unknown length); the chunks made before it stay: N, M MB` once a pool is made whole. Cost: such a pool is wired whole, though the GPU may read only part of it.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pool_chunks`: a short chunk in the lookup cache does not answer an unknown-length lookup, twenty starts in different strides and the pool's end share one buffer of the whole pool, a known-length lookup then takes it too, the short chunk stays, and the GPU writes 96 MB past the address through it. 23 of the checks fail on the 64 MB guess.
- **Found on:** Trails in the Sky 1st Chapter (`01002C9022770000`, 1.0.7, not yet in the [games table](../../dm/games/README.md#games); [issue #877](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/877), [PR #1002](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1002)), on an M1: 4,243 MB of shared buffers over its 1,507 MB pool, then out-of-memory failures. With the fix, 2,685 MB allocated after a save load and about 3,150 MB after seven minutes of walking and combat, with no failure (the PR author's runs). Not run here.

### Upload staging grows within one rendering submission

- **Symptom:** travelling produces a brief multi-GB footprint spike, then memory returns to its earlier level; periodic memory lines can miss the peak. Upload diagnostics show many swapped-out blocks still alive in one batch.
- **Cause:** `up_alloc` could append unlimited staging blocks before `up_flush`. Overflow staging then retired on the main rendering fence, so splitting uploads alone still retained completed staging while that submission was being built. Weak-table insertion and enumeration in the staging diagnostics also left temporary strong references in the submission's autorelease pool.
- **Fix:** split GPU deswizzle batches before another block would exceed 128 MiB. The three upload allocator slots provide backpressure, publishing deferred commits before waiting. Retire overflow and CPU-upload staging from the upload completion handler, remove residency and clear its retained array before freeing the slot; keep diagnostic weak-table operations in their own autorelease pools. Rendering destinations keep their existing lifetime. A single texture larger than the batch budget still gets its own block; this is not a cap on all renderer memory.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_upload_budget` uses actual Metal uploads during an open render pass, with direct and deferred encoding and Metal validation. A 2 GiB burst retained 2,048 MiB before the fix; afterwards it peaks at 384 MiB and retains only the three reusable blocks (96 MiB) after upload completion, before the rendering fence. It also checks every uploaded pixel and the render pass's clear. For a game, capture both frequent `phys_footprint` samples and the kernel's peak ledger; the existing `resident` log label denotes footprint, and staging blocks `created in total` is cumulative, not live memory.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#memory), investigating issue #147. Mac reproduction and device validation are tracked in the game note.

### GPU allocated counts the guest's memory more than once

- **Symptom:** the memory line's `GPU allocated` climbs far above the footprint and reads as a GPU leak: NieR:Automata on an iPhone 18 Pro Max went from 2.3 to 5.5 GB while its footprint went from 1.7 to 2.9 GB, and on the Mac it read 4,020 MB in the heavy factory scene.
- **Cause:** `GPU allocated` is Metal's `currentAllocatedSize`. Every memory pool under 512 MB is wrapped in a no-copy buffer over the guest's pages, and a large pool's chunks overlap by design, so the same guest pages are counted once per window onto them. On the Mac run the kernel's graphics ledger, which is what Metal really holds, read 1,477 MB at the same moment (`memstat.tsv`, 660 s).
- **Fix:** every game. The memory line gives two more figures right after `GPU allocated`: `graphics ledger N MB`, the task's graphics ledger and its compressed part from `task_info(TASK_VM_INFO)` (`mem_graphics_ledger`), and, with `DM_MEM_GUEST=1` only, `guest charged N MB`, the guest block's dirty and swapped-out pages (`mem_guest_charged`). The guest figure is off by default: on Ocarina of Time on the Mac its walk made 9 of 10 memory-line frames long (24-45 ms; 0 of 10 without it), though it runs on its own queue, most likely from contention with the guest's own page faults (a stand-alone probe did not reproduce it); `DM_MEMSTAT`'s guest column gives the same figure once a second. The guest figure walks the block's VM regions as `DM_MEMSTAT`'s guest class does, with each part of a VM object counted once and file-backed regions left out (their pages are not in the footprint, so it reads near 0 with `DM_GUEST_FILE`). Where a second mapping of the same pages overlaps a mapping that dirty-page tracking split into many regions, it counts the overlap once, which memstat's key on the region's start does not. The walk costs about 1 µs a region (4 ms for 4,096 regions on an M4 Max), so the line is printed from a utility queue of its own (`g_mem_q`), with every other figure read on the guest thread as before. `scripts/devlog.py` reads the two fields as memory series of their own, prints them before `GPU allocated` and compares them first ([devlog compares the graphics ledger, not GPU allocated](#devlog-compares-the-graphics-ledger-not-gpu-allocated)); a log from before them reads as before. Read the footprint, the graphics ledger and the guest charged figure for memory; `GPU allocated` only says how many windows Metal has.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_mem_line && dm/gfx/tests/t_mem_line`: by default the line has the graphics ledger and no `guest charged`; with `DM_MEM_GUEST=1`, a guest block of its own (`mem_line_guest.c`) with 64 MB written, 32 MB of it mapped a second time and 16 MB of a written file reads `guest charged 64 MB`, also with the 64 MB split into 4,096 protection runs; two no-copy buffers over those pages and a 64 MB private buffer raise GPU allocated by 192 MB and the graphics ledger by 64 MB; the line carries both fields in that place. `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py`: the memory line filled in from nvn_mtl.m's format strings is read with both fields, and a log of them gives the two series and the comparison rows.
- **Check, NieR on the Mac:** on the 720 s route through the heavy factory scene, every memory line's `graphics ledger` equals `DM_MEMSTAT`'s `graphics` column within 2% once the second is matched (the scene load moves it by 300 MB in five seconds), and `footprint -p`'s two graphics categories (owned physical footprint (unmapped) (graphics) plus IOAccelerator (graphics)) within 1.2% at all 11 samples. `guest charged` equals memstat's `guest` column (1,280 MB in the flight, 1,700 MB at the end), but reads 8-35% above `vmmap`'s Untagged plus shared memory (the guest's regions): 1,526 against 1,183 MB at 547 s. The two figures overlap: the ledger plus guest charged plus malloc came to up to 374 MB more than the footprint, growing when the scene load made more of the guest's pools resident, so some guest pages the region walk counts as dirty are charged to the graphics ledger instead. Add neither to the other; the footprint is the total.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), in the memory audit.

### Upload staging blocks kept after the uploads stop

- **Symptom:** after a scene with large texture re-uploads, the residency set's private buffers stay up to 192 MB higher for the rest of the run: NieR:Automata's went from 56 to 176 MB in the heavy factory scene and stayed there after it.
- **Cause:** each of the three upload slots keeps its primary block grown to its largest batch (up to 32 MB) and one 32 MB overflow block ([Reusing upload staging after a slot completes](frame-rate.md#reusing-upload-staging-after-a-slot-completes)), and nothing gave them back. NieR:Automata used its overflow blocks for 12 batches in 720 s.
- **Fix:** every game (`up_trim`, at each submission's start). A slot's overflow block goes once 600 submissions (`UP_TRIM_IDLE`, about 10 s at 60 fps) have passed since a batch of that slot used it, and its primary block above 4 MB goes, with the demand it was grown to (`g_up_slot_want`), once as many have passed since a batch of that slot needed more than 4 MB. A slot whose batch is open or still on the GPU is left for a later submission. Both leave through the deferred-release queue, which takes them out of the residency set after the next submission's fence. The next large batch makes its block again, at the cost of one allocation and one residency commit. The memory line counts them: `upload staging blocks released when idle N (M MB in total)`. `DM_NVN_UPLOAD_TRIM=0` keeps them, as before.
- **By time, opt-in:** `DM_NVN_UPLOAD_IDLE_MS=<ms>` (off by default on every platform; `nvn_mtl.m` `up_idle_release`, after `up_trim_idle` at a submission's start; upstream NXNative 93c76c1cb, [PR #1491](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1491), whose entry is "Upload staging keeps its largest blocks after a load"). Upstream has no trim of its own, so most of it is NX 18's trim above; what it adds: a wall-clock interval instead of 600 frames; a slot with no batch for that long lets go of its 4 MB primary block too, and its demand, so its next small batch makes a 4 MB block; a slot still in use is sized to the peak batch of its last interval (a 32 MB block whose batches now need 8 MB goes, the next makes 8 MB), where NX 18's trim keeps the largest while any batch needs more than 4 MB; an overflow block unused for the interval goes. A slot whose batch is open or on the GPU is skipped; the blocks leave the residency set at once (a slot not busy has had its batch's feedback) and the set is committed before the submission encodes, not through the deferred-release queue. Set, `0` included, it writes `upload staging: slots hold N MiB; N blocks allocated, N trimmed by DM_NVN_UPLOAD_IDLE_MS (N MiB in total)` at the start, at the first trim and every 30 s; its trims are also counted in the memory line's `released when idle`. Upstream's M4 Pro Mac run of Ocarina of Time (2,000 ms, five minutes): 192 MiB retained without it against 0 MiB at the 30-s samples, staging allocations 19 → 46, GPU p50 21-23 ms in both; its iPad run is pending. Not run on NX 18 or a device. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_upload_idle` (unset, `0` and `2000` with the encode thread off and on: a slot behind a GPU gate keeps both blocks; a fully idle slot loses both and its demand, an active one its unused overflow and its 32 MB block, shrunk to the recent 256-byte peak; the blocks leave the set and are freed before the next fence; small uploads then make 4 MB blocks; every upload reads back).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_upload_slots`, with reuse on, with `DM_NVN_UPLOAD_REUSE=0` and with `DM_NVN_UPLOAD_TRIM=0`. After the warm batches the slots hold 192 MB; 602 empty submissions later they hold none above 4 MB, the six blocks have left the residency set and are freed, and GPU allocated fell by 192 MB. Batches of the same shape then make their blocks again and copy every byte as before.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), in the memory audit: about 96 MB of overflow and 48-96 MB of slot blocks after the heavy scene. Not yet measured on a device.

### Upload staging blocks freed and made again on every burst

- **Symptom:** a scene or menu entry that uploads hundreds of MB of textures at once has one very long frame, much of it in residency commits: the long-frame line's residency share is large and `upload staging blocks: N created in total` climbs by about one 32 MB block per 32 MB uploaded past the slots' blocks, on every such load. S2Arm measured a sports game's menu entry uploading about 900 MB: a 630 ms worst frame, 418 ms of a 755 ms frame in the residency commit.
- **Cause:** an upload batch past its slot's two blocks ([Reusing upload staging after a slot completes](frame-rate.md#reusing-upload-staging-after-a-slot-completes)) takes temporary 32 MB private blocks (`up_alloc`), and the batch's completion retired them (`up_flush`): out of the residency set and freed. The next batch of the burst made new ones and added them to the set again, so a burst paid an allocation and a residency add for every block and a removal for each again, and each commit of the set had them to apply.
- **Fix:** every game (`up_pool_put`, `up_pool_get`, `up_pool_trim`, `up_pool_release` in `nvn_mtl.m`; ported from S2Arm 01afd92a, 6e708d3e (Apache-2.0)). A retired temporary block of exactly 32 MB stays resident in a pool, put there by the batch's feedback handler (so the GPU is done with it, and nothing still running reads a block the pool hands out), and the next temporary block `up_alloc` needs comes from the pool with no allocation and no residency change. The slots' own blocks and overflow blocks, the slots' 64 KB parameter blocks (`g_up_slot_pblk`, shared storage) and the zero-copy sources keep their own lifetimes, so the pool and the per-slot blocks never hold the same block. The pool holds up to 128 MB on iOS and 512 MB on the Mac (S2Arm's defaults) and goes whole, out of the residency set: 5 s after its last use (at a submission's start, its removal committed off the replay with `DM_NVN_RES_ASYNC`), at once when the headroom to the memory limit (`os_proc_available_memory`, the same reading as the render-target heaps' cap; none on the Mac) is under 1 GB, at every memory trim (`mtl_trim_at`: a pressure warning and going to the background, counted with the upload staging blocks in the `memory trim` line), and in `mtl_bg_purge` going to the background. A block is not kept while the headroom is under its size + 1 GB. The lines `upload staging pool: N blocks, N MB, released (5 s unused | memory headroom under 1 GB); N taken from the pool, N returned to it, N MB not kept for low memory headroom, so far` say what it did; the `upload staging blocks` line gives the pool's MB. `DM_NVN_STAGE_POOL_MB=0` frees the blocks after each batch as before, `=<MB>` sets the cap (kept in player builds).
- **Not ported:** the same S2Arm commit's upload-ahead list (`tex_pre.tsv`, a per-device record of the textures a long first-use frame uploaded, uploaded in earlier idle frames on the next run). A follow-up; it needs its own memory and battery A/B.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_stage_pool`, with the validation layer, the encode thread off and on, and `DM_NVN_STAGE_POOL_MB=0`: a burst's two temporary blocks are kept and stay in the residency set; the next slot's burst takes them with no new allocation and no residency add; with the queue held, three bursts in flight never share a block and the pool takes none back until the GPU has run them; six retired blocks leave the pool at its 128 MB cap and the other two leave the set and are freed; the pool is released after 5 s unused, with a fake headroom of 900 MB at once, is not filled under a block + 1 GB, and is released by the background and pressure trims and by `mtl_bg_purge`; every allocation's edge bytes come back intact. `check_t_upload_slots` and `check_t_upload_budget` count the pool beside the slots' blocks.
- **Found on:** no game here: S2Arm's numbers (M1 Pro, the menu entry: worst frame 630 ms → 251–272 ms with its upload-ahead list too). Not measured on a device; the pool's memory cost on the phone (up to 128 MB for 5 s after a burst) is not measured either.

### Textures kept in the footprint while in the background

- **Symptom:** iOS ends the app while it is in the background (the next return starts the game afresh, the log ends at `graphics suspended`), more often the more memory the game uses; nothing goes wrong in the foreground.
- **Cause:** after the drain going to the background (`dm_gfx_suspend`) the host keeps every texture it made, counted in the app's footprint, which the system weighs when it needs memory. Much of it the host can make again: the packaged textures are copies of the game's memory.
- **Fix:** `mtl_bg_purge` (`nvn_mtl.m`; ported from S2Arm 23b99c4a), from `dm_gfx_suspend` once the GPU has drained and the trim has run: the packaged textures (`pkg_upload`'s, noted in a weak table, `bg_pkg_note`) in the residency set are marked `MTLPurgeableStateVolatile`, which takes them out of the footprint and lets the system take their memory instead of ending the app. Not marked, as their content is only on the GPU and nothing would make it again: render targets, packaged textures made with render-target or shader-write use, packaged textures the GPU wrote since (a copy or buffer-to-texture copy into one, a clear: the replay's write hook `tu_wrote` takes them out of the table, `bg_pkg_gpu_wrote`), textures only a shader or a copy writes (compute images, block-compressed tiles a copy fills), textures in a heap (a heap is purged whole), views, textures over a buffer, sparse textures, and every buffer. Render targets and GPU-written packaged textures were marked too before the port of S2Arm 41eec704 (Apache-2.0): one S2Arm player's two returns to the foreground had 853 and 909 textures emptied, only 3 of them packaged; the rest were GPU-only, and those the game drew once (a glyph atlas, block-compressed textures a GPU copy filled, emptied to zeros: magenta) stayed broken after switching apps. Coming back, before the gate opens (`dm_gfx_suspend(0)`), they are made non-volatile again; a packaged texture the system emptied goes into the dropped-upload record (`g_up_lost`) and is uploaded again whole at its next use, as after a dropped upload batch (`pkg_upload_`), and the view caches take the slow path once (`g_ser_gen`). The lines `background: N packaged textures, N MB, marked purgeable …; N render targets and GPU-written textures, N MB, kept …; footprint A → B MB` and `background: … the system emptied N packaged textures … and N render targets …, N MB; footprint N MB at the resume (N MB going)` give what happened. It also runs when a Mac window is minimized and at the graceful exit, where the system rarely takes anything. `DM_NVN_BG_PURGE=0` turns it off (kept in player builds); the footprint lines stay.
- **Open:** with render targets kept, less leaves the footprint than S2Arm's first measurement below (which marked them). A packaged texture the GPU wrote stays out of the table even after the game's later upload replaces its content whole (`pkg_upload` notes only a new texture), so it is kept non-volatile for good. Not checked on a phone: how much the system takes now.
- **Measured:** by S2Arm on an iPhone (one game, 30 s in the background): 1,048 MB of footprint at the resume with it, 2,497 MB without (2,819 and 2,784 MB going), the game going on with the picture intact. Not measured here.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_bg_purge`: with the validation layer, a packaged texture is marked and a render target, a packaged texture a GPU copy wrote through a view (`tu_wrote`), a packaged texture with render-target use, a shader-written one, a plain one and one in a heap are not; coming back all are non-volatile, the packaged texture the test empties is in the dropped-upload record, the one it leaves and the kept ones are not; `dm_gfx_suspend(1)` and `(0)` mark and unmark; `DM_NVN_BG_PURGE=0` marks nothing.
- **Not ported:** S2Arm's 6be2acdb, which moves its background drain off the UIKit callback onto a serial queue under a background task. NX 18's `dm_gfx_suspend` already holds the main thread at most 3 s (`SUSP_LIMIT_NS`, its drain on a thread of its own and a retry after a resume that could not drain), well inside UIKit's watchdog, and the move would need a gate the callback closes at once with a hold that a resume still queued behind the drain must not reopen, shared with the other callers (the Mac window's minimize, the scripted background of `hle_oe.c`, the graceful exit), and the pipeline archive's and cloud save's background tasks moved behind the drain that starts them.

### Deswizzle parameter blocks freed behind the replay's fence

- **Symptom:** a GPU address fault (`kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorPageFault` or a lost-GPU stop) on an upload command buffer (`[RED] upload command buffer execution error`) soon after a run of first texture uploads. Reported by the S2Arm project from two players (a Mac and an iPad) on its copy of this layer; not seen here.
- **Cause:** the GPU deswizzle of an upload batch reads 64-byte parameter cells (`up_param_alloc`; Metal 4 has no `setBytes`) cut from a 64 KB block that every batch shared. A full block was released with `defer_obj_release`, behind the next replay submission's fence, and left the residency set then. Every batch that used it was committed before that submission, but the submission's completion says they are done only when it waits for them: its encoders' queue barriers do, but a submission with no encoder, or whose draw passes all begin without the queue barrier (`DM_NVN_BARRIER=game` after a relaxed pass end, `enc_r_begin_pass`), can complete first, and the block was then freed under a batch still reading it.
- **Fix:** every game (`up_param_alloc`, `g_up_slot_pblk` in `nvn_mtl.m`; ported from S2Arm ad3ddea6). The cells live as the batch's staging does: each of the three upload slots has a 64 KB block of its own, which a batch takes from its first cell once `up_begin` has waited for the slot's previous batch; a block filled within a batch goes with that batch's staging (`g_up_keep`) and leaves the residency set when the batch has run (`up_flush`). Memory: three 64 KB blocks stay for the run; a batch of more than 1,024 deswizzles takes a block of its own as before.
- **Check:** `t_up_params`, with the validation layer and with the encode thread: a batch starts on its slot's block, 1,100 and then 2,124 cells take one more block each, the filled block of the batch goes with its staging and not to the deferred-release queue, it leaves the residency set and is freed when the batch has run, and the slots' next batches start their own blocks from the first cell. The fault itself was not reproduced; whether an empty submission's completion can come before an earlier upload batch's was not measured either.
- **Found on:** no game here. Reported by the S2Arm project.

### Per-frame arena blocks made for every submission in flight

- **Symptom:** from about 105 s into NieR:Automata, after the upstream merge of 2026-11-10, the graphics ledger stays 100-150 MB higher than before and `per-frame arena blocks created` in the memory line climbs from 8 to 26-28 between frames 5,700 and 7,500, then never falls (Mac, memstat's graphics: 856 MB against 805 at 122 s, 982 against 849 at 296 s).
- **Cause:** each submission took 4 MB blocks of its own for its argument tables, uniform copies and other scratch (`arena_alloc`), held them until the GPU completed it and kept them for the rest of the run (only blocks above 32 MB, `ARENA_KEEP`, went once idle). So the blocks followed the submissions in flight, not the bytes. Since the submissions of a frame share a command allocator ([upstream PR #2090](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2090), `ALLOC_SHARE`), the submit thread no longer waits for one of 8 allocators and up to `POST_RING` (128) submissions can be in flight; NieR:Automata keeps about 30 in flight from its second scene on, so it made 26-30 blocks where 8 had been the bound. They were not idle, so releasing idle blocks alone gave nothing back: with blocks per submission and the idle release on, a 160 s run still held 30 (120 MB).
- **Fix:** every game (`arena_begin`, `arena_commit`, `arena_trim` in `nvn_mtl.m`). A submission goes on in the block the one before it ended in while that has room (`g_arena_next`); the block's fence is then the later submission's, so it is reused only once the GPU has completed every submission that wrote it, and blocks follow the bytes in flight. A new block is taken from those the GPU is done with, the smallest that fits and of those the one used last, so blocks a peak made stay unused. Those above 4 blocks (`ARENA_KEEP_BLOCKS`) that no submission has used for 600 submissions (`ARENA_IDLE`) leave the residency set and are freed at a submission's start, and a memory trim ([Nothing answered the system's memory pressure](#nothing-answered-the-systems-memory-pressure)) frees every one the GPU is done with above 4 at once. Neither touches a block a pending submission wrote (its fence above `g_done`) or the one the submission being encoded writes. The memory line gives `per-frame arena blocks created N in total (alive N, M MB; released when idle N, M MB in total)` and the memory trim's line `per-frame arena blocks N (M MB)`. `DM_NVN_ARENA_SHARE=0` takes blocks per submission and `DM_NVN_ARENA_TRIM=0` keeps every block, as before. Sharing blocks exposed a fault of the patched argument blocks ([Argument blocks encoded again when only a uniform buffer moved](frame-rate.md#argument-blocks-encoded-again-when-only-a-uniform-buffer-moved)): the patch took the block's address after `cbuf_slot` and `cbsize_table` had taken arena space of their own, so when that space came from a new block the copy was bound at the new block and the old one's offset; with shared blocks filling mid-submission every few frames the GPU hung 1-4 times in 160 s of NieR:Automata (`kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorHang`, always the same segment's shaders). The address is now read when the block is taken. A shader's last argument block (`ab_buf`) is borrowed from its arena, not kept (upstream NXNative `3fe574dfe`): it is reused only within its render pass, and kept strongly it held the buffer of a block `arena_trim` had retired for as long as its shader was not drawn again.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_arena_trim`, with the encode thread off and on, `DM_NVN_ARENA_TRIM=0`, `DM_NVN_ARENA_SHARE=0` and `DM_MEMORY_TRIM=0`, with Metal validation. With the GPU held by a shared-event gate 24 submissions of 3 MB make a block each; a memory trim asked then and the idle trims at 8 more starts free none of them, and once the gate opens each submission's GPU copy reads the bytes it wrote. 604 small submissions later 4 blocks are left, the other 20 have left the residency set and are freed and GPU allocated fell by 80 MB; grown again, `dm_gfx_trim` frees them at once. 32 small submissions with the GPU held share one block (a block each with `DM_NVN_ARENA_SHARE=0`) and each reads its own bytes. A patched argument block whose size table goes to a new block is bound at the block it was written in (it was not before the fix).
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), in the merge A/B (Mac, the merge's route, 300 s, two runs against the merged host's um-B and the host before the merge's um-A): blocks made 2 (um-B 28, um-A 8); at 122 s graphics ledger 790-793 MB (um-B 856, um-A 805), IOAccelerator 114-117 MB (180, 129), footprint 2,163-2,167 MB (2,225, 2,191); at 296 s ledger 873-876 MB (982, 849), IOAccelerator 165-169 MB (273, 143), footprint 2,296-2,299 MB (2,388, 2,283). Frame time p50 16.67 ms and p95 16.98-17.05 ms (um-B 17.17, um-A 17.25) over frames 3,000-17,400; no hang in 600 s of runs. Not yet run on a device.

### Guest texture source buffers accumulate within an upload batch

- **Symptom:** a scene load adds hundreds of MB of zero-copy buffers over guest texture storage to the residency set before its upload batches complete: upstream's Ocarina of Time field load on an iPhone 17 added 732 MB of them within a 300-frame window ([PR #2270](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2270)).
- **Cause:** a texture in a large pool (`BIG_MIN`) or a pool that holds only textures is read through a zero-copy buffer of its own, held by the upload batch until it completes (`up_big_src`). The batch's budget (`UP_BATCH_BYTES`, 128 MB) counts only its private staging, not these buffers over guest pages.
- **Fix:** opt-in (`DM_NVN_UPLOAD_SRC_MB=<MiB>`, off by default on every platform; `nvn_mtl.m` `up_src_budget`, `up_big_src`; upstream NXNative 31fdefeb4). The open batch's page-rounded source bytes are counted, and a texture whose source would pass the budget ends the batch and begins a new one before its buffer is made, between textures only; each batch lets its own buffers go when it completes. A single texture larger than the budget still goes in one batch (NX 18 has no GPU gather of sparse pools, upstream's other exception). In every mode: when taking its staging flushed the batch that held a texture's source, the upload makes the source again in the new batch, and if that is refused it now takes the CPU path (`[RED] could not retain a texture source in the new upload batch`) instead of reading through the old batch's buffer, which leaves the residency set when that batch completes. Writes `upload batches: zero-copy texture sources up to N MiB a batch` once when set.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_upload_src` (NX 18's fixture with upstream's source-buffer cases): with the budget off, 8 small textures take one batch and 8 buffers, three 64 MB textures 2 batches and 4 buffers; at 96 MiB, 3 batches and 3 buffers; every texel reads back in both runs.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), upstream. Not run on NX 18 or a device.

### Packaged-texture spares kept after the re-uploads stop
