- **Symptom:** a texture the CPU rewrote once keeps twice its memory for the rest of the run: NieR:Automata's two 32 MB arrays, re-uploaded on every slow frame of the heavy scene, kept 48-64 MB of spares after it.
- **Cause:** a re-uploaded packaged texture keeps the texture it replaced as its spare (`NvPkg` `spare`), so the next re-upload writes that one instead of making a new one. Only a later re-upload replaced the spare, and only the texture's release freed it.
- **Fix:** every game (`pkg_spare_trim`). Each re-upload that makes a spare notes its `NvPkg` in a weak table and the submission it happened in (`spare_sub`). Every 64 submissions the submit thread looks at them, and a spare no re-upload has used for 600 submissions (`PKG_SPARE_IDLE`) goes through the deferred-release queue, out of the residency set after the next submission's fence. A serial entry that took the spare over still keeps it resident (`defer_drain`). The next re-upload of that texture makes a new texture and copies the layers not written since from the one it replaces (`pkg_upload_`'s from-old path), or uploads it whole, as a texture with no spare always did; its `spare_stamp` and `spare_seq` are cleared with it, so a partial re-upload never builds on a spare that is gone. The memory line gives `packaged-texture spares N (M MB; released when idle K, L MB in total)`. `DM_NVN_PKG_SPARE_IDLE=0` keeps every spare, as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_texels_layers`, with the encode thread off and on, with `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_PART=0` and with `DM_NVN_PKG_SPARE_IDLE=0`. After its re-uploads the test's 2D array has a spare; 666 empty submissions later the spare is gone, out of the residency set, and counted; the next re-upload goes into a new texture, partial where partial re-uploads are on, and reads back as written, and the one after it goes into the new spare. With the switch the spare is kept and counted alive.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), in the memory audit. The saving is 0 while the heavy scene's re-uploads go on, and about 48-64 MB after it. Not yet measured on a device.

### Top mip levels left out on the phone

- **Symptom:** a game whose textures alone fill much of the phone's memory, and whose uploads are a large part of a scene change: Ocarina of Time uses about 3.8 GB of Metal textures on top of its 6.4 GB, and its README calls the iPhone "unlikely as it stands". Someone running it on a 2 GB Apple TV left out the two largest levels of its eligible compressed textures; NX 18's release-2 notes listed the same as R1 (`NX18-research/nx2navi-notes.md`).
- **Cause:** every level of a texture the host uploads from its storage is made at the game's size, though on a 6.9-inch screen the largest levels of a 2048 or 1024 texture are rarely read.
- **Fix:** an option, one level by default on the iPhone and iPad for the X2.0 test round (`DM_NVN_MIP_DROP=0` turns it off) and off on the Mac: `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP=1` or `=2` (`DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_MIN` the size, 1024 texels by default). A block-compressed (BC or ASTC) 2D texture or 2D array uploaded from its storage is made without its top 1 or 2 levels, a quarter or a sixteenth of its bytes, and those levels are neither deswizzled nor copied (`md_pick`, `pkg_upload_` in `nvn_mtl.m`; the host's level l is the game's level l + K). A sample with derivatives picks the same level of the game's texture as before, down to the first level kept, since the host texture's size is the game's halved K times. Eligible, for any game: an upload a texture ID's lookup asks for (`tex_by_id`, the sampling path), no flag but `MINIMAL_LAYOUT` and `COMPRESSIBLE` (no render target, image, display, linear or sparse texture; `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_COMPRESSIBLE=0` keeps `COMPRESSIBLE` ones whole, as before: on NVN the flag asks for a render target's lossless compression, which a BC or ASTC texture never is, so the host sees and samples such a texture exactly as one without it), the larger side at least the minimum and the smaller at least 16 << K, both sides even K times over and a whole number of blocks where the game's size is, every level down to 4 texels or fewer (`DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_PARTIAL=1` takes a chain of 3 levels or more that stops earlier too, off by default: an engine that cuts a chain short is likelier to sample it by explicit level, where the drop reads K levels blurrier), not in a sparse pool and never drawn into. Cubes (prefiltered light probes read by explicit level), 3D and rectangle textures stay whole. A texture ID's view of the game's levels [b, b + n) takes the host's [b - K, b + n - K), at least its first level. Every other host use of such a texture names levels by the game's numbers: a copy from or into it, a readback, a clear, an attachment, an image binding, a capture. Each goes through `tex_mtl` outside `tex_by_id`, which first uploads the texture whole from its storage (`md_whole`: the storage holds its content, the GPU's writes stored there as for any re-upload) and keeps it whole from then on; every texture ID resolves again. A CPU write re-uploads a dropped texture still dropped (no partial re-upload and no direct deswizzle for it). The memory line is followed by `mip drop up to frame F: N textures held without their top K levels now (M MB not allocated); U such uploads in total (X MB of levels not uploaded); W made whole again for a host use of their levels`, and the first three drops and the first eight restores are logged with their serials. Kept in a player build's `env.txt` (`player_env.h`).
- **Why an upload kept every level:** the mip drop line ends with a tally of the uploads that kept every level, by the first reason that held, and the MB of their top K levels (what leaving them out would have saved): `; uploads that kept every level in all, by reason (the MB of their top levels): not block-compressed N (M MB), not 2D or a 2D array …, flags or samples …, the COMPRESSIBLE flag …, in a sparse pool …, not a whole mip chain …, a chain that stops early …, too thin or odd-sized …, below the size floor …, drawn into or upgraded …, untracked …, first resolved by a host use …, made whole before …` (the reasons with any; `md_pick`, `md_shape_why`, `md_tally` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The texture's own reasons come first and the size floor last of them, so `below the size floor` counts the textures a lower `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_MIN` would take; then the upload's: `first resolved by a host use` is a texture whatever resolved first outside a texture ID's sample (`treg_tex`: a copy, a readback, an attachment, a pool walk), and `made whole before` one `md_whole` made whole. Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave and Ocarina of Time left out the levels of 0-1 textures with 1.3-1.5 GB of packaged textures in the 2026-11-15 round; the tally says which reason holds them. Each upload counts, re-uploads too. Logging only. `the COMPRESSIBLE flag` and `a chain that stops early` count what `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_COMPRESSIBLE` and `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_PARTIAL` take when on (before, in `flags or samples` and `not a whole mip chain`); the line then ends with what each of those switches saves now, `; of those held now, through DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_COMPRESSIBLE N (M MB), through DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_PARTIAL N (M MB)` (the switches that are on). Ocarina of Time's run 1 of the 2026-11-17 round (frame 23,400) counted `flags or samples` 2,865 (1,835 MB) and `not a whole mip chain` 9,862 (41,733 MB, re-uploads counted each time, so not the MB held at once).
- **Open:** what the host cannot see. A shader's integer read of such a texture (`texelFetch` / `read()`), its size query (`textureSize` / `get_width`) or its level count gets the host texture's, and an explicit level (`textureLod`) or a sampler's level clamp counts from the host's first level, K levels blurrier than the game asked. The translated shaders' reflection says nothing of which textures they read so, and a texture ID does not tell which shader will read it, so this is the residual risk of turning the option on: wrong texels where a shader fetches a large compressed texture by the game's texel coordinates, blur where it samples by explicit level. A reflection flag per binding (`dmsh_reflect.py`, at the next shader chain) would let `tex_by_id` make such a texture whole. Not yet measured on a device; the phone A/B decides any default.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_mip_drop`, with the switch at 2, 1 (encode thread on) and 0, with Metal validation: a 1024x1024 BC1 texture with 11 levels, each level a colour of its own, looked up by a texture ID is made at 1024 >> K; samples whose derivatives ask for the game's level L read level max(L, K); views of levels 3 + 2, 1 + 1 and 0 + 4 read the game's levels 3 and 4, max(1, K), and K to 3; a sampler clamped to level 0 reads K, one clamped to 1 reads K + 1 (the documented offset); both layers of a 1024x1024x2 array read their own colours; a CPU write into a 2048x1024 texture re-uploads it dropped with the new level; RGBA8, 512x512, a 2-level chain, a cube, a rectangle, an `IMAGE` texture, a 1024x16 strip and a texture a host use resolved first stay whole; a texture-to-buffer copy of levels 0 and 1 of the dropped texture reads them byte for byte, the texture is whole from then on (its ID and a view on another ID follow, and a later CPU write re-uploads it whole); the memory line's live count and bytes are those of the array and the 2048x1024 texture; a `COMPRESSIBLE | MINIMAL_LAYOUT` texture and a 6-level chain that stops at 32 texels are dropped when their switch is on (and read the game's level max(L, K)), kept whole and counted by their own reason when off, and counted live under their switch in the line's last part; `COMPRESSIBLE | IMAGE` stays whole under `flags or samples`. A fourth run has `DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_COMPRESSIBLE=0 DM_NVN_MIP_DROP_PARTIAL=1`. Without the view remap or the restore, 10 of the 42 checks fail; each excluded kind is counted by its reason in the line's tally (and nothing with the switch off), the copy's upload as made whole before.
- **Found on:** none yet; an option for the phone's memory and efficiency A/B. The bytes it can take at most: a dropped level is 3/4 of a texture's bytes at K = 1 and 15/16 at K = 2 (a 2048x2048 BC7 texture with every level is 5.3 MB; 1.3 MB at K = 1, 0.33 MB at K = 2), on the textures that pass the rule only. Not yet measured on a device. The tally of why uploads kept every level: [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) and [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md#found-on-this-game), 0-1 textures dropped in the 2026-11-15 round.

### Present effects kept after they are turned off

- **Symptom:** after the in-game menu turns edge smoothing or MetalFX off, or Picture Stretch or a TV makes MetalFX stop running, the footprint stays where it was with them on: 32.7 MB of CMAA2 state at 1080p on an iPhone, plus 14.4 MiB of MetalFX output (25.6 MiB on a Mac).
- **Cause:** CMAA2's texture and lists (`aa_state`) and the MetalFX scaler's textures (`fx_state`) were released only when a new size made them again, so turning an effect off kept them for the rest of the run.
- **Fix:** every game (`present_fx_idle`, after each live present is committed). The live present counts the presents each effect has not run in. After 120 of them (`FX_IDLE_PRESENTS`, 2 s at 60 Hz, so a crop that moves in and out of MetalFX's rule does not make a scaler every few frames), and once the last present that ran the effect has completed (its allocator's `g_present_alloc_done` reached that present's ticket, which waits for nothing), the effect's textures and buffers leave the residency set and are released, and a line says how many MB. Turning it on again makes them again: about 1.5 ms for the scaler, after `FX_REMAKE_PRESENTS`; CMAA2's kernels are kept. The memory line counts them: `present effects released when off N (M MB in total)`. `DM_PRESENT_FX_KEEP=1` keeps them, as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_present_alloc`, which also runs with `DM_PRESENT_FX_KEEP=1`. A 64x64 picture shown at 128x128 runs through both effects; with both turned off, 6 presents later CMAA2's texture and lists and the scaler's output have left the residency set and are freed, and both are counted; turned on again, both run with their state made again.
- **Found on:** the memory audit, on the iOS in-game menu's toggles (Persona 4 Golden's log: 32.7 MB of CMAA2 state at 1080p). Not yet measured on a device.

### Shader reflection blocks at a fixed size

- **Symptom:** a game that loads tens of thousands of shaders holds about 3.5 KB of host memory per shader for its reflection alone, most of it zeros. Persona 5 Royal's iPhone prewarm loads 30,357 shaders before the first frame (about 100 MB of it).
- **Cause:** every `NvShader` embedded an `nvn_refl` of 3,544 bytes: room for 128 bindings of 18 bytes (`NVN_REFL_MAXB`) and the readable-tile tables (`uv_aff[32][4]`, `fc_scale[32][2]` and five 32-entry arrays) whether the shader had them or not. A shader has 21 bindings on average, and half of NieR:Automata's have 8 or fewer.
- **Fix:** every game (`nvn_refl.h`, `nvn_refl.m`). `nvn_refl_from_json` converts into whole-size arrays on the stack, then moves the bindings into one malloc'd tail block of their real number, after the fragment tables (`nvn_refl_fc`) only when the shader has a readable-tile sample or a mixed slot, and the vertex tables (`nvn_refl_uv`) only when it has an attribute-fed varying. A shader without them points at shared empty tables (`nvn_refl_fc_none`, `nvn_refl_uv_none`: zeros, and -1 for the vertex attributes), so every reader reads what it read before. The bindings come last in the block, so a read past `nb` runs off it. Every reader of the bindings in `dm/` stops at `nb` (`nvn_mtl.m`, `nvn_geometry.inc`, `nvn_ndve.inc`, `nvn_capture.inc`); none copies an `nvn_refl` by value. The struct is now 344 bytes. A shader made by hand (`[NvShader new]`, the unit tests) gets room for 128 bindings and tables of its own, as before; the replay makes its shaders with `shader_new`, which gives an empty reflection. The memory line counts the blocks: `shader reflection blocks N (M KB)`. `DM_NVN_REFL_FIXED=1` gives every shader the fixed block again: 128 bindings, zeros past `nb`, and both tables.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_refl REFL_IMG=<build>/shader/<platform>/b-<chain>.img` converts every reflection json of that library in both layouts under Guard Malloc and compares every field, binding, table and c1 byte. NieR:Automata's 1,261 reflections hold 454 bytes a shader in tails against 3,232, Ocarina of Time's 42,848 hold 606 and Persona 5 Royal's 30,646 hold 687; all three libraries convert the same both ways. The `shcost` probe, which keeps 8,649 of Ocarina's prewarmed shaders as `shader_load` does, measured 8.31 KB a shader with the fixed layout and 4.98 KB with the tail (70.2 against 42.0 MB of footprint).
- **Found on:** the memory audit, on [Persona 5 Royal](../../dm/games/01005CA01580E000/README.md)'s and [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md)'s prewarm on an iPhone 18 Pro Max. Not yet measured on a device.

### Sparse tile heaps of 256 MB

- **Symptom:** the first sparse tile a game maps raises the footprint by 256 MB, and on average half a heap stays unused at the last one (`sparse tiles: placement heap N`).
- **Cause:** sparse tiles came from 256 MB placement heaps, and an empty heap made resident adds its whole size to the footprint. A freed tile went on top of a stack, so new tiles landed in whichever heap had freed one last, and no heap could empty.
- **Fix:** sparse-texture games ([Large sparse textures as Metal placement sparse textures](nvn-objects.md#large-sparse-textures-as-metal-placement-sparse-textures)). The heaps are 64 MB (`sp_ht`), and a tile takes the lowest free slot (`sp_free_pop`, a binary min-heap), so the first heaps fill and the later ones are left empty. A slot is still heap × tiles per heap + tile, with the tiles per heap fixed at the first look. One look may make heaps for 16 GB of tiles. Heaps are still never freed. The heap line gives each heap's size and the total, and the sparse texture line the heaps and their MB. `DM_NVN_SPARSE_HEAP_MB=<MB>` sets the size; `=256` gives the 256 MB heaps and their last-freed-first order of before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_sparse_tiles`, also with `DM_NVN_SPARSE_HEAP_MB=256`. The first tile makes one heap of the size asked for and GPU allocated rises by 72 MB (64 MB and the upload staging), against 264 MB with 256 MB heaps. 1,047 tiles of one texture fill the first heap and land in the second at slots that read back right. A tile freed low and one freed high, and the next tile takes the low one; with `=256` it takes the high one.
- **Found on:** the memory audit, from Mario Kart World's heaps ([Physical pages mapped at several places of a placement sparse texture](nvn-objects.md#physical-pages-mapped-at-several-places-of-a-placement-sparse-texture)). The expected saving is about 100 MB in each game with sparse textures. Not yet measured on a device.

### Render-target heaps kept by a flat cap on iOS

- **Symptom:** a game that churns render targets with its dynamic resolution keeps up to 256 MB of freed render-target heaps resident for reuse, however close it is to the memory limit: about 190 MB of Ocarina of Time's footprint at its churn peaks on an iPhone.
- **Cause:** freed render-target heaps wait for the next target they fit ([Render targets that move with the dynamic resolution get new Metal memory each time](rendering.md#render-targets-that-move-with-the-dynamic-resolution-get-new-metal-memory-each-time)), and `rh_trim` dropped the oldest only past a flat 256 MB (`RH_CAP`) or after 3 s.
- **Fix:** iOS (`rh_cap`). The cap follows the headroom to the system's memory limit, `os_proc_available_memory`, read at most every 250 ms when a submission begins: 256 MB with 3 GB of headroom or more, falling in a straight line to 32 MB at 1 GB and below. A headroom of 0 (no limit known, as in the Simulator) keeps 256 MB. The Mac keeps 256 MB. The memory line gives the cap in use: `waiting N (M MB; cap C MB)`. The cap is read at every submission's start whether or not a heap waits, so the line follows the headroom while none does. `DM_NVN_RT_RECYCLE_CAP=<MB>` sets a fixed cap on either; `=256` is the flat cap of before. A lower cap means more new heaps and residency commits while the resolution changes, which can cause hitches.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_rt_recycle`, also with `DM_NVN_RT_RECYCLE_CAP=16`. Eight freed 1080p targets (128 MB) all wait with no headroom source and with 8 GB of headroom; with 512 MB of headroom one of them (16 MB) waits under the 32 MB cap after a submission begins; with the switch at 16 MB none does. With none waiting, a headroom that falls from 8 GB to 512 MB moves the cap the memory line gives from 256 MB to 32 MB at the next submission's start.
- **Found on:** the memory audit, from [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md)'s iPhone log (228-255 MB of heaps waiting at churn peaks). NieR:Automata frees no render target, so it saves nothing. Not yet measured on a device.

### Large render targets over-allocate recycled heaps

- **Symptom:** a large render target sits in a Metal heap an eighth larger than it, the extra kept resident for reuse: upstream's Ocarina of Time residency census ([PR #2566](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2566)) found its 296 MB shadow target in a 333 MB heap.
- **Cause:** a new recycled heap (`rh_heap`) took an eighth of the target's size as headroom, so that a nearby dynamic-resolution size could reuse it. The resident overhead grew with the target.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `rh_heap`; upstream NXNative 943a61115). A new heap's headroom is at most 4 MiB (`RH_SLACK`): targets up to 32 MiB still get an eighth, and which waiting heap a target takes (up to half as large again), the iOS cap and the retirement rules are unchanged.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_rt_recycle` (a 48 MB target's new heap is at most `RH_SLACK` larger than it), `check_t_rt_lifetime`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), upstream. Not yet measured on NX 18 or a device.

### A full colour texture grew the shared zero buffer

- **Symptom:** the shared zero buffer stays at the size of the largest colour texture zeroed, up to 64 MB, resident for good: upstream's Ocarina of Time iPhone 17 residency census ([PR #2566](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2566)) found it at 64 MB.
- **Cause:** `tex_zero_fill` copied a whole colour image (one level of one slice, every depth slice of a volume) from the zero buffer at once, and `clear_rect_zero` the whole rectangle of a scissored zero clear. `zero_buf` grows the buffer to fit the largest copy and never shrinks it.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `tex_zero_fill`, `clear_rect_zero`; upstream NXNative 1d1c5b4af). Both copy bands of rows sized to the buffer, whole depth slices of a volume together where they fit, as the depth copies (`depth_zero_copy`) already did; they ask for room for one row, which the 4 MiB buffer holds at every width Metal allows (16384 x 16 bytes = 256 KB). The 64 MB size guard and the clear ordering (`lc_land`, `rp_flush` before the copy) are unchanged.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_zero_colour` (every byte of non-zero colour textures reads 0 after the fill, levels, array layers and 3D slices included, with the expected number of copies, and the zero buffer stays at 4 MiB; 9 checks fail without the fix), `check_t_lazy_clear_order` (a 1532x1533 scissored zero clear on a 1536x1536 target changes only its rectangle, and the buffer stays at 4 MiB), `check_t_zero_depth`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), upstream. Not yet measured on NX 18 or a device.

### Render targets drawn over stay while their texture objects live

- **Symptom:** a game with dynamic resolution or many scene and camera changes holds GPU memory for render targets it no longer draws or reads: footprint and GPU allocated climb at each change and stay there. Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max with dynamic resolution on (M1 pack, Sitting 2.7) peaked at about 5.1 GB of footprint and 7.2 GB GPU allocated. In NXNative's census of the same game on a Mac (8653238e), the 304 MB render-target pool's Metal textures held 2.9 GB in Kakariko Village, 2.7 GB of them unused for 20 s or more and drawn over by newer targets.
- **Cause:** the engine keeps its render targets as texture objects over one GPU-only pool and moves them as its resolution and passes change, so newer targets draw over the bytes of older ones whose objects it keeps. Each `(pool, offset, description)` is a Metal texture of its own (the sharing table), and the takeover (`alias_take_over`) frees one only when a new texture is made over it, and never while a live texture object still resolves to it, as an object rebuilt over the same storage must keep finding it ([Transient render targets shared by storage](rendering.md#transient-render-targets-shared-by-storage)). So a target stays for as long as its object lives, holding bytes the console no longer has. None of the idle timers covers them: the 3 s of `RH_IDLE_NS` ([Render-target heaps kept by a flat cap on iOS](#render-target-heaps-kept-by-a-flat-cap-on-ios)) starts only once a texture is freed, and these never are.
- **Fix:** every game, on by default as in NXNative (`DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=0` turns it off; `tu_*` and `alias_dead_locked` in `nvn_mtl.m`, after NXNative 8653238e, by hand onto this tree's in-file sharing table). A shared texture that the GPU alone writes (a `CPU_NO_ACCESS` pool, render-target or shader-write usage, not display, video, linear or sparse) and that a pool walk reads as the dummy until a pass writes it (`COMPRESSIBLE`, `pool_unwritten_target`) has two stamps: its last use and its last write by the GPU. Uses are a lookup by texture object outside a pool walk (attachments, copies, clears), a binding of one of its texture IDs (`tex_fast`, `tex_fast_rid`, `tex_by_id`; each pass encodes its bindings again), a handle table kept for a new pass, and a read the shaders mark in a pool ([Shaders report which textures they read through a pool](#shaders-report-which-textures-they-read-through-a-pool); the pass clears the marks already folded, so the next one sees new reads). A pool walk resolves every ID and stamps nothing. When a submission begins, every quarter of the wait (in steps of 100 ms, 100 ms to 1 s: 200 ms at the default), such a texture goes when it has been unused for the wait (`DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_MS`, 3000 ms) and another covered texture of its pool wrote its bytes after that last use: its content is gone on the console too. That is what makes it obsolete, not the time alone: a target the game reads every frame, such as one the engine packs into the part of a larger one its resolution leaves unused (PR #1453, which a reclaim by overwrite alone broke), is never unused for the wait. It leaves every serial entry that names it or a view of it (live and ghost, each a note) and the sharing table (retired after the submissions that used it), and its texture IDs drop their views, so a pool walk reads it as the dummy, as a target never written. A pin, or a lookup in the last 16 submissions (`ALIAS_IDLE`), keeps it. A binding or a write makes it again, filled as a new texture is. At most 256 a pass. The memory goes back as the texture held it: a recycled heap waits in the free list for the next target ([Render-target heaps kept by a flat cap on iOS](#render-target-heaps-kept-by-a-flat-cap-on-ios): reused, which saves a residency commit, or freed after 3 s or past the cap), a texture's own allocation is freed, and a resolution family member gives back nothing alone (the family's heap goes with its last texture), so it is counted apart. With the switch off no texture gets a record, and the stamps cost one store a binding.
- **Resolution families go whole:** a resolution family (the sizes of one dynamic-resolution target at one offset, sharing a heap: `image_texture`) goes only when every member the sharing table holds is dead by the rules above, and then all of them go in the same pass, even past its 256 (`alias_dead_locked` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The size in use writes the family's bytes every frame, so every other size counted as drawn over, went alone and gave back nothing (the heap stays with the size in use), and was made again when the game took that size back: in Luigi's Mansion 3's run of the 2026-11-15 round, 7,780 of the 7,902 textures released were family members, 4,733 were made again, and the whole run gave back 12 MB. Kept, such a member costs nothing it was not costing (its bytes are the family heap's), and the notes, slow-path syncs and residency adds of its release and its remaking go. A member with no use record is never known dead, so it keeps its family. The pass's 256 counts no family member: a kept member stays and is asked again at every pass, so counted, the kept ones ahead of a dead target of no family in the table would take its slot at every pass (with 256 of them, for good). Releases of anything but a family member are unchanged. The window's line (still only when something went or came back) says `K resolution-family members in W whole families, their heaps H MB; X members kept now for a member of their family in use` (X: the members the last pass kept, a count of members, not a sum over passes, as each pass keeps the same ones again), and the totals `W whole resolution families (H MB of heaps)`. A member kept at its last use is not counted for the pass's skip (`tu_kept`, `tu_rec_idle`): counted, the kept members, unused for the wait by definition, made every check run a pass (every 700 ms, the second walk over the sharing table included) for as long as their family lived. The mark holds until the member is used again, a pass finds it not dead (a pin), or an entry leaves the sharing table (`g_alias_gone`, until a pass has asked every entry): a family goes only once a member not dead becomes dead, and such a member is then unused for the wait and not marked, or it left the table. **Asked per family, not per member** (the round of 2026-11-16): Luigi's Mansion 3's dynamic resolution moves in 1-pixel steps, so every size its ~14 scaling targets visited stays a kept member, 4,180 of them by the end of the owner's iPhone run (build 29dcbb4d), and the pass, which asked each one (`alias_dead_one`, whose walk over the member's cells meets every other size at its offset, then `objc_getAssociatedObject` and `tu_kept`), grew from about 0.6 ms to 3.4 ms (its own timing: 26.89 ms for 8 passes at 4,275 covered), every 700 ms under the shared-texture lock. A family stays as soon as one member is not dead, so the pass now asks first the member that kept the family at the last pass (`NvImageHeap`'s hint), then its most recently used member (the largest last use); if either is not dead the family stays and no other member is asked. Only when both are dead are the rest asked, and the rule decides as before (every member the table holds covered and dead, one of them before the pass's limit), so the releases are the old rule's. Each use record keeps its member's family heap (`tu_rec.fam`, set in `tu_register`), so the walk groups members without asking the Objective-C runtime per member (a family about to go whole is checked member by member against its heap); `tex_names_move`, the sharing table's in-place writer, now drops a record a freed texture left at the new texture's address, as `alias_put` does, so no record names another texture's family; the walk reads the records under their lock a block at a time. The members not asked are marked at their last use when unused for the wait, the one found not dead unmarked, so the skip still runs a pass once that member can be dead. The hint is a texture address compared and never followed: a member that leaves the table (a release, a pool's `Finalize`, a takeover) leaves nothing dangling, its address matching no entry or another member of the same family. Pins of members not asked are not counted in `kept by a pin`, and `members kept now` counts the members unused for the wait of a family kept by a member in use. The table's growth stays (a kept member costs no memory: its bytes are the family heap's). On an M-series Mac (`t_dead_targets`, `-O1`, 14 families of 300 sizes plus about 2,100 other covered targets) a pass went from about 35 ms to 0.35-0.43 ms, asking 23 family members instead of about 4,200; not yet measured on a device. `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_FAMILY=0` releases members one by one, as before (`ab` alternates it with the `DM_AB` phases). Not yet measured on a device: the expected effect is a few thousand fewer releases and remakes an hour in Luigi's Mansion 3 (Mario Kart World and Donkey Kong Bananza have families too), a small CPU gain, and no memory change.
- **The wait:** NXNative waits 3 s and looks once a second. An NXNative run on a 2 GB Apple TV waited 1 s and looked every 100 ms, and Ocarina of Time's peak at a camera change (Saria) fell from 2,086 to 1,886 MiB. Here the wait defaults to NXNative's 3 s, with a pass every 700 ms (`DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_MS=1000`: the Apple TV's wait, a pass every 200 ms): a pass reads every texture ID and every shared texture (0.4 ms for 4,200 covered targets on an M4 Max in the test, 0.64 ms on average in NXNative's Ocarina run on an M6), so it costs the submit thread about 2-3 ms a second, where looking every 100 ms would cost twice that for at most 100 ms sooner. A pass is skipped while no covered target has gone unused for the wait (`tu_check`, `tu_any_idle`: a pass only raises a target's last use, so it would release nothing); the round's iPhone logs measured 0.5-0.7 ms a second of passes in every game. A wait cut too short releases targets the game still wants: each comes back as a new texture and a residency commit, which costs power, and the line's `made again` count shows it. `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_MS=3000` is NXNative's own timing.
- **At memory pressure** (`DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE`; B5 of the X2.0 round's optimizations, texture ageing and the jetsam net, its first part): in Ocarina of Time's 55-minute iPhone 18 Pro Max run to Hyrule Field (2026-11-15) the round's first iOS memory warning came at the Field load, 1,580 MiB from the limit, the footprint up 792 MB and the graphics ledger 779 MB in about 8 s, while the old area's targets, which the Field's had drawn over, waited out the 3 s: they went, 457 MB, about 2.5 s after the warning (GPU allocated 8,372 → 7,818 MB between 3093.1 and 3094.1 s; the warning at 3091.4 s). Only the wait changes, never the rule: a target still goes only once another drew over its bytes after its last use. While the headroom to the system memory limit (`os_proc_available_memory`, read when a submission begins at most every 200 ms; iOS only) is under 2,048 MB, until it is back over 2,560 MB, the wait is 300 ms and a check comes every 200 ms (`tu_due`, `tu_low_now`); a memory warning runs a pass at 300 ms at once in the trim, before the waiting heaps go (`tu_press_warning` in `mtl_trim_at`). In that run the headroom went under 2 GB about 2 s before the warning (GPU allocated 7,822 → 8,285 MB from 3087.7 to 3089.0 s, less the 10,290 MB its memory lines pair it with) and back over 2.5 GB after the release; of the log's 332 readings only one more went under 2 GB (2,023 MB at frame 153438, another area load, no warning). That is simpler than telling an area load from a burst of new targets, and as early: no rule can release a target before another draws over it, which the new area's first frames do, and by then the headroom is low. A pressure pass's wait is never shorter than the last 4 frames took (`tu_frames_wait`), so a target used in them stays, however long the load's frames are (up to 790 ms in that run), and never longer than the usual wait. What a pressure pass releases gives its memory back at once (`g_tu_drop`: its recycled heap or its family's heap is freed) instead of waiting in the free list. The back-off (`tu_backoff`): once 8 or more of the targets pressure passes released since the last step or the low spell's start, and a quarter or more of them, are made again within 10 s of their release, the low-headroom wait doubles (600 ms, 1.2 s, 2.4 s, then the usual wait), and it comes down a step each time the headroom recovers; a warning's pass keeps 300 ms. A target released before a step or the spell's start and made again after it counts toward no later step (`g_tu_gone` keeps the window each one went in): counted, 20 of a batch released before the first step and made again after it made a second step on 10 released since, its line reading `20 of 10 textures … made again`. Estimated for that run: the 457 MB go within about 0.5 s of the Field's frames drawing over them instead of 3-3.7 s; the 3 s release puts that moment between about 3089.5 and 3091.0 s, so they would have gone between about 3090.0 and 3091.5 s, before or at the warning, and the peak would have been up to about 450 MB lower. Costs: in a low spell a check every 200 ms (a pass about 1 ms on the phone when one runs), and the targets made again, which the spell's end line counts. `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE=0` turns both off (`ab` alternates them with the `DM_AB` phases; player builds keep it). Under pressure the idle margin that guards against a false "drawn over" shrinks from 3 s to the longer of 300 ms and the last 4 frames: a write stamp (an attachment, an image binding) counts for the whole texture, so a target lying in a part of a larger one that the larger one's writes leave alone (the PR #1453 case) is kept only by a read within that margin, something to watch on the device. Not yet run on a device.
- **Log:** once, `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD: render targets drawn over and unused for N ms are released, a pass every M ms`. Then, every 300 frames in which something went or came back: `render targets drawn over and unused for 3.0 s, released early up to frame F (DM_NVN_RT_DEAD): this window N textures, M MB (and K resolution-family members …), R made again; in all …; a pass every 700 ms, P passes in this window (S more skipped in all: …), T ms`. For an A/B, compare footprint and GPU allocated at the same scene changes with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=0` and the default (and `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_MS=1000` as a third arm), or with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=ab DM_AB=<frames>`, and check the window's MB against the footprint drop and `made again` against new residency commits. At memory pressure: once, `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE: render targets drawn over and unused for 300 ms (and the last 4 frames) are released at a memory warning and while the headroom … is under 2048 MB …`; a low spell's start, `render targets drawn over (DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE): headroom to the system memory limit N MB, under 2048 MB, at frame F: …`, and its end, `… headroom back to N MB, over 2560 MB, at frame F after S s low: N textures, M MB released sooner in it, R of them made again within 10 s; the wait is 3.0 s again`; each release once the GPU is done with it (releases within a second share a line), `render targets drawn over and unused for 0.3 s, released at low memory headroom (DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE): N textures, M MB (and …), freed, not kept for reuse; headroom to the system memory limit B → A MB, footprint B → A MB; from frame F, P passes` (`at a memory warning` for the trim's pass); a back-off step, `… K of N textures released at memory pressure since the low headroom began were made again within 10 s: the low-headroom wait doubles to 0.6 s (back-off step 1)` (`since the last back-off step` for a later one). The window's line gives its pressure releases' waits beside the usual one, `unused for 3.0 s (0.3 s at memory pressure)` (`0.3 to 0.6 s` when they differed), and adds `; N textures, M MB of them released sooner at memory pressure (DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE)` and the memory trim's line `render targets drawn over N (M MB)`. `scripts/devlog.py` lists a low spell's start and each release among the memory events, beside the warning.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_dead_targets`, under Metal validation, on and off: a target drawn over and unused goes, with its serial entry, and its ID drops its view; the one that drew over it stays, as do one nothing overlaps, one read every frame, one whose neighbour wrote before its last use and one a pin holds; drawn into again, it is made again and counted; `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_MS` sets the wait and the pass interval; a check before any target has gone unused for the wait skips the pass, one after runs it; a pass over about 4,200 covered targets releasing 256 takes 0.4 ms on an M4 Max; a family whose two sizes a target of another format drew over goes whole in a call that asks for one, with its heap counted, and a size its family's size in use drew over stays (and goes alone with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_FAMILY=0`); a call that asks for one releases a dead target of no family with kept members ahead of it in the table; a second pass keeping the same member counts one and prints no line; a kept member is not counted for the skip, counts again once an entry leaves the table or a pass finds it not dead (pinned), and its family then goes whole at the next pass. With 14 families of 300 sizes, their largest read every frame, a pass asks at most 2 members a family (each family's member in use) and releases exactly what the old rule (every member asked) releases, over passes where families go whole, a pinned size keeps its family (and is asked first after), the family goes once unpinned, and a family whose member asked first left the table is asked another; the pass's time is printed. With `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=0` nothing is covered or released. At memory pressure, with a stand-in headroom (also run with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE=0`, where nothing goes sooner): the hysteresis over every headroom reading of the Ocarina run's log (low at its warning until 2,643 MB, and at 2,023 MB until 2,719 MB, nowhere else) and over its GPU allocated around the Field load (low from 3089.0 s, 2.4 s before the warning, until 3095.1 s); with 4 GB of headroom an old target drawn over stays after 200 ms; under 2 GB it goes after 600 ms, counted, with its heap freed and not waiting (with the switch off it goes at the usual 3 s and its heap waits); one read 200 ms before stays and goes 400 ms after its read; with four frames of 500 ms the pressure wait is 1.6 s and a target used in them stays, and at 60 fps again it goes; 2,300 MB keeps the spell, 2,600 MB ends it and 2,300 MB from above does not start one; 8 of 12 released targets made again within 10 s double the wait to 600 ms (a warning's stays 300 ms), so a target unused 500 ms stays and goes at 800 ms, one made again 11 s after its release is not counted, and the recovery takes the step back; a critical warning (`mtl_pressure(2)`) releases a target unused 500 ms with 4 GB of headroom and a backed-off wait, before the other classes, counted in the trim's bytes; each group of releases has one line, once the GPU is done with it and a second has passed; over more than one window, 10 of 40 released targets made again take a step, 20 more of them made again after it take none, and 8 of the 10 released since take the second, and a new spell's start begins a window, so 12 of the last spell's made again in it take no step on 4 released in it (each part failed before the fix); no step or spell's end says more made again than released, and the window's line gives the pressure waits; a resolution family and a target in a recycled heap released at low headroom keep their heaps alive and resident while the GPU holds a submission committed before the release (a shared event), and the heaps are freed, not left waiting, once it completes; with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE=ab` the low spell is followed in every phase, but a target drawn over goes at the pressure wait, and a warning's pass releases, only in the phases it is on. Not yet run in a game on this tree.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (Switch 2, `0400486024DEC000`): NXNative's Mac census and its 2 GB Apple TV run; this tree's iPhone run peaked at 5.1 GB of footprint. Any game whose targets move with its resolution or scenes can use it (Mario Kart World, Donkey Kong Bananza, Xenoblade, Breath of the Wild). The family rule: [Luigi's Mansion 3](../../dm/games/0100DCA0064A6000/README.md)'s round log. At memory pressure: the owner's Ocarina of Time run to Hyrule Field of 2026-11-15 (the round's first memory warning).

### GPU-only render-target physical pool wired whole by texture addresses and small lookups

- **Symptom:** on an 8 GB iPhone 17 walking from Hyrule Field into Kakariko Village, Ocarina of Time's residency set reached 3.9 GB (wired memory 5.7 GB of 7.67 GB, 147 MB free). `DM_CTL restop` showed its 305 MB physical render-target pool (`0x18c`) wired whole in the residency set even though the GPU only reads its render targets through Metal textures.
- **Cause:** two. (1) Ocarina places its render targets in `CPU_NO_ACCESS` virtual pools over that 305 MB physical pool and queries `nvnTextureGetTextureAddress` and `nvnMemoryPoolMapVirtual`. Both marked the physical pool addressed for raw global memory (`gmem_map`), even though a block-linear `CPU_NO_ACCESS` texture's texels live exclusively in its Metal texture here and never in guest RAM. (2) Once removed from `gmem_map`, one compute shader still bound a 200 KB storage buffer inside the 305 MB pool every frame, and because `BIG_MIN` was 512 MB, that lookup still created and wired a whole-pool buffer over all 305 MB.
- **Fix:** general (`hle_nvn.c`, `nvn_obj.h`, `nvn_mtl.m`): (1) `nvnTextureGetTextureAddress` does not admit a pool into `gmem_map` for a block-linear, non-buffer texture in `CPU_NO_ACCESS` storage (`tex_bytes_elsewhere`), and `nvnMemoryPoolMapVirtual` (aliased mode) admits a `CPU_NO_ACCESS` virtual pool only if a reachable address was already handed out (`pool_t.asked`). Linear textures, buffer textures, `nvnBufferInitialize`, `nvnMemoryPoolGetBufferAddress`, CPU-visible pools, and draw-time pointer scans still admit the pool. (2) `BIG_MIN` is lowered from 512 MB to 256 MB so 256–511 MB pools wire only the 16 MB chunks lookups actually reach (~31 MB instead of 305 MB). `t_gpu_only_addr` and `t_pool_chunks` check both.
- **Result on Ocarina of Time,** iPhone 17 (8 GB, Kakariko Village, [PR #2566](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2566)): cuts ~274 MB of wired memory on the 305 MB physical target pool (with the rest of PR #2566, residency set 3.5 → 3.1 GB, wired 5.3 → 4.9 GB, frame time unchanged).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (v131072, 2026-11-13).
- **NX 18:** ported from upstream NXNative 3dd1f7a65 (MIT) on 2026-11-22; our `nvnTextureGetTextureAddress` and `MapVirtual` tag their handouts by kind (`mtl_pool_addressed_k`), which stays.

### Shared textures kept by the sharing table alone

- **Symptom:** a long session's memory line keeps `N kept by the sharing table alone, M MB …, the oldest idle K submissions`, K growing with the run. Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (Hyrule Castle, frame 23,400 of the 2026-11-17 round's first run): 190 of them, 425 MB, every byte of it drawn into or shader-written, the oldest unused for 536,436 submissions (about the whole run), at a 5.5 GB footprint with 2.6 GB of headroom; the round's audio drop-out and critical memory warning came at such peaks. Across that run the count read 27-190 and 168-425 MB at every memory line.
- **Cause:** an entry of the sharing table (`g_alias`) that no live or finalized serial, view or pin holds stays so that a texture object made again over the same storage with the same description finds what the old one held. Only a pool's `Finalize`, a takeover by a new texture over its storage (`alias_take_over`) or the release of a target another drew over (`DM_NVN_RT_DEAD`) let one go, so an entry whose storage nothing reuses stays for good.
- **Fix:** every game, off by default since 2026-11-22 ([released while the game still used them](#shared-textures-released-while-the-game-still-used-them); `=1` turns it on) (`shared_idle_locked`, `shared_idle_commit` in `nvn_mtl.m`). At a commit every 1,024 submissions a walk of the table (under the shared-texture lock, at most 256 a walk) releases each such entry that no lookup found for `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE_SUBS` submissions (65,536 by default, about 40-60 s of Ocarina) and whose storage holds what it holds: a texture the GPU cannot draw into or write (no render-target or shader-write usage) in a pool the CPU can read, not sparse, `COMPRESSIBLE`, `LINEAR`, display or video (`tex_storage_ok`: its content came from the storage, and a GPU copy into it is stored there too), or one the GPU can draw into whose writes are stored into its storage ([GPU textures in storage the CPU can read](nvn-objects.md#gpu-textures-in-storage-the-cpu-can-read), `gsb_store`: uncompressed, a format the host neither widens nor makes depth, `gsb_host_ok`). It leaves as a takeover takes an entry (`alias_del`, a note that names no texture): retired behind the submission in flight, its recycled heap waiting for the next target. A new texture object over that storage is then made from the storage, as any is. A resolution family member stays (alone it gives nothing back). `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=0` keeps every entry, as before. `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=2` releases the entries whose content is the GPU's alone as well (a render target in a `CPU_NO_ACCESS` pool, whose storage never gets what the GPU drew): off by default, as a game that makes a texture object again over such storage long after and reads it before drawing would read the storage (zeros or the dummy) instead of what was drawn. The idle wait is long because a texture released and wanted again costs an upload, which must not become a stutter.
- **Log:** once, `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=1: shared textures kept by the sharing table alone and unused for 65536 submissions are released when their storage holds their content`. Each memory line, after the mip drop line: `shared textures kept by the sharing table alone, unused for 65536 submissions, released up to frame F (DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=1): this window N textures, M MB; in all N, M MB; left at the last walk because the GPU's content is in them alone N (M MB) (DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=2 releases them too), because a texture ID still names them N; walks T ms in all`. The left-behind figure is what `=2` would add; compare it with the memory line's `kept by the sharing table alone` MB.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_shared_idle`, under Metal validation, at the default, `=2` and `=0`: a BC1 asset under two keys and an RGBA8 target in a CPU pool go and count their bytes once; a target and a read-only texture in a `CPU_NO_ACCESS` pool and a `COMPRESSIBLE` target stay at the default (counted as left behind) and go at `=2`; an entry looked up lately, one a serial holds, the parent of a view a serial holds and a resolution family member stay; the commit's walk releases an idle entry within 1,024 submissions; with `=0` nothing goes.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), the 2026-11-17 round's logs. Not yet run on a device. Expected at Ocarina's peak: at the default little (all 425 MB were drawn into or shader-written; the line's left-behind figure says how much is the GPU's alone), with `=2` up to the 425 MB of the line's figure once each entry has idled past the wait.

### Shared textures released while the game still used them

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, after the player went to the far side of Hyrule Field and came back: patches of the field near the castle drawn as rectangular blocks of other ground textures, with the grass on them thinned out. Fine at the start of the run, and fine again after a trip into the town (a scene load). No red line. It came back with `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=0`, `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_PART=0` (a run with no partial re-upload at all), `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_COALESCE=0`, `DM_NVN_CACHE_VOLATILE=0`, `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_TRIM=0`, `DM_NVN_HOST_BARRIER=0` and `DM_DRS_GPU_TARGET=0` each, and not in the run with `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE=0` (with three others).
- **Cause:** the [idle release of shared textures](#shared-textures-kept-by-the-sharing-table-alone). Not fully known: it released 7-10 textures (2-3 MB) a run, and one of them the game wanted back with content the texture made again from the storage did not have. Two things found on the way are fixed anyway: "unused" came from the sharing table's lookups alone, while a texture the game samples through its texture pool is found through the ID's cached view and the pool tables, with no lookup; and the release recorded a note of serial 0, which neither the texture ID cache (`ser_sync`) nor the pool tables' patch path (`pool_gpu`) maps to any ID, so the tables kept naming the released texture. In the run with those fixed (cabc2e012) no released texture was named by a registration, and the field still broke.
- **Fix:** `DM_NVN_SHARED_IDLE` is off by default (every game). With `=1` or `=2`: a texture that a live registration's view, type or class view (or a parent of one) names stays (`treg_held_set`; S2Arm `50f032a62`, MIT, whose orphan reclaim turned terrain black), and a release records a note that names everything (`ser_note_all`): the ID cache starts a new epoch and the pool tables are built whole.
- **Log:** with the switch on, the memory line's shared-texture line adds `because a texture ID still names them N`.
- **Check:** `t_shared_idle` (run at the default, `=1` and `=2`): the default releases nothing; with `=1` an idle texture a registration names stays and one no ID names goes, the release leaves an unknown pool-table log entry and starts a new texture ID epoch, and a registration whose pool went holds nothing.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, 2026-11-22, eight device runs (builds 077a34112 and cabc2e012). The default off not yet run on a device.

### Live textures the game keeps but no longer draws

- **Symptom:** in a long open-world session the host's textures keep growing toward the iOS memory limit although the game's live texture objects plateau. S2Arm measured Ocarina of Time on iOS at 345 MB of host textures at frame 600 and 3.1 GB at frame 80,700 (1.4 GB left to the limit). This tree's Ocarina all-data run reached its low-headroom releases at a 6.2 GB footprint (NX18-X2.0 `oot-alldata-analysis.md`); long Tears of the Kingdom and Breath of the Wild sessions stream the same way.
- **Cause:** each live NVN texture object holds a host texture (a packaged upload, or a GPU texture filled from its storage) until the game finalizes the object. An open world keeps thousands of objects alive that it no longer draws with, where on the Switch an object is only a descriptor over guest memory. Finalized textures ([A finalized-texture table that kept every texture it had held](#a-finalized-texture-table-that-kept-every-texture-it-had-held)), render targets drawn over, waiting heaps, spares and snapshots each had a release; a live, idle texture whose content came from guest memory had none.
- **Fix:** every game, ported from S2Arm 8adc5673, 987b31ef (Apache-2.0) (`tex_idle_commit`, `tidle_ids`, `tidle_tex` in `nvn_mtl.m`). At a submission's commit, while the headroom to the system memory limit (`os_proc_available_memory`, read at most once a second; none on the Mac, which never runs it) is under `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_MB` (1536 by default, `0` off), live entries go least recently used first until about twice the threshold is free. An entry qualifies only when every texture ID registered with its serial was bound and none for 3 s (the per-ID stamp the bindings already keep for `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD`, `treg_t used`, now reset when an ID takes another texture; S2Arm counts 300 submissions), no pool table names any of its IDs (`tt1`, a stand-in), its storage is what the host takes content from (`tex_storage_ok`: a pool the CPU can read, not `COMPRESSIBLE`, `LINEAR`, sparse or multisampled; not display or video-decode) and the GPU wrote none of it since the CPU last did (`gws_owned`), its texture has no render-target or shader-write usage, and nothing else names it (one serial entry, no finalized serial, no sRGB twin's view, no pin, not a resolution family member, a view, a buffer or a sparse texture). It goes as a Finalize's entry does: `live_del_locked`, whose deferred release (`defer_obj_unref` / `defer_obj_release`) sits on the same line as the entry's removal (987b31ef's borrow-guard order), so the object waits for this submission's fence and a pointer borrowed while encoding stays valid; its IDs' views and its view-cache set go as deferred unrefs, and its sharing-table entry when nothing else holds it (`alias_del`). The next use makes it again from guest memory. With `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD=0` the ID clock stands still and nothing goes.
- **Log:** once, `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_MB=1536: under this headroom to the memory limit, live textures unused for 3 s whose storage holds their content are released`. Each sweep that releases any (the first 8, then at most one line every 300 frames): `idle live textures released for memory headroom (DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_MB): N, M MB, headroom H MB under 1536 MB (I idle, C of them over storage the GPU did not write), frame F; …` with the totals.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_idle_tex`, under Metal validation, at the default and `=0`: with no headroom known and above the threshold nothing goes; under it the idle CPU-sourced texture goes with its ID's view, its object waiting in the deferred-release queue for the open submission's fence and freed after it; one bound lately, one whose storage the GPU wrote, one a pool table names, a display texture and a pinned one stay; a copy afterwards reads what the CPU wrote (made again from guest memory); the commit itself releases the one bound lately once it too is idle. Taking out the idle, GPU-written, pool-table or display rule fails the test.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), S2Arm's iOS measurement. Not yet run on a device here: headroom insurance for long Ocarina, Tears of the Kingdom and Breath of the Wild sessions. A texture used only by object (a copy source never bound by ID) after its IDs went idle can go at each sweep and be made again: it costs an upload, not content.

### Kept vertex conversions held while the app is in the background

- **Symptom:** a game with long vertex streams in 8- or 16-bit formats keeps its converted vertex buffers (up to 256 MB) resident on an iPhone while the app is in the background.
- **Cause:** [kept vertex conversions](rendering.md#kept-vertex-conversions) went only when their source changed, the GPU wrote it, or it was not drawn for 600 submissions, under a flat 256 MB cap (`VK_BYTES`). The memory trim ([Nothing answered the system's memory pressure](#nothing-answered-the-systems-memory-pressure)) did not touch them.
- **Fix:** every game (`vk_trim_all` in `nvn_mtl.m`). Going to the background, the trim after `dm_gfx_suspend`'s drain drops every kept conversion (the trim line's `kept vertex conversions N (M MB)`; the `vertex conversion:` line counts them as `going to the background`). Each is made again from its source at its next use after the app comes back, without the back-off a changed source gets. A memory pressure warning or critical level leaves them: the game is still drawing them, so they would be made again in the next submission, a CPU hitch for nothing. `DM_BG_TRIM=0` keeps them going to the background too.
- **Note:** the 256 MB cap (`VK_BYTES`) stays flat on iOS near the memory limit, unlike the render-target heaps' ([Render-target heaps kept by a flat cap on iOS](#render-target-heaps-kept-by-a-flat-cap-on-ios)). A conversion the cap refuses is not skipped: it is made again into the frame arena at every submission that draws it, which costs the CPU conversion each time (EA SPORTS FC 27's stadium took 26.8 ms a frame on an M4 converting all of its streams, the cost keeping is there to remove) and holds the same bytes once for each submission in flight. A cap that fell with the headroom (32 MB at 1 GB, about 88 MB at 1.5 GB) would so raise the footprint of a stream drawn every frame, not lower it. A smaller footprint near the limit would come from dropping conversions not drawn for a while, which the 600-submission sweep already does.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_vconv_keep`. Conversions kept through a warning and a critical trim are bound again; the background trim drops both (1.7 MB), their buffers leave the residency set and are freed, and the next sighting keeps the stream again. With 512 MB of headroom, where the render-target heaps' cap falls to 32 MB, a 35 MB conversion is still kept.
- **Found on:** a code review of the memory trim (the comment on `vk_get` names EA SPORTS FC 27's 7.65 million vertices a submission). Not yet measured on a device.

### Pipeline archive files of an older shader chain opened at every start

- **Symptom:** after an app update or a host build with a new shader chain, every start still opens the old chain's pipeline archive files and none of them serves a pipeline. Each open file costs about 1 MB plus 1.6% of its size in memory: Super Mario Odyssey's 41 files of an older chain on the Mac cost 60 MB of footprint and 1.8 s at every start, Mario Kart 44 MB.
- **Cause:** a pipeline's index key names its library by file name only (`lib_name`), not by the chain that translated it, and nothing removed a file of another chain. Only the prebuild's own files were ever trimmed (`pre_trim`), and only when a later prebuild found nothing in them.
- **Fix:** every game. Each index now carries the chain it was written under (`DMPSOIX2`, `arch_keys_write`), and `pso4_init` removes a file of another chain and its index without opening it: `pipeline archive: N files of another shader chain removed without opening them, M MB`. A file written before the stamp (a `DMPSOIX1` index, or none) is opened as before and probed: each lookup that asks it is counted (`arch_probe_note`), and once the prewarm ends (`arch_probe_settle`) a file that served a pipeline gets this chain's stamp, keeping its keys, and a file that served none is removed when its own index named 8 keys it did not hold (`ARCH_PROBE_MISS`) or lookups asked it 256 times (`ARCH_PROBE_ASKS`; not for another program's prebuild file, `pb<seq>_<program>`, in which this program's lookups miss by design). Any other file stays unstamped for a later start. One line gives the outcome: `N files without a chain stamp probed: S served this chain's pipelines and were stamped, R served none and were removed (M MB), U undecided`. A wrong removal only costs a rebuild of its pipelines. A host built before the stamp reads a stamped index as no index, so it looks through such a file in turn, which is slower but not wrong. The depth mode is not in the stamp, since a game sets it after the archive opens and its vertex entry points (`main0`, `main0_z`) already give different keys. Metal's `com.apple.gpuarchiver` folders are never removed on the Mac, since their parent folder is shared by every program (`ga_dir`). The 83ce and fbea `pso_archive` folders on the game drive have no stamp: they are probed at their game's next start, and the index-less `p` files that the prebuild wrote there go only when lookups ask them 256 times. `DM_PSO_KEEP_OLD=1` opens every file as before and removes none.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_archive`, whose `test_pso_archive.py` `test_stale_chain_files` runs two fresh processes over six one-base files. Of them, one carries an older stamp, two have a `DMPSOIX1` index (one of them naming a key it does not hold) and two have no index. The start removes the older one unopened. After five lookups, the probe stamps the two that served a base, keeping the first's keys and giving the index-less one an empty incomplete index, and removes the one with a named miss and the index-less one asked twice for nothing. With `DM_PSO_KEEP_OLD=1` all six stay and nothing is probed.
- **Found on:** the memory audit, on the game drive's `pso_archive` folders (Super Mario Odyssey, Mario Kart, Breath of the Wild, Persona 5 Royal, Luigi's Mansion 3, Catherine and 13 Sentinels: about 6.4 GB of older chains). Not yet measured on a device, where `Documents/pso_archive` gains old-chain files after each app update with a new chain.

### Nothing answered the system's memory pressure

- **Symptom:** an iPhone game ended by the system for memory leaves no line about it: no log has a memory warning, so a jetsam cannot be told from a crash, and nothing idle is given back when the system asks.
- **Cause:** nothing in `dm/` listened for memory pressure (no `DISPATCH_SOURCE_TYPE_MEMORYPRESSURE` source, no `didReceiveMemoryWarning`), and nothing released idle caches on going to the background.
- **Fix:** every game. The front ends watch the system's memory pressure with a dispatch source (`front_ios.m` `front_ios_power_watch`, `front_mac.m` `front_run`, with a window or without) and call `dm_gfx_pressure` (`hle_nvn.c`). It logs the level, the footprint and the room left: on iOS `front end: iOS memory warning (N s after launch): footprint N MiB, N MiB left`, the line `scripts/devlog.py` reads as a memory event, and `os_proc_available_memory`; on the Mac the system's free, purgeable and file-backed memory and the kernel's memorystatus level. It then asks for a trim (`mtl_pressure`), which the next submission's start runs (`mtl_trim_at` in `mtl_sub_begin`). The trim releases what is idle and large: at a pressure warning first the render targets drawn over and unused for 300 ms ([Render targets drawn over stay while their texture objects live](#render-targets-drawn-over-stay-while-their-texture-objects-live), `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD_PRESSURE`), their heaps freed with them rather than filling the list the trim then empties, then render-target heaps waiting for reuse (`rh_trim_all`), the upload slots' blocks above 4 MB and their overflow blocks, packaged-texture spares, same-pass feedback snapshots, the render-target shadows of depth and compressed textures ([Render-target shadows made anew at every size change](#render-target-shadows-made-anew-at-every-size-change)), the finalized-texture table's oldest entries above 512 (a profile's `ghost_table` sets more: [A finalized-texture table too small for a heavy area](frame-rate.md#a-finalized-texture-table-too-small-for-a-heavy-area)), what the deferred-release queue holds whose fence has passed, and it asks the present thread to release edge smoothing and MetalFX if they are off (at its next present, once the last present that ran them has completed). What it releases waits in the deferred-release queue for the fence of the submission that starts there, after which no present or submission committed before it can read it. The guest's files are swept once at the same time when `DM_GUEST_FILE` is on (`dm_guest_file_trim`, `hle_svc.c`). One line gives the MB per class: `memory trim (memory pressure warning): N MB in all: render targets drawn over …, render-target heaps waiting …, upload staging blocks …, packaged-texture spares …, feedback snapshots …, render-target shadows …, deferred-release queue …; footprint N MB before`, and the memory line counts `memory trims N (M MB in total)`. Going to the background, `dm_gfx_suspend(1)` runs the same trim after its drain (`dm_gfx_trim`), for the last fence, which has passed, and it also drops the kept vertex conversions ([Kept vertex conversions held while the app is in the background](#kept-vertex-conversions-held-while-the-app-is-in-the-background)). Each class is made again on its next use, as after the idle trims. `DM_MEMORY_TRIM=0` logs the pressure and trims nothing; `DM_BG_TRIM=0` keeps the trim on going to the background off. `DM_CTL` takes `pressure <0|1|2>` to act as the system's level, since macOS lets only root simulate one (`memory_pressure -S`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_mem_trim`, with the encode thread off and on and with each switch. Two 32 MB heaps waiting, six upload blocks (192 MB) and three snapshots of three sizes, 266.5 MB in all: `dm_gfx_trim` releases every one, they leave the residency set and are freed, and GPU allocated falls from 267 to 0 MB. A pressure warning releases nothing until the next submission starts, which trims; with `DM_MEMORY_TRIM=0` it trims nothing. `dm_gfx_suspend(1)` trims after its drain unless `DM_BG_TRIM=0`.
- **Found on:** the memory audit (both trees had no listener). The saving under a warning is 0 on NieR:Automata, which keeps nothing of these idle, and up to about 255 MB at Ocarina of Time's churn peaks (render-target heaps waiting). Not yet run on a device, where Xcode's Debug > Simulate Memory Warning gives the line.

### Same-pass feedback snapshots kept after the scene that needed them

- **Symptom:** after a scene that snapshots attachments of several sizes, the footprint keeps up to eight attachment-sized textures: Tears of the Kingdom takes 4-5 sizes a frame (1600x896 down to 400x224); NieR:Automata takes one 1920x1080 RG11B10F snapshot (8 MB) a frame.
- **Cause:** the snapshot cache (`g_fbc`, [Same-pass feedback read through a snapshot](rendering.md#same-pass-feedback-read-through-a-snapshot)) replaced an entry's texture only for another shape and never let an unused one go.
- **Fix:** every game (`fb_trim`, at each submission's start). An entry is reused only within the submission it was taken in, so the submission it was last taken in is its last use; an entry no submission has used for 600 submissions (`FB_SNAP_IDLE`, about 10 s at 60 fps) gives its texture to the deferred-release queue, out of the residency set after the next fence. The next snapshot of that size makes a texture again. The memory line gives `feedback snapshots N (M MB; released when idle K, L MB in total; …)`. `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_IDLE=0` keeps them, as before. A snapshot that comes back after this release is kept from then on ([A large feedback snapshot made again each time the game came back to it](#a-large-feedback-snapshot-made-again-each-time-the-game-came-back-to-it)).
- **Check:** `t_mem_trim` (in `check_t_mem_trim`, also with `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_IDLE=0`): three snapshots of three sizes are kept for 599 empty submissions and released at the 600th, counted and freed; with the switch all three stay resident.
- **Found on:** the memory audit, from Tears of the Kingdom's snapshot sizes (GitHub #931). NieR:Automata saves nothing while it takes its snapshot every frame. Not yet measured on a device.

### A large feedback snapshot made again each time the game came back to it

- **Symptom:** a game that samples a large attachment from inside its own pass only every 10 s or more gets a submit stall of 7-10 ms on each such use after the first: the residency lines show the same 64 MB added again and again at `fb_snap`'s `res_add` line, and the memory line's `released when idle` count climbs by one each time. 13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (2026-11-11 log, 330 s): its 4096x4096 RGBA8 snapshot was made three times, the second and third with residency commits of 7.3 and 9.9 ms inside the frames' submissions (frames 2660 and 16227).
- **Cause:** the idle release ([Same-pass feedback snapshots kept after the scene that needed them](#same-pass-feedback-snapshots-kept-after-the-scene-that-needed-them)) lets a snapshot go after 600 submissions without a use (about 10 s at 60 fps), and a use after that makes the texture again. A new texture joins the residency set, and the set is committed before the submission's command buffer goes, on the submit thread. That commit has to happen there: a command buffer that reads the snapshot must not be committed before the texture is resident, or the GPU faults on it. So the cost cannot move off the thread, only be avoided.
- **Fix:** every game (`fb_keep_try`, `fb_gone_note` and `fb_trim` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The idle release remembers the shape (pixel format, width, height) of each texture it lets go, the last 8 shapes. A texture made again for a remembered shape is kept from then on: the idle release skips it. The game has shown it comes back after a pause longer than the idle limit, so releasing it again would cost the same commit at each return, while keeping it costs only its memory. What is kept so is capped at 128 MB in all (`FB_SNAP_KEEP_MB`: the heap of one 64 MB snapshot with room for smaller ones, or two 48 MB snapshots; about 1.6% of the 7.96 GB the iPhone still had to its limit above 13 Sentinels' 2.1-2.4 GB footprint); a texture that would pass the cap is released when idle, as before. The cap counts the memory each kept snapshot holds: the recycled heap `rh_place` made it in (an eighth larger than a new texture, so a fresh 64 MB snapshot holds 72 MB), else its own allocation. The recycled heap does not save the commit by itself: a released snapshot's heap waits in the list only 3 s (`RH_IDLE_NS`) before it leaves the residency set, so a return after 10 s or more takes a new heap. A kept texture is by design the one used least often, so when a new shape needs an entry, `fb_snap` gives it any other entry no draw can reuse (an empty one, or another shape's texture) before the kept one; ranking those alike had the kept texture go whenever a shape came back to an emptied entry, two or three shapes over a long run being enough. A memory trim ([Nothing answered the system's memory pressure](#nothing-answered-the-systems-memory-pressure)) still releases every snapshot, kept ones included. Under memory pressure it also forgets the shapes, so a snapshot is kept again only once it has come back again. Going to the background keeps the shapes, so the snapshot made again on return is kept at once. The first return still makes the texture once (the release cannot know beforehand that the game will come back); every later one finds it. Each time a texture is made again for a remembered shape the log says `feedback snapshot WxH (pixel format P, N MB) made again after the idle release: kept from now on` (or `over the cap`), at most 16 lines a run. The memory line adds `kept after coming back N, M MB, made so K in total` to its feedback snapshot figures. `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_KEEP=<MB>` sets the cap, and `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_KEEP=0` restores the old rule. `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_IDLE=0` still keeps every snapshot.
- **Check:** `t_mem_trim` section 5 (in `check_t_mem_trim`, also with `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP_KEEP=0`): the 640x360 snapshot that section 4 let go is made again and kept through 601 idle submissions, still resident, while a 200x100 one never let go is released. A background trim (`dm_gfx_trim(3)`) releases the kept one, and the one made after it is kept at once. A pressure trim (`mtl_trim(1)`) forgets the shape, so the next one is not kept. Of three 4096x3072 RGBA8 snapshots (48 MB each) that came back, the 128 MB cap keeps two, and the full trim releases them. With all 8 entries taken, the kept one first, and the 7 others released when idle, a new shape takes an emptied entry and the kept texture stays the same object, kept and resident. With the switch, none is kept. On a device: in 13 Sentinels' `log.txt` the line `made again after the idle release: kept from now on` appears once a run for its 4096x4096 snapshot (once more after each memory trim); more often means its kept texture was given to another shape, and `over the cap` means the cap is too small. On the Mac (Xcode-beta, all six `check_t_mem_trim` runs pass) each 48 MB snapshot holds a 54 MB heap, and two of them fit the cap. Checked on Linux before by a simulation of the cache's code (13 Sentinels' pattern, one use every 700 submissions for 30 uses: 2 textures made instead of 30).
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md#found-on-this-game), iPhone 18 Pro Max log of 2026-11-11. Two avoidable re-makes in 330 s, 17.2 ms of submit in all, worth at most about one dropped frame each; they are not the cause of that run's drop bursts (GPU spikes and runs of 33 ms frames). The fix saves one of those two in that run: frame 2660's re-make is the first return, which still makes the texture and pays its commit, and only frame 16227's goes. Not yet measured on a device: with the fix, the residency lines should show `fb_snap`'s 64 MB add twice in a run (the first creation and the first return) and no more.

### Edge smoothing's blend item list sized for every pixel

- **Symptom:** turning edge smoothing on costs about 33 MB at 1080p, 16 MB of it a list that is almost empty.
- **Cause:** CMAA2's deferred blend items (8 bytes each) had room for one per pixel. The bench's dense foliage at 1080p wants 237,056 of them (11.4 % of the pixels), the typical picture 34,845.
- **Fix:** every game that turns edge smoothing on (`aa_make`, [Edge smoothing in the present](rendering.md#edge-smoothing-in-the-present)). The list starts at a quarter of the pixels (`aa_items_frac`), 11.9 MB less at 1080p (12.9 MB of lists instead of 24.7 MB). A run whose items ran past the list (CMAA2's overflow bit 2, read at the present's completion in `aa_note`) makes the list one per pixel from the next present on, for every size, and logs `CMAA2's blend items ran past their list (N wanted): one per pixel from the next present on`; that one run smooths fewer edges, as any clamp does. The create line gives the share: `blend items for 25 % of the pixels`. `DM_PRESENT_AA_ITEMS=<fraction>` sets the start; `=1` is one per pixel, as before. The present thread (`aa_make`) and the present's completion handler (`aa_note`) both ask for the share, so the switch is read once (`dispatch_once`) and the share kept in an atomic, and a bad value gives its `[RED]` line once.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_post_aa`, which also runs with `DM_PRESENT_AA_ITEMS=1` and with shader validation. The live list holds a quarter of the pixels; 1080p's lists are 12.9 MB against 24.7 MB; the bench's 1080p dense foliage fits with room. The same foliage at 256x192 wants 42.7 % of the pixels: that present's list runs full and says so, and the next present has one per pixel, no overflow, and the same picture as a list of one per pixel from the start. Before any present, two threads that ask for the share at once, the first held inside the read, read the switch one time and get the same share.
- **Found on:** the memory audit (CMAA2 at 1080p held 32.7 MB in Persona 4 Golden's iPhone log). Small pictures with dense foliage can want more than a quarter, so they pay one clamped run before the list grows. Not yet measured on a device.

### Three drawables kept for the game layer

- **Symptom:** the game layer keeps three drawables of the picture's size: 7.9 MiB each at 1920x1080 on an iPhone, 28.6 MiB each at a MacBook Pro's 3456x2168 window.
- **Cause:** `maximumDrawableCount` was never set, so Metal kept its default of 3.
- **Fix:** an experiment, not a default. `DM_DRAWABLE_COUNT=2` gives the game layer two drawables on the iPhone, iPad and Mac (`front_drawable.h`, applied in `front_ios.m` and `front_mac.m`), and the log says `front end: 2 drawables (DM_DRAWABLE_COUNT)`; `=3` sets three; unset keeps Metal's 3, as before, and another value is refused with a red line. Two can make `nextDrawable` wait, which shows as dropped frames.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> run` runs `t_drawable_count`: a new layer has 3; unset and empty leave it; 2 and 3 set it; 4, x and 23 are refused and leave it as it was. On a device, an A/B of `DM_DRAWABLE_COUNT=2` against unset at 60 and 120 Hz with `scripts/devlog.py`: the on-screen dropped frames and the frame-interval p99.
- **Found on:** the memory audit. Not yet run on a device.

### Residency changes waited for another thread's commit

- **Symptom:** in a frame that makes many new textures (a scene's first), `res_add` on the submit thread takes tens of milliseconds in all, a few milliseconds at a time, though each is a hash-table insert.
- **Cause:** `res_commit` held `g_res_mu`, the lock every `res_add` and `res_remove` takes, for the whole `[MTLResidencySet commit]`, and the encode thread (`nvn_rec.h`) and the one-off command buffers (`once_commit`) held it for each queue commit. A commit that adds memory takes about 0.04 ms per MB while the GPU is busy (well under a millisecond on an idle one), and the upload batches' completion handlers commit their staging's removal on their own threads. So the submit thread, adding the next textures, waited for those commits.
- **Fix:** every game (a lock and plumbing change; port of NXNative 0c078130, PR #2379). `res_add` and `res_remove` only queue the change, with a reference to its allocation, under `g_res_mu`; `res_commit` takes the queue and applies and commits it under `g_res_commit_mu`, which nothing takes inside `g_res_mu`. A `res_commit` returns once every change queued before it is committed, also one another thread's commit took (that commit holds `g_res_commit_mu` until it is done), and a removed allocation stays alive until the commit that takes it out of the set.
