That commit releases its queue references under `g_res_commit_mu` (still outside `g_res_mu`), before it marks the changes done, so a concurrent `res_commit` never returns while another thread still holds the last reference to removed descriptors and their recycled heaps (ported from NXNative 7f015ff87). The queue commits that held `g_res_mu` to stay apart from the set's commit (the present snapshot's, the one-off command buffers', and the encode thread's for every command buffer) hold `g_res_commit_mu`. Every taker goes through `res_commit_lock`, which tries the lock first and counts a wait only when it blocks: the `translation detail` line (`DM_FIELD_LOG` on) ends with `the replay for the residency commit lock … ms (N), other threads for it … ms (N)` per frame ([A submit cost that does not say which thread spent it or what it waited for](frame-rate.md#a-submit-cost-that-does-not-say-which-thread-spent-it-or-what-it-waited-for)).
- **What to read:** the `residency set up to frame` line's `commits N / … ms` is the commits' time, which now also holds applying the queued `addAllocation` / `removeAllocation` calls (they were in `res_add` / `res_remove`, outside the timed part, before); the time other threads used to wait behind them in `res_add` is gone from the replay's wall clock, and what the replay still waits for a commit under way on another thread (its own `res_commit`) is the line's `the replay for the residency commit lock`.
- **Found on:** Nintendo Switch Sports Resort (`04008E001F098000`, no profile yet) upstream, the court's first frame (855 textures, 908 MB) on an M2 Max: the submit thread's `res_add` calls in that frame went from 42-56 ms in all (the longest 8.8-10.5 ms) to 1.5-1.7 ms (0.08-0.13 ms), and the frame from 242-263 ms to 224-232 ms (two 75 s runs each from the same state). Ported for [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), whose iPhone 18 Pro Max area loads take 235-585 ms with hundreds of new textures and 10-25 ms residency commits, and about 1.7 s of commits in the first start; not yet run on the phone.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_res_commit`: 200 `res_add` / `res_remove` while another thread holds a commit for 100 ms take under a millisecond, and a `res_commit` then waits for that commit and commits its own changes; a change another thread's commit took is in the set when `res_commit` returns (50 racing rounds); a removed buffer stays alive until its removal is committed, then goes. `check_t_wait_event`'s `wait_counters` counts a `res_commit` behind a held commit lock as one other thread's wait and an uncontended one as none.

### A long residency commit held back every GPU completion

- **Symptom:** the picture stops while audio goes on, with nothing in the log ([A freeze with nothing in the log](threads.md#a-freeze-with-nothing-in-the-log)). Now a residency or queue commit, or a wait for the residency commit lock, of 100 ms or more says so: `[RED] slow residency commit: N ms (adds A, B MB; removes R, S MB) on thread T (#k)`, `[RED] slow queue commit: N ms at <site> on thread T (#k)` or `[RED] slow wait for the residency commit lock: N ms at <site> on thread T; held by host thread H from <site> (for N ms as the wait began) (#k)`, with `, on the feedback queue` when it ran there.
- **Cause:** every Metal 4 commit feedback handler runs on one serial queue (`g_feedback_q`), and two of them committed the residency set there under `g_res_commit_mu`: the upload batch's (the staging it retires) and the one-off command buffer's (what `once_use` made resident); the whole-window screenshot's readback also made and committed its one-off command buffer from the offscreen present's handler. So one long `[g_res commit]`, or a long queue commit holding the lock on the encode thread, held back every completion behind it (`g_done`, the presents, the game's SyncWaits), and nothing timed either kind of commit: the `residency set up to frame` line only sums them every 300 frames. A pool lookup that made its pool resident also committed under `g_mu`, so every other pool lookup waited for that commit too.
- **Fix:** every game. No feedback handler commits the residency set: the upload batch's handler and the one-off command buffer's only queue the removal (`res_remove`, a moment under `g_res_mu`) and hand the commit to the residency commit worker, a serial queue of its own at user-interactive QoS (`res_commit_async`, `nvn_mtl.m`). A removal is still committed only once the GPU is done with what it removes: the handler queues it after the GPU has finished, and any commit after that takes it. An upload batch that retires staging counts as pending (`g_up_pending`) until the worker has committed it, so `nvnQueueFinish` and the drain still return with its staging gone; one with nothing to retire (its slot's own blocks, the usual case) is done in the handler as before. `once_wait` commits its own command buffer's removal on the waiting thread once it wakes, so a readback buffer is still gone when the readback returns; a wait that ends at the suspend's limit hands it to the worker. The screenshot readback starts on the worker. `pool_res` commits after `g_mu` is let go (`pool_res_commit`); `g_pool_res_open` counts the pools marked resident and not committed yet, and a lookup that sees one commits too (waiting for the commit under way), so no lookup returns a pool before it is in the committed set; the pool table (`gmem_map`) counts the pools it marks the same way. Each commit is timed (`CLOCK_UPTIME_RAW`, two reads): `res_commit` (the queued `addAllocation` / `removeAllocation` calls and `[g_res commit]`), the queue commits (`[g_q4 commit:]` on the encode thread, `RO_COMMIT` and `RO_COMMIT_REAL`, in `rec_commit` and `rec_commit_upload` without it, `once_commit` and `snap_take`), and a blocked `res_commit_lock` (its site and the holder's, from the lock note). Each kind says its first 20, then every 100th.
- **What to read:** the lines name the stuck step directly. A slow residency commit with large adds is wiring (a new pool, a scene's textures); one on the residency commit worker no longer holds back completions. A slow wait names who held the lock and where (`nvn_mtl.m:<line>` of `res_commit`, `nvn_rec.h` for the encode thread's queue commit). A commit that never returns writes no line of its own: the freeze detector's dump names the lock's holder, and its upload line and `up_wait_zero`'s 10 s line say `residency commit worker N commit(s) queued, the current one for N s` when upload batches are pending on the worker rather than on the GPU (`rcw_text`).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#open): test 3 (`baf45a97`) froze once on an iPhone 18 Pro Max as Link stepped into water, with nothing logged; a long residency commit on the feedback queue was one of the paths the audits could not rule out. Not run on a device yet.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_res_commit`: a 150 ms wait for the commit lock says so once with both sites; with the threshold lowered (`g_slow_commit_ns`) a residency commit's line has its adds, removes and sizes and says when it ran on the feedback queue, 20 lines then every 100th, and a one-off command buffer's queue commit has its site; with the commit lock held on another thread an upload batch's and a one-off command buffer's handlers return and the feedback queue runs on, their removals stay queued (the batch pending, its staging alive) until the lock is free, then the worker commits them and frees the staging (while it waits, the freeze detector's dump shows its commit in progress beside the pending batch, then `idle`); `once_wait` returns with its readback buffer freed; a pool lookup marks its pool resident and lets go of `g_mu` while its commit waits, and neither it nor another thread's lookup of that pool returns before the pool is committed.

### A finalized-texture table that kept every texture it had held

- **Symptom:** the memory line's headroom to the system memory limit falls through a long run while `finalized textures still referenced (table of 4096, …)` climbs and stays: Ocarina of Time test 3 (`baf45a97`) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max held about 2,200 finalized textures in a four-minute run with the headroom down to 2.3 GB, where test 2 (a table of 512) had 3.3-4.0 GB.
- **Cause:** the finalized-texture table (`g_ghost`, `tex_put` in `nvn_mtl.m`) was a ring whose oldest entry went only when a new one came, and nothing but a memory trim at a pressure warning let one go. Each entry holds its texture alive and resident (a retired one waits in `g_retire_held`), so with the profile's ceiling of 4,096 ([A finalized-texture table too small for a heavy area](frame-rate.md#a-finalized-texture-table-too-small-for-a-heavy-area)) every texture a heavy area put there stayed after the game had moved on, until the ring wrapped over it.
- **Fix:** every game, after NXNative 034e91a8 (without its `nvn_owner.m` refactor, which this tree does not have). The table is kept by use: each entry's last lookup or put (`tex_get`, `mtl_tex_pin`, `tex_put`: the frame and the submission being encoded) is kept in a list from least to most recently used (`ghost_used`). At the ceiling a put lets the least recently used go unless a submission still in flight used it (its submission past `g_done`, `ghost_in_flight`): the table then goes past the ceiling rather than push out what the GPU reads and the next submission resolves again, up to twice the ceiling, where the oldest goes all the same (its texture waits for its fence, as every entry's does), so a GPU that stops completing cannot grow it without end. Past the ceiling a put lets two go once the GPU is done with them, so the table comes back down to the ceiling as the game keeps putting (with one a put, a game replaying more than its ceiling held up to twice it until 600 idle frames). At each submission's commit (`ghost_commit` from `mtl_sub_commit`) up to 32 entries unused for 600 frames or 16,384 submissions go, least recently used first, while the table is above 512 (or a smaller ceiling): a table that grew in a heavy area drains back once its textures idle. On iOS, when the headroom to the memory limit (`os_proc_available_memory`, the memory line's figure, read at a commit at most every 250 ms while the table is above 512) is under 3 GB, every entry above 512 that no submission in flight uses goes at once; a memory trim (`ghost_trim`) does the same. The ceiling stays for a game that replays more than it holds (the thrash case), and `ghost_table` / `DM_NVN_GHOST` still set it. For a game whose table never passes 512 the entry that goes at the ceiling is the least recently used rather than the oldest put, an entry the GPU is still reading is kept (up to twice the ceiling), and each commit takes one more lock.
- **Log:** the start line now reads `finalized-texture table: up to N textures that finalized texture objects still resolve to, the least recently used going when a new one comes, idle ones drained back to 512 (…)`. The `finalized-texture table up to frame` line every 300 frames ends with `; in the window D idle entries drained, L let go at low memory headroom`, and a window with a drain or a drop and no put writes one too. A drop at low headroom writes `finalized-texture table: headroom to the system memory limit M MB, under 3072 MB: N entries let go at once, H held now (down to 512 but for what submissions in flight use), frame F`, at most every 300 frames.
- **What to read:** `H held` above the ceiling means the GPU is behind (entries in flight), and near twice it a GPU that is not completing. With the drain working, `finalized textures still referenced` goes back toward 512 within about 600 frames of leaving a heavy area. `scripts/devlog.py` sums the drained and the dropped in its `ghost table` row.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#open): test 3 on an iPhone 18 Pro Max. Not run on a device yet.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_ghost`: `lru` (an entry looked up again or pinned outlasts the ones not used again), `in_flight` (a full table goes past its ceiling while its entries are in use, to twice it and no further; once the GPU passed the fence a put lets two go, back to the ceiling, then one a put; a release stops at what the submission being encoded put), `idle_drain` (nothing at 599 frames, 32 a commit from 600 back to 512 in 48 commits with an entry used again kept, through a real `mtl_sub_commit` too, by 16,384 submissions with the frame standing still, nothing below a ceiling of 100), `low_headroom` (2.9 GB takes 1,500 to 512 at once with one line, 3.2 GB does not, the headroom read at most every 250 ms, entries the submission in flight put stay), the line's drained and dropped counts, and the list's links and order after each; disabling the lookup's stamp, the in-flight test, the idle-by-submissions test or the low-headroom drop each fails it.

### GPU readback without an autorelease pool

- **Symptom:** GPU memory grows with repeated `nvnTextureReadTexels` calls on a long-lived game thread, even after each readback completes.
- **Cause:** the GPU path in `mtl_read_texels` runs outside a submission's autorelease pool. Metal's temporary command buffer and blit encoder keep the readback buffer alive in the calling thread's undrained pool.
- **Fix:** `mtl_read_texels` encloses texture resolution, readback encoding, the GPU wait and the CPU copy in its own `@autoreleasepool`. The guest-memory path needs no Metal objects.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md), measured upstream in MacNativeGames commit `635fb31` and ported in [PR #195](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/195): about 130 readbacks a second retained 3.7 GB of GPU memory after 30 minutes. Those game measurements have not been repeated here.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 check_t_readback` checks literal GPU and CPU readback pixels and observes the real readback buffer through a weak reference while the caller's outer pool remains open. Removing only the local pool leaves that buffer alive and fails the lifetime check.

### Completion readbacks survive a guest waiter's pool

- **Symptom:** process footprint and Metal allocation grow during stationary gameplay even after readback fences complete; the deferred-release queue and command allocator sizes remain bounded.
- **Cause:** `post_run` can execute on a long-lived guest waiter through the GPU-event fast-wake path, not only on Metal's feedback thread. `MTLBuffer.contents` returns an interior pointer (`NS_RETURNS_INNER_POINTER`), whose receiver may be retained and autoreleased by ARC. Explicit CF readback/visibility ownership is balanced, but those temporary receiver lifetimes escape into the guest thread's undrained outer pool. A submission's pool or a later callback on another thread cannot drain it.
- **Fix:** give the shared `post_run` body its own autorelease pool, ending after its mutex unlock. Guest-storage writes, event effects and existing GPU retirement fences keep their order. This caller drains its temporary completion objects before returning; this is not a new global drain-before-completion synchronization guarantee.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_readback` (`store_completion`) gates real GPU feedback and exercises both `DM_NVN_FAST_WAKE=1` guest-owned completion and `0` callback-owned completion, with the guest waiter's outer pool open throughout. It checks literal copied pixels in the storage and event visibility at a successful wait, and delayed-feedback idempotence. Its buffer-lifetime assertion went with the readback into storage (2026-11-10, now [stored on the GPU](nvn-objects.md#gpu-textures-in-storage-the-cpu-can-read)), whose landed buffer it watched; the pool still covers the visibility buffers `post_run` reads, which no test watches.
- **Found on:** [Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#memory), M2 Pro stationary cave route. A bounded paired diagnostic on chain `d1043b780a74` showed footprint growth of 617 MiB over about 86 seconds without the pool versus 1 MiB over about 83 seconds with it. This is route-local memory evidence, not an FPS improvement, complete allocation census or verified fix for an iPhone termination.

### A pipeline archive on an external drive is read into memory

- **Symptom:** `footprint` shows `Malloc Large` about the size of `pso_archive/` (the log's `pipeline archive …: N files, … MB`), resident or swapped for the whole run. `MallocStackLogging=1` with `malloc_history <pid> -allBySize` gives `newArchiveWithURL` → `MTL4ArchiveImpl::loadFromURL` → `LoaderGlobalState::loadFile` → `-[NSData initWithContentsOfFile:options:maxLength:error:]` → malloc.
- **Cause:** where the file is. Metal loads each archive through `NSData` with `NSDataReadingMappedIfSafe`, and Foundation counts a file on an external APFS drive on USB (`Removable Media: Fixed`) as unsafe to map: it reads the whole file into allocated memory. On the internal disk it maps the file. `newArchiveWithURL:error:` takes only a URL.
- **Fix:** `arch_open_begin` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game: while the host opens its archives (one window around every open, the archive bundled with the app, `Resources/pso/<gpu>.mtl4a`, and the device's `pso_archive/`), it replaces that `NSData` method's implementation, and a read of a `.mtl4a` asks for `NSDataReadingMappedAlways`. It is a private method, so a system without it reads the files as before. A workaround of the framework's choice, as no public call offers one. The game already runs from that drive, so a drive pulled out ends the run either way. Mapped files are clean pages the system can drop, and they do not count in the footprint.
- **Check:** a 133 MB archive on the external drive: footprint +129 MB without it, +2 MB with it. Ocarina of Time on its walk with a 3.5 GB archive: footprint 15.0 → 11.8 GB, peak 16.7 → 12.8 GB, `Malloc Large` 3.5 GB → 59 MB, opening 4.6 → 3.0 s. Long frames did not change. The bundled archive first opened before the window, so an app on an external drive still read it whole: Ocarina of Time, app on an external SSD, same save and scene, footprint 19.0 → 12.2 GB, `Malloc Large` 5,032 → 135 MB (one 4.8 GB `NSData`), 60 fps either way (GitHub PR #1817).
- **Found on:** [Kirby Star Allies](../../dm/games/01007E3006DDA000/README.md): the same stage route gave a footprint of 5,109 MB with its 1.13 GB archive on the external drive and 4,013 MB on the internal disk. [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game). iOS was not measured; its files are on the internal disk.

### Pool tables and readback buffers made anew each frame

- **Symptom:** the periodic `residency set up to frame` line shows hundreds of allocations added and removed per 300 frames in a scene where nothing loads: Ocarina of Time, 1,500 (`pool_gpu` 900 of 180 KB, `mrbuf_get` 600 of 0.5 MB), and 650 commits.
- **Cause:** a replaced pool table was released and a new buffer made, about 3 a frame. The readback cache turned a buffer away when it was full (16 buffers or 16 MB) and never evicted, so once it held other sizes every readback of a new size was a new buffer.
- **Fix:** `pool_table_take` and `pool_table_give` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game. A replaced table is kept with the fence of the submission that may read it, which the last segment's completion covers, and reused at the same size (rounded up to 64 KB) once that has passed. `mrbuf_put` evicts its oldest buffers to make room. A walk also skips an ID whose texture type it has seen and which is not the table's (`tt1`), so that ID is neither looked up nor charged to the budget. The idea of reusing tables and skipping by type comes from [PR 1428](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1428).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback` (`reuse_tables_case`). Ocarina of Time: 11 tables made and 18,990 reused in 11,700 frames; residency per 300 frames, added 1,506 to 6, commits 654 to 12, and 47 to 0.4 ms. Long frames did not change from this alone. The `pool walk` line reports the counts.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### Render-target shadows made anew at every size change

- **Symptom:** the periodic `residency set up to frame` line shows hundreds to thousands of allocations added per 300 frames, nearly all from one `nvn_mtl.m` line, with about 600 residency commits, in a scene where nothing loads: Luigi's Mansion 3 on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, `line 5662` with 1,300-1,900 allocations and about 1,600 MB per 300 frames (the line of `res_add(sh); res_add(buf)`, now in `bcrt_slot`). The game draws into a depth texture through a colour view, or into a compressed texture through an uncompressed one (`render target in a depth texture: view format … drawn into a …-block shadow` once in the log).
- **Cause:** such a pass draws into a colour shadow, copied in before and back after it ([A depth texture drawn through a colour view](rendering.md#a-depth-texture-drawn-through-a-colour-view), [Compressed textures as render targets through an uncompressed shadow](rendering.md#compressed-textures-as-render-targets-through-an-uncompressed-shadow)). The shadow and its staging buffer were cached by the attachment's number in the pass, which starts at 0 again every pass, and made again whenever that number's format or size differed from its last pass. Luigi's Mansion 3 draws D16 shadow maps of 1024, 512 and 256 square through R16 views, one attachment per pass, so attachment 0's shadow was made again about 3 times a frame: a private texture and a private buffer each time, added to the residency set with a commit and removed later with another.
- **Fix:** every game (`bcrt_slot`, `bcrt_cap`, `bcrt_trim` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The 8 entries are found by the shadow's Metal format and size in blocks: a pass takes an entry of its shape that is not attached in the same pass (two attachments of one shape get two shadows), else an empty one, else the least recently used one not attached in the pass, whose old pair goes through the deferred-release queue behind the open submission's fence. The cache keeps at most 64 MiB of shadows and buffers (`BCRT_CAP`; beyond it the least recently used entries not attached in the open pass go, and the first time that happens one line names it: `render-target shadows: the cache holds … MiB, over its 64 MiB …` with every entry's size, Metal format and MiB), and an entry no pass has taken for 5 s goes at the next submission's start (`BCRT_IDLE_NS`), as does every entry at a memory trim. The copies in and back are as before: every pass copies its base's blocks in first and back out after it, so which entry it takes cannot change a picture. The residency line gains `render-target shadows: passes N, made N, reused N, retired N, M MB kept` (passes times 4 is the copies these passes cost). `DM_NVN_BCRT_CACHE=0` takes the entry by attachment number again, as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_bcrt_cache` (Mac): three D16 sizes cycled 20 times in a submission and once in each of 3 more make 3 shadows and each texture reads back its own value; two attachments of one size in a pass get two shadows; nine sizes through the 8 entries let the least recently used go with every value right, and with `DM_RES_CHECK=1` nothing is released too early; a 32x32x32 BC4 3D texture drawn one plane per pass through an RGBA16UI view makes one shadow; six 16 MiB shapes keep the cache within 64 MiB; the residency site table counts 2 additions per shadow made at `bcrt_slot`'s `res_add` line (6 for the three sizes); an entry unused for over 5 s goes at the next submission's start, and `dm_gfx_trim` lets every entry go and returns their bytes. It runs with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=0` and `=1`, and once with `DM_NVN_BCRT_CACHE=0` checking the old counts. Not yet measured on a device: an iPhone A/B of `DM_NVN_BCRT_CACHE=1` against `0` in the same room should show the line's additions fall to a few once and the commits from about 600 to under 20 per 300 frames. Read it by the `; render-target shadows:` suffix of the residency line (`made` a few once, then 0, and `reused` about `passes`), not by the `line N` count: other changes to `nvn_mtl.m` move the line number. If a game's shadows add up to more than 64 MiB, `made` and `retired` keep rising and the one-time `over its 64 MiB` line names the shapes. Diagnosed from the iPhone log, the churn is not what holds the game below 60 fps (its GPU time is), so no frame-rate gain is expected from this alone.
- **Found on:** [Luigi's Mansion 3](../../dm/games/0100DCA0064A6000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### A texture in a sparse pool came out CPU-written at its first use

- **Symptom:** with a profile that has `tex_dirty_upload`, a texture in a sparse pool that the game draws into or writes as an image logs `CPU-written texture ser=…` at its first use, and takes its content from its storage. `t_sparse_tiles` crashed at `and unmapped, not drawn: its heap tile goes back` when built with such a profile, and passed with one without the key.
- **Cause:** `ft_add` listed it for later uploads. A sparse pool's addresses are no range `nvn_dirty` tracks, and an untracked range counts as written, so `nvn_ft_written` found it written at its first use. The list exists for memory the CPU writes, which those addresses are not.
- **Fix:** `ft_add` in `hle_nvn.c`, for every game: a texture in a sparse pool is not listed. What the CPU writes to the physical pages behind one is seen through the page table ([sparse textures](rendering.md)). A virtual pool aliased onto one physical pool has no sparse state and stays listed.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0100F2C0115B6000 check_t_sparse_tiles` passes under both kinds of profile. No game was seen drawing wrong from this.
- **Found on:** the unit test, on Tears of the Kingdom's and Ocarina of Time's profiles. Monster Hunter Rise is the other game with the key and a sparse pool.

### Memory that steps at area loads read as a leak

- **Symptom:** `scripts/devlog.py`'s memory section gives a run's footprint, GPU allocated memory and shared textures as a steep slope: `footprint 731 → 4486 MB (… +5290.2 MB per 10 min)` for Ocarina of Time at 60 fps on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, 9.6 minutes, which reads as a leak of half a gigabyte a minute.
- **Cause:** the slope was one least-squares line over the whole run, and the memory of that run rose in steps where the game loaded an area and stayed about flat in between: footprint 765 MB to 199 s, 2829 MB to 292 s, 3402 then 3833 MB to 411 s, about 4500 MB to the end. The line through the steps is the steps.
- **Fix:** devlog reads each memory series as plateaus: a run of samples within 8% of their median (at least 64 MB; 20% and 16 for counts of textures, which churn more), where a sample outside the band starts the next plateau only when the sample after it is outside on the same side too. With more than one plateau it prints them under the series (`24–199 s 765 MB, 205–292 s 2829 MB, …`) and, instead of the run's slope, the drift within them: one slope fitted to all plateaus at once with a level of each plateau's own, over two minutes of plateaus or more (`+454.6 MB per 10 min within them`). A single plateau prints as before. In a comparison of two logs the rows are the drift (`footprint drift / 10 min`) and the number of plateaus. A sample that two lines give for the same moment (a memory line and an on-screen line) counts once.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py`: three flat steps give three plateaus and no drift where the run's slope is over 3,000 MB per 10 minutes; a rise of 10 MB a minute within two plateaus is a drift of 100; one spike is no plateau; a flat series prints as before.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#memory) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max. What drift is left there is mostly the plateau after the first load (2591 → 2863 MB in 90 s) and the churn of the last one (4109–4854 MB).

### devlog compares the graphics ledger, not GPU allocated

- **Symptom:** two logs compared with `scripts/devlog.py` differ by gigabytes of `GPU allocated` that the footprint does not show: NieR:Automata's heavy scene reads about 4.0 GB where the memory Metal holds is about 2.6 GB.
- **Cause:** `GPU allocated` is `currentAllocatedSize`, which also counts the zero-copy buffers over the guest's memory pools, so the guest's memory is counted once as guest memory and again as GPU memory (Ocarina of Time on the Mac: 10,990 MB against a graphics ledger of 4,114 MB).
- **Fix:** the memory line carries `graphics ledger N MB` (the kernel's graphics ledger) right after `GPU allocated N MB`, and `guest charged N MB` after it with `DM_MEM_GUEST=1`. devlog reads both, prints them first in its memory section with `GPU allocated (counts the guest's pools again)` after them, and in a comparison lists `graphics ledger` and `guest charged` (last, most, drift per 10 minutes, lower is better) before the `GPU allocated` rows. A log from before the fields has neither row, and a default log (no `DM_MEM_GUEST`) has no guest charged row; both read as before.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py` (`LedgerFields`): the fields are read and shown before GPU allocated, a lower ledger compares as better, and a line without them gives no row.
- **Check:** `test_memory_line_format_matches_the_producer`: the memory line filled in from `nvn_mtl.m`'s own format strings gives all seven fields, so devlog and the host print the same text.

### iOS ends the app once the guest's touched pages reach its memory limit

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone the app is ended by the system (a jetsam event: no crash report, no `[dm] CRASH` line) once its footprint reaches the limit: about 6 GB in LiveContainer on a 12 GB iPhone (6,143 MiB measured; an installed app with the entitlement on an iPhone 18 Pro Max has about 8 GB), 3.3 GB without it. The log stops mid-frame.
- **Cause:** every guest page the game has touched is anonymous memory: `phys_footprint` charges it, and iOS cannot write it out as a Mac swaps it. What the game gives back is charged again as soon as it maps the range anew.
- **Fix:** the guest block in sparse files, unlinked at once, mapped shared over the reservation at the same addresses (`hle_svc.c`; `DM_GUEST_FILE_DIR`, which the iOS app sets to `NSTemporaryDirectory()`). File pages take the same memory while they stay in it, but they are out of the footprint and the kernel writes them out when it needs the room. The cost is disk (Mario Kart World on a 16 GB Mac: 5.6 GB on disk and 8-10 GB of logical writes in 5.5 minutes), so a game that fits stays anonymous: on iOS the files are on when the game's memory pool and half of it again, for the host's own memory, are larger than `os_proc_available_memory()` at start ([The guest stayed anonymous when its pool fitted the room but the host's memory did not](#the-guest-stayed-anonymous-when-its-pool-fitted-the-room-but-the-hosts-memory-did-not)), and the log's `[mem] guest memory` line gives all three; on the Mac only with `DM_GUEST_FILE=1`, and `0` keeps them off anywhere. A game whose memory is mostly outside the block is not helped: Unreal's heap is a host malloc zone ([Thread stacks from the host's Unreal heap](#thread-stacks-from-the-hosts-unreal-heap)). The files are 1 GB each (`DM_GUEST_FILE_CHUNK`), since the stall at the system's periodic flush, about every 30 s, grows with the file (on a 16 GB M5 Mac, with 1 GB touched, up to 148 ms with one 20 GB file, 4 ms with 20 files of 1 GB). `guest_release` makes a range the game gives back `PROT_NONE` there, since a fresh anonymous mapping would bring it back into the footprint, and its disk blocks are punched out ([Guest files keep the blocks of memory the game gave back](#guest-files-keep-the-blocks-of-memory-the-game-gave-back)); a page that keeps its bytes is zeroed by `alias_initialize` and `SetHeapSize` when it is opened again, leaving the 4 KB pages that already read 0. `DM_GUEST_FILE_MIN_FREE` (default 2 GB of room after the dirty pages, the same entry) stops the game (exit code 87) before the disk fills; under it at start the guest stays anonymous, and on the Mac, which can swap, under that plus 8 GB. `make -C dm/host/tests memory-file` runs the memory SVC tests with the guest in files.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md) 1.0.0 in handheld mode, on the switch2ios fork (a 12 GB iPhone, iOS 27.3, LiveContainer's 6,143 MiB limit; host from nxnative `8c0b8083` with that fork's changes): ended at 90 s with the guest anonymous; with one file, 7 min 33 s played, peak 5.56 GiB; with 1 GB files (and a larger texture view cache), a 14-minute session held, peak 5,663 MB, no 30-s stall left. On a 16 GB Mac the peak footprint fell from 11.6 GiB to 5.6. On this host (`975bb4c1` with this switch, 1 GB files, a larger texture view cache and the profile's map limit), a 16 GB M5 Mac with the iPhone's settings peaked at 4,365 and 4,792 MiB over an 18- and a 30-minute session, and the same build plays on the 12 GB iPhone. On iPhones with an 8 GiB limit ([#1184](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1184)), where the game was ended at course load, or 30 s in at 8.1 GB, the fork's version of this switch merged by hand let it finish races: 4.41 GB at most over 13 minutes and two races on an iPhone 18 Pro with 512 MB files. Those phone runs also had a larger texture view cache (`DM_NVN_VC_WAYS=64`), which is not on main.

### The guest stayed anonymous when its pool fitted the room but the host's memory did not

- **Symptom:** on an iPad (12 GB limit) the log's start line says `[mem] guest memory: memory pool 9.00 GB, room to the memory limit 11.97 GB, DM_GUEST_FILE unset: anonymous`, the footprint climbs over a long session (8.14 GB at 186 s, 10.64 at 1,255 s, 11.94 at 1,711 s), the `memory limit … MB` lines' room falls under 100 MB, and the log stops mid-line: a jetsam kill.
- **Cause:** the rule compared only the game's memory pool with the room to the memory limit at start, which the host reads before the game runs. The host's own memory grows beside the guest's pages and comes out of the same room: mostly Metal's copies of the game's textures (Metal cannot sample the guest's block-linear storage), render targets and upload staging. The `[nvn-mtl] memory` line's `GPU allocated` does not measure it, as Metal's allocated size counts the zero-copy buffers over the guest's own pools too.
- **Fix:** general (`hle_svc.c`, `guest_host_margin`; ported from nxnative `fb26adfc9`): the guest goes in files when the pool and a margin of half the pool do not fit the room, and the start line names the margin. What has to fit is the guest's touched pages and the host's own memory together. Upstream measured the host's own share against the pool on a 16 GB Mac with the guest in files (footprint less `DM_MEMSTAT`'s guest class): from 0.13 (The Binding of Isaac) to 1.0 (Mario Golf: Super Rush), host and guest together up to at least 1.33 of the pool (Mario Kart World on the iPad), so no smaller margin on the host alone would hold. With half the pool every 9 GB pool goes in files on iOS, and a Switch game's 3.21 GB pool stays anonymous with 6 and 8 GB limits (4.8 GB against about 5.7 and 7.97 of room). `DM_GUEST_FILE_HOST=<size>` sets the margin (`0`: the rule before it). The Mac, with no limit known, is unchanged.
- **Check:** `t_svc_memory` (`file_rule`, under `make -C dm/host/tests memory memory-file`): a 9 GB pool goes in files with 11.97 GB of room and did not without the margin; a 3.21 GB pool stays anonymous with 7.97 and 5.8 GB; the switch and `DM_GUEST_FILE_HOST` override it.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md) base v0 on an iPad mini (A20 Pro, iOS 27.2), upstream ([nxnative #1184](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1184)). Not yet run on NX 18's iPhone or an iPad with the margin.

### Guest files keep the blocks of memory the game gave back

- **Symptom:** with the guest in files on an iPad, `[RED] [mem] guest file: 5.0 GB free on its disk, under 5.0: stopping the game` after a few minutes of play. The files held 6.6 GB on disk (`allocated on disk` in the `[mem] guest file` line) while the heap was 6.2 GB and the alias region had 2.5 GB mapped, though mapped up to +3.8 GB. The disk's free space went from 12.0 to 5.0 GB, almost all of it the files.
- **Cause:** two. (1) What the game gave back only went `PROT_NONE` (`guest_release`): its pages kept their disk blocks for the rest of the run, and a range mapped again elsewhere took new ones. (2) The stop rule read `f_bavail` alone, which leaves out what the system can purge, against a 5 GB margin far larger than what the files could still take. iOS writes dirty file pages out within seconds of the game touching them (the iPad's files went from 0 to 4 GB on disk in 10 s), so on iOS the files' size follows what the game touches. The Mac writes nothing back unless it needs the room (0 GB allocated after 30 minutes).
- **Fix:** general (`hle_svc.c`). `guest_file_punch` gives a range's blocks back with `F_PUNCHHOLE`. Over a mapping that also drops the pages, written out or still dirty, and they read 0 again. A wired page, under a Metal no-copy buffer the GPU still holds, keeps its bytes, so ranges are still zeroed when opened again. The heap's given-back ranges are punched at once. The alias region's are only marked: the game unmaps and maps about 0.5 GB of the same ranges a second (Ocarina of Time), and punching those at once makes every page fault back in from a hole. A sweep every 10 s (`guest_file_sweep`, under the map lock) punches the pages still given back after a whole interval. `DM_GUEST_FILE_PUNCH=0` keeps the blocks. The stop rule counts the room: free space less the files' dirty pages, counted every 10 s with `mincore` over what the game has opened (about 20 ms a gigabyte of address space; `SEEK_DATA` cannot tell which dirty pages already have blocks, as APFS reports a file with dirty mapped pages as all data). The game stops under `DM_GUEST_FILE_MIN_FREE`, now 2 GB by default. When `f_bavail` alone is short, the free space with what the system can purge counts (`kCFURLVolumeAvailableCapacityForImportantUsageKey`, at most every 10 s, as the query goes to a system service), as long as `f_bavail` keeps half the minimum, since the system purges only as the disk fills. The 10-s line adds the dirty bytes, what was given back as holes and the purgeable figure. `DM_GUEST_FILE_SCAN=1` adds the dirty pages by region and how many of them read all 0. `t_svc_memory` (`file_sweep`, under `make -C dm/host/tests memory-file`) checks that the sweep punches only pages given back over a whole interval and that they read 0 when mapped again.
- **The dirty count's cost:** `guest_file_dirty` (`mincore` over what the game has opened) took 190-275 ms every 10 s on an iPhone 18 Pro Max in every game with a 9 GB pool. It now runs every 10 s only while the disk's free space, less the whole guest block (dirty file pages never take more), is within a gigabyte of the minimum, and at once when the disk first comes that near; farther from it no count can stop the game or turn the line red, so it runs every 60 s for the log (`guest_file_count_every`; the `[mem] guest file` line says how long ago it counted and why). `t_svc_memory` `file_rule` checks the edge.
- **Why stop at all:** a full disk is worse. Measured on the Mac with a 300 MB APFS image and 600 MB of a shared file mapping written, `msync` returns 0 with 291 MB written, the other 309 MB stay dirty in memory for good (`mincore`), and every other write to the disk fails with `ENOSPC`. On a phone that is memory taken from everything else until something is killed, and saves, the shader cache and the system's own writes fail.
- **APFS fills short gaps (measured on the Mac):** a page written back through a mapping past a file's last allocated block takes blocks for the whole gap below it when the gap is under 16 MB. On macOS 27.3 a page at 15 MB of a new file took 15 MB of blocks and one at 16 MB or higher 16 KB, and 1 MB holes between pages written back past the end were filled. A page inside a punched hole below allocated blocks takes only its own, and `pwrite` takes only its own wherever it writes. Not checked on iOS, nor how much of the files' growth it makes.
- **Result on Ocarina of Time,** 5 minutes on an M4 Pro Mac, where the dirty bytes are what the files would hold on an iPad: 7.80 GB without punching, 5.98 GB with the sweep (1.84 GB given back as holes; the alias region's dirty pages 4.56 → 2.87 GB). Punching at once gave 6.32 GB, with 87 GB punched in 3 minutes. Frame time and CPU were the same with the sweep (p50 about 21 ms, GPU-bound). The game's own zeroing leaves about 770 MB of dirty heap pages that read all 0. Punching those would need the write tracker's help (a write between the check and the punch would be lost), so it is not done.
- **Open, a GPU hang reported elsewhere:** S2Arm, another project running Switch games on Apple hardware, saw Metal report `kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorHang` in 6 of 6 runs of Ocarina of Time on an M1 Pro (16 GB, macOS 27.0.1) when it punched its guest files during play, either each unmapped range in one call or 16 KB at a time from a background thread ([S2Arm #258](https://github.com/jhaizhou-ops/S2Arm/issues/258), [#305](https://github.com/jhaizhou-ops/S2Arm/pull/305)). The punching thread sat in `fcntl(F_PUNCHHOLE)` while Metal's submission thread waited in `IOGPUCommandQueueSubmitCommandBuffers`, and a 1-s pause after each large unmap made it go away; it now never punches. Not reproduced here (M4 Pro, 48 GB, macOS 27.2, Ocarina of Time idle on the Kokiri Forest deck save, 8 minutes a run). A probe wrote, fsynced and punched 16 MB of alias-region pages the game had not mapped, in the files it uses, every cycle: 36.5 GB in 2.39 million 16 KB punches in one run and 49 GB in 140,000 whole-run punches in another, with no command buffer error. Four runs with the sweep itself had none either, though the game gave memory back only while loading. Under the map lock the probe's 16 KB punches took up to 784 ms a cycle and whole runs up to 53 ms; the sweep punches runs (16–80 ms a sweep, once 162 ms for one 0.7 MB punch). Frames over 1 s came with and without punching. What may differ: the machine and the OS, and that S2Arm's hanging versions punched ranges the moment they were unmapped, where the sweep punches only pages given back a whole interval earlier (the heap's are punched at once). Not run on an iPad or an M1-class Mac; `DM_GUEST_FILE_PUNCH=0` turns punching off.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an iPad Pro (M5, iOS 27, 12.5 GB of guest files). The stop rule and the sweep have not run on an iPad yet. The GPU hang above was reported by S2Arm.

### The residency commit froze over the guest's files

- **Symptom:** with the guest's memory in files (`DM_GUEST_FILE`; the iOS app's default when the game's pool and the host's margin do not fit the memory limit), the game stops presenting for good while sound plays on: a black screen, or the last picture held. On an iPhone 18 Pro Max (Ocarina of Time, build `431d5d18`, an NPC conversation after a save load) the log said `[RED] stall: no present for 5.2 s`, then `residency commit lock (g_res_commit_mu) held by host thread … from gfx__nvn__nvn_mtl.m:795`, the encode thread `waiting for the residency commit lock`, and that thread at `iokit_user_client_trap ← res_commit ← gmem_map ← encode_args_` in both dumps, 16.5 s apart. Going to the background then waited its 3 s for the graphics call around it (`[RED] suspend: the GPU did not drain in 3 s (… 1 graphics call in progress …)`), iOS ended the app soon after, and the relaunch stayed black until the phone was restarted. Upstream's M1 iPad froze the same way at the title movie and walking in Link's house, with 7.6 GB of headroom: not memory pressure.
- **Cause:** a residency commit wires the pages of each buffer it adds, and pages a file in from the GPU driver's own wiring path. Upstream's sysdiagnose spindump during the freeze: the commit's thread in `IOGPUFamily` → the VM → `apfs` pagein, `blocked by krwlock for reading owned by launchd`, a thread of launchd's background I/O queue that had stopped 4 ms after it, inside APFS, and never ran again. What that thread waits for is not known (the kernel had no symbols). Ordinary page-ins of the same files (700,000 in a run) never hung. The raw pool table adds a large pool as one whole-pool buffer (`gmem_map`), so one commit can page in hundreds of MB of file at once.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `fwire_range`, `fwire_all`, `res_add_at`, `res_commit`; port of NXNative 2261a388). A shared buffer whose pages are file-backed (inside the guest's block with the guest in files, from the host's layout, `hle_svc.c` `dm_guest_file_backed`; elsewhere `vm_region_recurse_64` reports an external pager) is wired with `mlock` before `[g_res commit]`, so the pages come in through the ordinary fault path and the commit finds them resident. The commit gives the `mlock`s back right after `[g_res commit]`, which holds the pages for the GPU from then on. Anonymous memory and Metal's own buffers are not touched, so nothing changes with the guest anonymous (every Mac run unless `DM_GUEST_FILE=1`). NX 18's changes: `res_add` only notes the range with the change, and the commit that applies it wires it, under the residency commit lock and inside its mark (below). Upstream wires in `res_add`, whose callers may hold `g_mu`, the pool table's lock (`pool_res`, `gmem_map`'s loop over the pools): a page-in of hundreds of MB there (upstream: 2.1 GB in 6.7 s) would stall every pool lookup on every thread, which is why the commit for a pool already runs after `g_mu` (`pool_res_commit`). Outside the guest's block the buffer's first mapping decides (one short look, where the write tracking splits an anonymous pool into thousands of mappings; **The add's look at the mapping** below); a refused `mlock` leaves the commit to page the rest of the file in as before, with one red line that gives the reason and the limit: `[RED] residency: file pages under a buffer of N MB could not be wired before its commit (mlock: …, errno …, after M MB of it; RLIMIT_MEMLOCK …)` (a refusal with `EAGAIN` has its own, below). The `[nvn] residency page-ins up to frame F:` line every 300 frames (the residency line's end until 2026-11-22) counts them: `file-backed buffers wired before their commit <n>, <MB>, <ms> (refused <n>)`, with `calls over 100 ms <n>, the longest <ms> ms` and `not tried with no wired room <n>` when there are any, and `read ahead <MB>`.
- **Wired in calls of 8 MB:** `fwire_lock` wires a range in `mlock` calls of 8 MB, not one call a buffer. While one call runs, XNU keeps every map entry it has wired so far in transition until the whole call returns, and an `mprotect`, `munmap` or `MAP_FIXED` mapping that meets one sleeps until then: a single call over a pool of hundreds of MB (upstream: 2.1 GB in 6.7 s) held the guest's write tracking (`mprotect`) and the virtual pools' remaps on that pool for all of it. Each now waits for one call at most. The commit's mark counts what the calls have wired and when the current one began, so the 1 s line says how far the page-in got and whether one call is stuck. A call slower than 100 ms (under 80 MB/s, where an ordinary page-in runs at hundreds) is said once (`residency: an mlock of 8.0 MB of file pages before a commit took N ms (over 100 ms), D of that buffer's M MB wired by then: the calls go on …`) and counted, with the longest (`calls over 100 ms <n>, the longest <ms> ms`), and the calls go on. The first version (d9bb8a40) stopped the buffer's wiring at one such call and left the rest to `[g_res commit]`. That made the commit no shorter, as the commit then paged the same bytes in from the same disk under the same lock, and it sent them through the driver's own wiring, the path that froze, likely in one call over the rest of the buffer, which brought back the long stall on the guest's `mprotect`s that the calls bound. That stall has not been seen on the Mac: on an M4 Max (macOS 27.3) an `mprotect` pair (read-only and back) on one page 128 MB into a range, 40 ms into a single cold `mlock` call over 1 GB, took under 0.01 ms, whether the call went through the writable mapping or a read-only one. Not run on a device yet.
- **Read ahead:** when `res_add` notes a file-backed range, its pages are asked for ahead (`fwire_advise` → `hle_svc.c` `dm_guest_file_advise`: `fcntl(F_RDADVISE)` on the guest files under the range, the address's offset in the block being its offset in the files). The kernel reads what is not in memory into the file cache and returns before the reads complete; nothing is mapped, wired or dirtied, and a hole comes in as a clean zero page (`mincore` on the Mac: 16 of 16 pages in, none modified). The reads are then on their way when the commit's `mlock` calls meet the pages, issued at once where a fault reads a cluster at a time. It goes in batches: `res_add` puts the range on a list, and only the add that finds no run queued queues one (`fwire_advise_run`), which takes the whole list, sorts it, merges ranges that touch or overlap, asks for each, and takes the list again until it is empty (each add queued its own ask before). It still starts at the first add, not at the commit: an upload's source buffer is committed later, with its batch, and its reads get that head start. Up to 65,536 ranges wait; more, while a run is stuck on the disk, are dropped, as advice. It runs on a serial queue of its own with no lock held (`res_add`'s callers may hold `g_mu` and `g_res_mu`; the list has a lock of its own, held for a moment by each add and each run, never across a call; an advice that waits on the APFS lock the freeze waited on stalls only that queue), at user-initiated QoS: a utility thread's reads go out as throttled I/O (tier 1), and the commit's page-in waits for any page whose read is in flight. Advice only: a range the game lets go meanwhile is read in and dropped like any file page. The residency line counts it (`read ahead <MB>`). **Not measured:** how much shorter the commit's page-in gets; the commit usually follows `res_add` within the frame, so the gain is the reads' depth, not a head start.
- **The add's look at the mapping:** Donkey Kong Bananza's first load on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (build `29dcbb4d`) took 834.9 ms in one frame, against 304–357 ms on the three builds before the pre-wire, and the long-frame line split it `residency adds 1966, 428.5 ms; residency commits 213, 98.1 ms; textures made 265, 12.7 ms`, with no lock wait; later frames of 25–50 ms had 2–4 adds of about 3.7 ms each. `fwire_range` asked the kernel about every shared buffer with the long flavour of `vm_region_recurse_64` (`VM_REGION_SUBMAP_INFO_COUNT_64`), whose page counts (resident, dirty, swapped out) come from a look-up of every page of the whole map entry around the buffer, not of the buffer, under the map's and the file's VM object locks. A guest file's map entry is up to 1 GB (65,536 pages) where the write tracking has not cut it, and 1,241 of those adds were `gmem_map`'s pools, made under `g_mu`. On an M4 Max (macOS 27.3) one look at a 1 GB entry took 117 µs with nothing resident, 411–490 µs resident and 1.8–2.6 ms at the first look after its pages were written or cleaned, against 1.0–1.3 µs for the short flavour (`VM_REGION_SUBMAP_SHORT_INFO_COUNT_64`); the cost follows the entry's size (8 MB pieces: 4.5 µs an add), which is why most of the phone's adds took 3–14 µs. The look also held back the read-ahead and the game's page faults on that file. Now the guest's layout decides, with no system call: the block is the host's own, and with the guest in files every page of it is theirs for the whole run (what the game gives back is only protected and punched, `MapMemory` copies), so a buffer wholly inside it, as the pools and the uploads' source buffers are, is file-backed, and with the guest anonymous it is not (`hle_svc.c` `dm_guest_file_backed`). Anything else (Metal's own buffers, a sparse pool's runs, which `vm_remap` the guest's pages to a range of their own, a pool in a module's data, a buffer made before the guest's memory) is asked of the kernel with the short flavour (`fwire_kernel`). It reads the entry's object chain alone and says `external_pager` wherever the long one does: the same answer on 21 of 22 kinds of memory (the guest's `MAP_SHARED` files in every state, their aliases through `mmap` or `mach_vm_remap`, shared or copied, anonymous memory, Metal's own buffers, a submap); the 22nd, a `MAP_PRIVATE` file whose every page was copied on write, says 1 where the long one said 0, and such a range is wired, which brings in only pages that are there. So no file-backed range goes unwired before its commit, and the commit is unchanged: the same 8 MB calls before `[g_res commit]` under its mark, the wired-limit handling and the read-ahead's own queue, now in batches (above). **Measured** on the same Mac through `res_add` and `res_commit` in the shape of Bananza's load (10 sparse 1 GB files mapped as the guest's block, 1,750 pools over them, 1,050 MB, and 216 Metal buffers, 336 MB; a burst of 1,241 adds under a mutex and a commit, then 725 adds with a commit every 4), the host's layout answering for the block: with the pools resident (four runs each), the adds took 1.0–1.1 ms in all against 673–767 ms (the burst under the mutex 0.4 ms against 450–516; per add a median of 0.2 µs against 408–472), the commits 41–55 ms against 45–52, 42–56 ms in all against 718–818, and the read-ahead queue was busy 2.1–2.9 ms against 597–677 (with the kernel's short look for every buffer instead of the layout, the adds took 2.9–3.7 ms). No add queues an ask of its own any more; in this shape the pools lie apart, so the batches merged few asks (1,724 against 1,750). With the pools written out and dropped, the adds took 1.2–1.3 ms against 470–548 ms, and the commits 213–265 ms against 165 (the read-ahead no longer runs ahead while the adds are slow), 215–266 ms in all against 636–714. A thread reading guest pages meanwhile waited at most 0.6 ms against 162 ms (resident) and 12 ms against 101 ms (dropped). **Not measured on the iPhone:** the load frame should lose most of its 428.5 ms of adds, to about 400 ms, and the lone adds of 3.7 ms should go; it should stay above the 304–357 ms of the builds before the pre-wire, which paid for the same page-ins inside `[g_res commit]` (the 98.1 ms of commits, about 74 ms of them the pre-wire, stay). The long look is still made where its page counts are wanted, each behind a switch or off the frame: `memstat.m` (`DM_MEMSTAT`), the memory line's `guest charged` (`DM_MEM_GUEST=1`), and `hle_cheats.c`'s description of a bad address.
- **A wired limit met (`EAGAIN`):** in XNU `mlock` has no entitlement or sandbox check of its own, and `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` is unlimited by default; a call is refused with `EAGAIN` when it would pass a wired limit: the task's (`RLIMIT_MEMLOCK`, `vm.user_wire_limit`) or the system's, `vm.global_user_wire_limit` against every wired page in the system, the GPU's included (about 80% of RAM on a device with jetsam: about 9.6 GB on 12 GB; 37.9 GB of 48 GB on an M4 Max). One red line gives the system's wired count and the limits, or the errno of each it cannot read: `[RED] residency: a wired-memory limit was reached: an mlock of 8.0 MB of file pages under a buffer of N MB (M MB of it wired) was refused with EAGAIN before its commit. Wired now W GB of R GB of memory (host_statistics64); vm.global_user_wire_limit …, vm.user_wire_limit …, RLIMIT_MEMLOCK …; refusals over the system's / a task's limit since boot … / …`. The commit pages the rest in itself, and no buffer is wired before its commit while the wired count leaves no room for a call (`fwire_room`: the wired bytes 8 MB or more under `vm.global_user_wire_limit`, checked at each commit with file pages until there is room): each would be refused in turn. When the limit cannot be read (the iOS sandbox may hide it), nothing is wired before a commit again in that run. A task's limit is not waited out: it counts only the task's own `mlock`s (Metal's wiring of the same pages is the kernel's and does not count), the host's are these alone, and each commit gives them back, so the next commit has its room again and wires up to it; only the rest of the commit that met it goes in unwired, at the cost of one refused call a commit (on this Mac with `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` at 2 MB, three rounds of `mlock` in 1 MB calls and `munlock` wired 2 MB each, then `EAGAIN`). On this Mac `vm.user_wire_limit` equals `vm.global_user_wire_limit` and `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` is unlimited, so the system's limit is the one met; iOS has not been checked. **Not run on a device:** whether iOS lets the app read the limits and `host_statistics64`, and whether a game reaches the limit.
- **What the wiring dirties (measured on the Mac):** `mlock` wires a page for writing when its mapping is writable at that moment. Over the guest's writable mapping it marks every page dirty, holes included; over pages the write tracking has protected (`PROT_READ`), or through a second `PROT_READ` `MAP_SHARED` mapping of the same file range, it marks none. Measured on an M4 Max (48 GB, macOS 27.3, 16 KB pages) with a 1 GB unlinked file, every page written and written back, then every other MB punched out as `guest_file_punch` does (512 MB of holes), its pages out of memory (`msync(MS_INVALIDATE)`; `mincore`: 0 in). `mlock` in 8 MB calls over the writable mapping left all 65,536 pages dirty, the 32,768 in holes too; once they were given back, `msync` wrote 1,024 MB and the file's blocks grew by 512 MB. Over protected pages, or through the read-only mapping, the same calls took 186–270 ms, wired every page (system wired +1,024 MB; every page survived `MS_INVALIDATE`) and dirtied none: 0 MB written, no growth, and writes through the writable mapping stayed coherent with it. Either way the commit paged nothing in: `[set commit]` took 8–14 ms, against 175–290 ms with nothing wired first (an `MTLResidencySet` on an `MTL4CommandQueue`, made resident by a submission or by `requestResidency`).
- **What the commit dirties (measured on the Mac):** the commit itself marks every page under a buffer the GPU can write dirty, holes included, whatever wired them first, and with nothing wired first (0 → 65,536 dirty across `[set commit]`). Nothing is written while the GPU holds them; once the buffer leaves the set, `msync` writes the whole 1,024 MB and the holes take their 512 MB of blocks. Metal makes a no-copy buffer over write-protected pages read-only: its commit dirties nothing, the GPU reads the CPU's later writes through it, and a GPU write into it is dropped with no error in the commit feedback. The host's two no-copy buffers are one of each. `pool_buf` makes a pool's buffer (a chunk of a large pool, or the whole pool for the raw pool table, `gmem_map`) over pages opened for it (`nvn_dirty_open`), as the GPU writes through it, so its commit dirties all of it: 1,024 MB written and +512 MB of blocks after no pre-wire, the writable `mlock` or the read-only mapping alike. `up_big_src` makes the upload's source buffer over the pages the upload has just protected, so neither its commit nor `fwire_lock`'s `mlock` of those pages dirties any: 0 MB and +0 each way. So the pre-wire stays on the guest's own mapping: a read-only one would change neither kind, and would spare only pages left writable under a read-only buffer, which the game's own write has made dirty already. (A read-only mapping costs 2–3 µs to make and drop untouched at any size up to 9 GB, 4.3–4.6 ms to drop after 1 GB was wired through it.)
- **Open, the writes under GPU-resident memory:** the guest files' writes and filled holes under GPU-resident memory come from the commits of the pools' writable buffers, and were there before the pre-wire. A buffer over a read-only mapping avoids them only for memory the GPU never writes, and a wrong one loses the GPU's writes without an error. Not measured on a device: how much the iPhone writes for it.
- **If a commit sticks anyway:** the freeze detector names it at about 1 s, in or out of focus: `[RED] stall: a residency commit ([g_res commit], N changes, M MB of file pages wired for it) in the kernel for 1.0 s on host thread …, holding the residency commit lock (presents wait behind it; …)` and a dump (`dm/host/stall.c` `commit_step`; `mtl_res_commit_stuck` reads a mark `res_commit` sets around its page-in and its kernel call, with no lock). A page-in that sticks reads `a residency commit's page-in (mlock of M MB of file pages before [g_res commit], N changes; D MB wired so far in calls of 8 MB, the current call S s in) in the kernel for …` (`between two calls` when none is in the kernel: the calls go on, slowly). The 5 s line of the same freeze then points at that dump. Going to the background no longer waits for it: once such a commit has been in the kernel 1 s, while the suspend waits for the graphics calls or for its drain, the suspend starts no drain (each step would wait for its lock, and nothing may touch `g_res` while the commit is there) and goes with `[RED] suspend: not waiting for a residency commit … in the kernel for N s` ([Going to the background while the GPU does not drain](nvn-objects.md#going-to-the-background-while-the-gpu-does-not-drain)). That is logging and the way out only; a thread inside the kernel cannot be called back.
- **After such a freeze:** the relaunch may stay black until the device restarts. The likely cause is the frozen process itself: iOS cannot reap a process whose thread waits inside the kernel, and does not start the app again while it is there (upstream: `devicectl device process launch --terminate-existing` hung with the process gone from the list); the frozen run's log was still `Documents/log.txt` after the black relaunch, which a start that reached the host would have replaced. The app cannot help there: restart the phone. What a killed run leaves behind does not hold a start: the pipeline archive's files and the crash guard's record are written whole or not at all (a temporary file renamed over), the crash guard took its record back at frame 300 of that run, and the guest files are unlinked when made. `pso_user.tsv`, which takes a line at a time, now gets a newline first when a run ended in the middle of one, so that line and the next run's first are not both lost to the prewarm.
- **Result:** upstream, on the M1 iPad, the route that froze twice: alive past frame 12,900, no red line, 20 fps in the house (GPU 34-42 ms), headroom 5.3 GB, residency commits under 2 ms per 300 frames, 26 to 3,729 buffers wired per 300 frames, 2.1 GB in 6.7 s while the house loaded; on a 16 GB M4 Air with `DM_GUEST_FILE=1`, a route that had stalled 7-37 s a frame in the same commit played through (frame time p50 16.7 ms, 3.9 GB wired at most). **Not run on an iPhone yet:** whether iOS lets the app `mlock` (the red line says when it does not), and whether the freeze is gone there.
- **Check:** `t_file_wire` (upstream's test, with NX 18's checks): `res_add` under `g_mu` wires nothing, the file pages are wired and resident just before `[g_res commit]` (a hook there), the commit is marked then with the MB wired for it and gives the wiring back, an outside `mlock` stays, a buffer added twice is wired once, anonymous and Metal-allocated buffers are not wired, the residency line counts them, the page-in runs in calls of `g_fwire_call` bytes (before each, exactly the calls before it have wired their pages) with its progress in the mark, a slow call is said once and counted with the longest, and the calls after it run, so the whole range is wired before `[g_res commit]`, a page-in is marked as the commit's, no commit is left marked in the kernel, and with `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` under the range (`EAGAIN`) the buffer goes in unwired with the wired-limit red line (the wired count, the limits), the next buffer of the commit is not tried, nor any while there is no room for a call, and they are wired again once there is; with `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` held at half a range across two commits, each wires up to it (the set's own wiring of buffers over the same pages not counted) and goes in partly wired, with nothing said again; any other refusal (a hole: `ENOMEM`) has its own red line, once; each file-backed range is asked for ahead once, on the read-ahead queue, without `res_add` waiting for it, and an anonymous one is not; in a 1 GB file mapping with every page resident, an add outside the guest's block makes one short kernel look and the fastest of four takes under 100 µs (575 µs with the long look), a burst of 256 adds under `g_mu` inside the block, as the layout gives it, asks the kernel nothing and takes under 20 ms (132 ms with the long look), and each such buffer is still wired before `[g_res commit]` and given back after it; with the guest anonymous a buffer in its block is not wired and the kernel is not asked; adds and a removal made while another thread's commit is inside an `mlock` call (held there) return at once, and the next commit wires them; ranges added while a read-ahead waits are asked for in one batch, sorted, touching and overlapping ones merged (3 asks where each add made its own, 6). On `808b80a9` the timing and batch checks fail; they pass with the layout and the short look. `t_svc_memory` (`make memory-file`): pages written out and dropped come back in clean after `dm_guest_file_advise`, a range across two files is asked of both, one partly outside the block only for its part inside, and nothing with the guest anonymous; `dm_guest_file_backed` says 1 for ranges inside the block with the files (0 with the guest anonymous), and the kernel's short look agrees on pages never touched, mapped and written, and given back; -1 before the guest's memory is made and for a range not wholly inside the block. `t_stall` (the 1 s line, once per commit, the 5 s line pointing at it) and `t_gfx_suspend` (the suspend and the resume do not wait for such a commit and start no drain; one that turns stuck during the wait for a graphics call, or for a drain begun before it, ends the wait at about 1 s; a page-in's mark at its longest is named whole).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (build `431d5d18`, 2026-11-15), and upstream on an M1 iPad (v131072, 2026-11-08). The add's look: [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (build `29dcbb4d`, 2026-11-16).

### The guest-file watcher could start before guest memory was ready

- **Symptom:** with the guest in files (`DM_GUEST_FILE`), the watcher's first dirty-page count could read the guest layout (`g_heap`, `g_stack`, `g_alias`) before `mem_init_once` set it, and its sweep the alias bitmaps before they were made: a crash or a wrong count in `guest_file_dirty` at start. Not seen here: the sweep already waited for `g_mem_ready`, and the first count comes a second after the start.
- **Cause:** `guest_file_init` started the watcher right after mapping the files, before `mem_init_once` laid out the block and allocated the bitmaps; only the sweep checked `g_mem_ready`.
- **Fix:** `hle_svc.c` `mem_init_once`, ported from NXNative 71a9a829d (PR #2870, MIT): the watcher is created once the layout and the bitmaps are set (after `g_mem_ready`), and only when the files were mapped, so its creation publishes that state to both of its paths.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> memory memory-file`: `t_svc_memory` checks at the watcher's creation that the files, the layout and every alias bitmap are there, and that it was started exactly when the guest is in files. With the old order the assertion on the layout fails.
- **Found on:** NXNative's review of the start order ([PR #2870](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2870)). Not seen on a game here.

### Memory pools nothing gave an address to stay out of the raw pool table

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game goes past Metal's recommended working set while its first 3D scene loads: `Insufficient Memory` command buffer errors, then a hung Mac. Ocarina of Time on a 16 GB Mac: 12 GB and more wired, once a kernel panic in `IOGPUGroupMemory.cpp` at 14.7 GB wired.
- **Cause:** a shader that loads or stores through a pointer it read from memory may reach any pool ([Global memory at an address loaded from memory](shaders.md#global-memory-at-an-address-loaded-from-memory)), so the raw pool table listed every pool and made each resident. That wires the game's texture-only pools too, gigabytes the GPU reads only through Metal textures that hold their own copy.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `mtl_pool_addressed`, `gmem_map`). A GPU address exists only if the driver handed one out: `nvnMemoryPoolGetBufferAddress`, `nvnBufferInitialize`, `nvnTextureGetTextureAddress` and a virtual pool's mapping note their ranges, and the table lists only the pools that meet one. A pool with none stays out, and a texture upload from it borrows a temporary no-copy buffer that the upload batch holds (`up_big_src`). Ocarina of Time before its first scene: 2.9 GB wired against 5.3 GB.
- **Fix, shader-code pools:** a shader-code pool's own address (`NVN_MEMORY_POOL_FLAGS_SHADER_CODE`, not virtual) is asked for to place programs, and the GPU never reads that code (the shaders run translated, their constants are copied into the host's buffers), so `nvnMemoryPoolGetBufferAddress` on such a pool only counts it (`mtl_pool_code_address`, a `raw global memory: shader-code pools …` line at the first and every 50) and leaves it out of the table. A buffer made in it still puts it in, and a lookup by address still makes it resident. Gated on the pool flag, not on a game. Upstream: Ocarina of Time on the iPhone, 223 such pools, 317 MB wired for nothing (nxnative `1c92e400`); on the Mac 150 pools, 316 MB (MacNativeGames `32c3ef13b`, MIT, which took it for Ocarina of Time alone). Not yet measured on NX 18.
- **Check:** `t_addr_known`: a shader-code pool's address keeps it out of the table and not resident, another pool's puts that one in, and an address from a buffer in the code pool puts it in.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md).

### Shaders report which textures they read through a pool

- **Symptom:** a game whose shaders read texture handles from memory wires far past the working set while a scene loads, with `Insufficient Memory` errors or a panic, and stutters when new textures stream in. With a limit on how many it makes, surfaces in shadow go near black instead.
- **Cause:** handles read from game memory may name any registered texture, and which ones is known only on the GPU, so the pool walk (`pool_gpu`) had to make all of them. On a console that costs nothing: a handle is a number and the data is already in the pool. Here each is a Metal texture beside the pool's copy. Ocarina of Time registers about 33,000 IDs and reads about 300 of them, about 1 GB. The rest were made in case, or, under a budget, exactly the large ones the shaders read were left out, and read the dummy for good. Each upload also reads pages the system may have swapped out and wires them: one 300-frame window spent 8.6 s in residency commits.
- **Fix:** general, in the chain and the host. A read of a texture pool sets the ID's bit in a bitmap (`dmsh_reflect.py` `pool_arrays`, `dm_pool_mark`; its address in the table's head, `DMSH_POOL_HEAD`). The word is read first, with a plain load ([an atomic one queues the pixels on one word](frame-rate.md#pool-marks-queue-on-one-word-when-they-test-it-with-an-atomic-load)), so nearly every read costs a load, not an atomic. The host folds the bitmap at each submission (`pool_seen_scan`) and makes those textures whatever the budget, up to 128 MB a frame. It also puts them into the existing tables in place, as a table can stand for a submission once walks have taken their 2 ms: dummy to texture is safe for a draw already encoded. Registering another texture at an ID clears its bit. A render target no pass has written is not made for a read: it holds nothing, and a pass makes it when it renders (`pool_unwritten_target`). A walk still makes the smallest textures up front, 32 MB a frame up to a twentieth of the working set, as a pass that runs once has no second frame to find its texture in. `t_pool_chunks` checks this.
- **Result on Ocarina of Time,** a 16 GB Mac, the village: Metal textures and targets 5.5 → 3.9 GB, physical footprint 14.4 → 13.0 GB, p95 from 42–79 ms (with long frames of 300 ms and more) to 28–32 ms, and the house and bark in shadow lit as on the console.
- **Cost:** the write makes each such fragment shader one with side effects, which may lose early depth tests. Not measured apart from the other changes: without the bitmap the same textures are missing, so the frames are not comparable.
- **Not done:** the guest's copy of a texture stays. Only 154 MB of it lies in wired pools on Ocarina of Time, and the system can swap the rest, so releasing it would save swap and no RAM. It would also corrupt a later texture over the same bytes under another description, as those bytes would be gone.
- **Reading the numbers:** `currentAllocatedSize` ("GPU allocated") read 16 GB on this game while `vmmap` showed 3.9 GB of Metal textures and targets. The memory line's `graphics ledger` gives what Metal holds and `guest charged` what the guest's pages cost ([GPU allocated counts the guest's memory more than once](#gpu-allocated-counts-the-guests-memory-more-than-once)); `vmmap -summary` (`owned unmapped (graphics)`, `IOAccelerator (graphics)`) splits the first.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md).

### Pools that hold shader code made resident to key the shaders

- **Symptom:** memory pools that hold only shader code are in the residency set, though no shader reads them. In The Great Ace Attorney Chronicles, three shared buffers, about 5 MB: chunks of its 1,000 MB pool (`DM_CTL` `restop`: shared buffers 241 MB at 30 s, 236 MB with the fix).
- **Cause:** `keys_hash` (`hle_nvn.c`) checks that a shader's code lies in a pool before it hashes it, and it asked `mtl_addr`, which also makes the pool resident (and, in a pool in chunks, makes the chunk that holds the code). The GPU never reads that code: the shaders run translated, and a shader's c1 constants are copied into the host's own buffers.
- **Fix:** general: `keys_hash` asks `mtl_addr_known` (`nvn_mtl.m`), the pool lookup with no side effect that texture uploads already used: it makes no pool resident, and in a pool in chunks it makes no chunk (it answers from the pool's range). A pool the GPU does read through a binding or an address is made resident there, as before. Keys are unchanged. `t_addr_known` checks it.
- **Where it does not help:** a game whose shaders read raw global memory has every pool it took an address in made resident by the raw pool table (`gmem_map`), shader code pools included. Ocarina of Time is one: on the village walk it kept 4,276 pools resident before the fix and 4,277 after, 2,324 on both at the title. The 222 pools and 334 MB first reported there came from a build with other changes.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (reported), [The Great Ace Attorney Chronicles](../../dm/games/010036E00FB20000/README.md) (measured).

### A texture object finalized after its pool kept the pool's memory

- **Symptom:** the residency set stays high after an area load that finalizes a memory pool, and at the next load that makes the pool's textures again it holds them twice. Upstream, on the Mac with the iPhone's settings, Ocarina of Time's set stayed about 360 MB higher through Hyrule Field than in Kakariko Village before it, and a large recycled heap was let go only about 20,000 submissions after its pool's Finalize; upstream's iPhone 17 was ended for memory (`JetsamEvent`, `vm-pageshortage`) entering the castle grounds, with two 300 MB heaps in the set.
- **Cause:** on NVN a memory pool's Finalize ends its memory, and with it every texture made on that storage, but the host ended only the pool's entries in the sharing table (`mtl_alias_drop_pool`). A texture object the game finalizes after its pool kept its entry in the serial table, and the entry held the Metal texture: a retired texture a serial entry still holds stays resident until the last such entry goes (`g_retire_held`; [GitHub issue #1326](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1326) says why). Packaged textures, which the sharing table never names, were kept the same way, and so were finalized serials' entries (the finalized-texture table, `g_ghost`, kept by use and never drained under its floor). Ocarina of Time keeps a 12288x12288 Depth16 shadow target alone in a 288 MB GPU-only pool, placed in a 300 MB recycled heap of its own (on the iPhone, where [lazy depth](#large-depth-textures-that-are-only-sampled-take-their-whole-size) is on, the save load's whole clear to a non-zero depth makes it that normal texture); it finalizes that pool at the load into Hyrule Field and the texture object only at the load back into Kakariko Village (or into the castle grounds), right after making the pool and the target again.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `pool_finalize_locked`; upstream NXNative 13c35e90a by hand: NX 18 has no `nvn_owner.m`, so the index sits beside its in-file serial tables). The host indexes the live texture objects of each pool from their initialization to their Finalize (`mtl_tex_init` to `mtl_tex_release`; `g_pool_tex`, `g_tex_pool`, the pool recorded at initialization). A pool's Finalize ends those objects' entries, whatever they hold (a shared texture, a view of one, a packaged texture, the texture the GPU's first write took over from a package), then every live or ghost entry that still names one of those textures, a packaged texture of the pool (`g_pkg_share`) or a part of one (`pkg_parts`), each as a note (`ser_note`). The held counts (`g_by_serial_held`, `g_ghost_held`) say how many entries name them, so that walk is skipped when none does and stops once it has found them all (`drop_entries_naming_locked`, which the release of a drawn-over target, `alias_dead_locked`, uses too). Work encoded before the Finalize keeps the textures until it completes (deferred release), and a pin (a queued descriptor or a saved binding) still holds them. A later lookup of such a serial makes nothing and reads as an empty slot ([A texture read after its memory pool object was freed](nvn-objects.md#a-texture-read-after-its-memory-pool-object-was-freed)); the object's own Finalize finds nothing left to end and retires its descriptors as before. A target a pass of `DM_NVN_RT_DEAD` released before the Finalize has no entry left for it, and one the Finalize ended leaves the next pass nothing: each texture goes once, its recycled heap either waiting or, after a pressure pass, freed.
